===== [nav 0004]
PREFACE
Students appearing for DGCA Exams and airline job interviews faced
considerable difficulty in the absence of any Indian book on the subject of Air
Navigation. The problem became more acute after DGCA revised it's syllabus in line
with EASA syllabus. Students depended on foreign books, which are not only costly
but also do not follow Indian examination pattern. Hence, a student remains confused
even after spending a fortune.
To help students overcome these problems, I have compiled this book strictly
as per DGCA syllabus. The book has been written in easy to understand language.
Revised edition includes more than 2200 questions, both solved and unsolved. Eight
sample papers are included for students to practice and analyse their weaknesses.
Two new papers have been added based on recent questions asked in DGCA exams.
Additional topics like INS/IRS, warning systems, radar calculations etc. have been
included to enhance it's utility.
The book is also useful for those interested in preparing for airline exams as well
as for refreshing their knowledge.
Other than pilots, flight dispatchers, controllers and other aviation personnel
will find the book helpful in assimilating the Navigation knowledge.
I am encouraged to revise it and take out a seventh edition. Important topics like
Performance, GNSS etc. have been reviewed. New questions added and some obsolete
topics taken out.
I always look forward for a positive feed back to improve my books. Somehow, I
find a few students who probably are still in a learning stage finding faults with topics
not clearly understood by them.
My books have gone through several editions and chances of mistakes are
minimal. Still, if anyone has doubts, please e-mail me.
I will be happy to receive feed back and suggestions for improvements from
readers.
I thank the aviation community for the overwhelming response received for the
Air Navigation book.
Wish you all safe skies and happy landings.
Ghaziabad  Wg. Cdr. R.K. Bali (Retd.)
https://www.facebook.com/groups/5544317049004204: Wg. Cdr. R.K. Bali Aviation Group

THE SOLAR SYSTEM
The Earth is one of the planets in the Solar system. Each of the planets describes
an elliptical orbit around the Sun. The Sun, therefore, can be considered to be
the stationary centre of the solar system. Each planet takes a year to complete one
orbit of the Sun, but since the planets are positioned at different distance from the
sun and they are traveling at different speeds through space, each planet's year
is different. The Earth's year is equivalent to 365 days 5 hours 48 minutes and
45 seconds.
All heavenly bodies rise in the East.
Kepler's law of planetary motion:
(i) All planets revolve around sun in an elliptical orbit with sun at one of
its foci.
Line vector joining planet to sun sweeps equal area in equal time.
Orbital velocity of earth is not constant in its orbit. It is fastest when
closest and slowest when farthest.

PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
During the course of the year, the position of the sun relative to the equator
varies; this is because the Earth's spin axis is tilted at an angle of 66.5" to the plane
of the Earth's orbit. If the spin axis was perpendicular to the plane of the orbit,
the Sun would remain directly above the Equator the whole year round and there
would be no seasons. The orbit is inclined at an angle of 23.5" to the celestial
equator and it is this inclination which causes the seasons during the course of
a year. As already mentioned the apparent path of the sun across the celestial
sphere is called the ecliptic. The angle between the earth's celestial equator and
the ecliptic (23.5") is known as the 'Obliquity of the Ecliptic'.
• Plane of the elliptic is at 23.5° to the celestial equator.
• Earth orbit is elliptical with the sun at focus.
• Plane of the elliptic is approximately 66.5° to the plane of earth's orbit.
Rotation, revolution and tilt has the following effects:
• Rotation - Day / night
• Revolution - Year
• Tilt of earth axis by 23.5° - Seasons
Seasonal Variations

Perihelion: Sun is closest to the earth. SH-Summer NH-Winter
Aphelion: Sun is furthest from the earth SH-Winter NH-Summer
Equinox: Day and night are equal, 0°, (spring and autumn).
The periods of maximum rate of change between day and night.
Solstice: is sun's highest/lowest point in summer/winter.
Winter solstice: Shortest day in NH, 23.5° S tropic of Capricorn (22nd December)
Summer solstice: Longest day in NH. 23.5° N tropic of Cancer (21st June).
THE SOLAR SYSTEM
Twilight Period: It is a time period before sunrise and after sunset when light
of sun is visible to an observer. This is the light refracted and scattered by the
atmosphere.
Although the Sun is below the horizon, the Sun's rays are being refracted
in from the atmosphere and result in an extended period of light. It is therefore
worthwhile to consider what we call the 'horizon'.
Observer
Sensible Horizon  • Sunset
Twilight Period is Divided in Three Parts
Depends upon center of sun being below the sensible horizon as given below:
C - Civil twilight used in flying  sun 0°-6° below sensible horizon
N - Nautical twilight  sun 6°-12° below sensible horizon
A - Astronomical twilight  sun 12°-18° below sensible horizon
Visible Horizon: Atmospheric refraction causes an observer to observe objects
which are actually below his sensible horizon. Horizon which is below sensible
horizon is called visible horizon. Sunrise and sunset times are taken based on sun's
Sensible Horizon: Horizon which is visible to naked eyes without catering for
atmospheric refractions is called sensible horizon and it is above visible horizon. A
spirit level senses sensible horizon. Twilight period is related to sun's center being
The centre of the Sun is approximately 50 minutes of are below the sensible

A Remember:
When upper edge of any heavenly body rises above the visible horizon it is
said to be risen (eg; Moonrise, Sunrise). And when it goes below visible horizon
then it is called set (eg; Moonset, Sunset).
Twilight is related to sensible horizon.
Sunrise/sunset and twilight in air almanac table are in LMT.
Atmospheric Refraction causes the sun rise to occur earlier and the
sunset to occur later.

Moon: The Moon does not emit its own light, shining instead by reflecting
sunlight. Depending on the relative positions of the Earth, Sun and Moon, varying
amounts of the lunar surface appear to be illuminated.
(i) One revolution of moon is completed in 29 ½ days, called synodic
period.
(ii) Because of direction of rotation of earth and direction of revolution of
moon is same. Therefore there is one day in a month when there is no
moon set and one day there is no moonrise. "Waxing" means growing
and "waning" means shrinking. "Gibbous" is swollen on one side.
"Crescent" is the figure of moon defined by a convex and concave edge.
(ii) Every day moon rises or sets approximately 50 mins late.
THE SOLAR SYSTEM
YEAR: A period of one civil year is defined as the time taken for a planet to
describe one orbit around the sun. For the earth, this orbit takes approximately
365.25 day. A leap year every 4 years corrects for the fractional quarter of a day
of orbit time.
This familiar leap year correction is, however, not quite exact. There is a
further correction needed which will lose 3 day every 400 year. This is achieved
by suppressing 3 leap years every 4 centuries. Every century year is classified as
an ordinary year of 365 days, except when the century number is divisible by 400
in which case it is classified as a leap year. So 1700,1800, 1900 were not leap years,
while 2000 was a leap year.
DAY:
Transit: Time taken between the sun's appearance overhead a
meridian of longitude and the next transit over the same meridian is
called a day.
Apparent solar day: It is the time interval elapsed between two
successive transits of true sun over an observer meridian (varies
between 23:44 - 24: 14 hrs). Length of a day varies not because the
speed of rotation varies or because of the tilt of the axis, but because
the orbit is not symmetrical.
• Mean solar day: It is measured with respect to imaginary sun which
goes around earth over equator at a constant velocity. This is used
because, length of apparent solar day is not constant (24 hrs.)
===== [nav 0011]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Sidereal day: It is time interval elapsed between two successive transits
of a fixed point, other than sun, in space or two successive transits of
a First Point of Aries over an observers meridian (23: 56 hrs). The star
The equation of time (EOT): Difference between apparent and mean
solar day.
EOT = Apparent Solar Time (AST) - Mean Solar Time (MST), or
EOT = Sundial Time - Clock Time.
An apparent solar day/noon can be 20 seconds shorter or 30 seconds
longer than a mean solar day/noon. Long or short days occur in
succession, so the difference builds up until mean time is ahead of
apparent time by about 14 minutes near February 6, and behind
apparent time by about 16 minutes near November 3. AST becomes
equal to MST four times in a year during April, June, September and
December
• Mean sun: It is a fictitious body. It moves with constant speed along the
celestial equator.
• 66.6° N/S: Sun does not rise in winter of the hemisphere.
• 64.5° N/S: Sun does not set in summer of the hemisphere.
• 60.5° N/S: Sun does not go 6° below horizon in summer, there is
continuous twilight between sunset and sunrise.

Chapter

THE EARTH
SPI
Earth is an Oblate Compressed Spheroid / Ellipsoid.
Compression Ratio: For navigational purposes, the Earth is assumed to be a
perfect sphere. In reality, it is slightly flattened at the poles and can be described
as an ellipsoid or oblate spheroid. The Earth's polar diameter is approximately
23½ nm shorter then the equatorial diameter. When considering the full diameter
of the Earth, this is negligible and can be disregarded for the purposes of practical
navigation.
Nautical Mile (NM): NM was defined as the length on the Earth's surface
of one minute (1/60 of a degree) of are along a meridian (north-south line of
longitude). However due to earth not being a perfect sphere, the length of NM
varies at poles (6105') and equator (6046'). One NM is longer at Poles than at
Equator. In 1929 the nautical mile was redefined as exactly 1.852 km., about
6,076.11549 feet or 1.1508 statute miles.
12
THE EARTH
Polar Radius  6 356 752 meters  3432 nm
Equatorial Radius  6 378 137 meters  3443 nm
The compression ratio is the ratio between the polar diameter and
the equatorial diameter and indicates the amount of flattening. The ratio is
approximately 1/297 but geodetic information obtained by satellite shows that
the Earth is in fact pear-shaped with the larger mass being in the Southern
Hemisphere.
Ratio of Ellipticity = (Major Axis - Minor Axis)/Minor Axis
Anti
Clockwise
Earth Rotation: When viewed from North Pole, earth rotates anti clock wise.
Earth rotates from West to East.
Viewed from South Pole, earth rotates clock wise. Earth rotates from East to
West.
Latitude:  The angle subtended by short arc of meridian from equator to
the point to be identified.
Parallels of Latitude: A parallel of latitude is an imaginary line that joins
points of equal latitude; it is a small circle that lies parallel to the Equator (the
Equator, therefore, is also a parallel of latitude; the only one which is a great
circle). Parallel of latitude are used to define position in terms of latitude.
Whole degrees of parallels of latitude are numbered from 1° to 89° North or
South of the Equator. Since parallels of latitude are parallel to the Equator and
run East/West they cross all meridians at right angles and are therefore rhumb
lines.
13
===== [nav 0014]
PART 1 - GENERAL NAVIGATION
The definition of latitude given above is based on the center of the Earth,
namely it is the smaller angle between the line joining the point to the center of
the Earth and the plane of the Equator. This is Geocentric Latitude. Because the
Earth is actually an oblate spheroid, a different definition of Latitude can be used,
namely, Geodetic (or Geographic) Latitude, represented by the smaller angle
between the normal (90°) to the meridian at the point on the spheroid and the
plane of the equator. The normal line does not necessarily pass through the center
of the spheroid as illustrated in the diagram below. Note that the shapes used in
the diagram are exaggerated for illustration purposes and the real spheroid is
much closer to a sphere then shown. The latitudes plotted on navigation charts are
Geodetic Latitude. The maximum difference between Geocentric and Geodetic
Latitudes occurs at approx 45° N/S and is about 11.6 minutes of arc.

Longitude: The angle subtended at the center of the earth by shorter are of
the equator from Prime meridian to the point to be identified.
THE EARTH
Meridians. A meridian is a semi-great circle between the North and South
poles. It is a line joining points of equal longitude and crosses the Equator at right
angles. All meridians define the directions of True North and South.
The Prime Meridian. The Prime Meridian is the semi-great circle passing
through the poles and also passing through Greenwich, in London; it is also
known, therefore, as the Greenwich Meridian and defines a longitude of 0° East
and/or West. The Greenwich anti-meridian lies at 180° East/West.
Meridians are used to define the angular difference of a position from the
prime Meridian which is referred to as longitude. Whole degrees of meridians
are numbered from 0° to 180° and are labeled east or west of the 0° meridian of
longitude (the Prime Meridian). For every meridian, there is an anti-meridian
that lies geometrically opposite (in other words 180° of longitude removed). A
meridian together with its anti-meridian forms a complete great circle.
The longitudes of a meridian and its anti-meridian will always add up to 180°;
they will also be in opposite hemispheres. Hence the anti-meridian of 030°W is
150°E.
Graticule: is the net work of meridians and parallels of latitude on a map or
chart.
Great Circle: A circle whose plane divides earth into two equal hemispheres
is called great circle.
15
===== [nav 0015]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
eat Circle
Properties of GC:
(i) Shorter arc of great circle is the shortest Distance between two points
on the earth.
(ii) Its radius, diameter and center is same as that of the earth.
(iii) On earth surface it appears as a straight line.
(iv) Only one great circle can be drawn between two given points unless
they are diametrically opposite. In such case infinite no. of GC can be
drawn.
(v) Radio signals follow great circle path (shortest path).
Small Circle: A Small Circle is any circle on the surface of the earth other
than a Great Circle. In other words, the radius and center of a small circle are
different from that of the earth.
Rhumb Line: A line which cuts all the meridians at equal angle is called
rhumb line. Rhumb line properties are:
(1) Along a rhumb line an A/C follows a constant direction.
(2) It is not the shortest distance between two given points.
(3) Any rhumb line spirals up towards nearest pole.
16
THE EARTH
Difference/change of Latitude:
Difference of latitude (d lat), which is also known as change of latitude (ch
lat), is the difference between two latitude angles normally expressed in degrees
and minutes of arc. Fig shows three positions at different latitudes but at the same
longitude (that is to say, on the same meridian). Point A is at 40"N, point B is at
10"N and point C is at 30"S. in this example the d lat between A and B is 30",
whereas the d lat between A and C is 70Ạại
Where two points are in the same hemisphere, the d'lat is the difference
between the two points.
Difference/Change of Longitude: Difference of longitude (d long), which is
also known as change of longitude (ch long), is the angular difference between
two longitude angles normally expressed in degrees and minutes of arc. Fig
represents three positions at different longitude but at the same latitude. Point A
is at 030°W, point B is at 030°E and point C is at 070°E. in this example the d long
between A and B is 60°, whereas the d long between A and C is 100".

Questions
Q1. Calculate the d'long between position A 165°W and position B 103°W.Aircraft is
flying from A to B.
Ans. The two points are in same hemisphere. Subtract small number from large no.
165°-103° = 62°E. (See diagram below)
Remember that anti clock wise movement is East (090°) and clockwise
movement is West (270").
When two positions are in different hemispheres the situation is different.
Q2. Calculate the d'long between A 165°W and B 170°E.Aircraft is flying from A to
B.
Ans. See the diagram below.
The difference between 165° and 180° is 15°.
The difference between 170° and 180° is 10".
The d' long therefore is 25°W because the movement is clockwise.

Chapter
3
PROJECTIONS
Any attempt at reproducing a spherical shape on to a flat surface must
produce inevitable distortions. Thus, on a flat surface we must expect distortions
of the ideal properties of the reduced earth.
Projections on flat surfaces: The object of making a map or a chart is to
represent the spherical earth, or at least a part of it, on a flat surface. Ideally it
should possess the following features:
(a) The scale: it should be both correct and constant.
(b) The shapes should be correct.
(c) Areas should be shown correctly.
(d) The bearing measurements anywhere on the reduced earth should be
identical to the measurements on the earth.
(e) Great circles should be straight lines, just as on the earth.
(f) Meridians and parallels should intersect each other at 90°.
(g) Adjacent sections fit perfectly together.
The geometric projections are also called True Projections and the sheet on
which the graticule is to be projected is called the developable surface. Examples
(a) Cylindrical projections, eg, Mercator.
(b) Conical projections, eg, Lambert.
(c) Zenithal projections, the plane of the projection is tangential to the
earth's surface, eg, polar stereographic.
21
===== [nav 0018]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
When a projection contains only a graticule of latitude and longitude with
perhaps very few geographical features, it is called a chart. A map, on the other
hand contains both the graticule and an abundance of ground features.
Maps: They have more geographical details depending upon scale of the
map.
Charts: They have lesser details and have information for which they have
been made.
Method Of Construction:
(a) Geometric, perspective. A flat surface, cylinder or a cone is placed conveniently
on the surface of the reduced earth and a light source placed (at the center or
at one of the poles of the reduced earth) projects the graticule. An example is
the polar stereographic.
(b) Geometric, mathematically modified. The basic geometric projections may
be improved by modifying them mathematically. This is normally done to
achieve a certain property. Mercator and transverse Mercator projections are
examples.
(c) Mathematical. Some projections are produced entirely by mathematical
methods, although the basic concept might be drawn from the geometric
projection. Lambert's ortho morphic projection is an example here.
Projection
Perspective  Non perspective
(Light source and graticule)  (Mathematically constructed/modified)
Ortho morphic (Conformal): It is property in which bearings are correctly
shown. For a projection to be ortho morphic, following must be met.
(a) Meridians and parallel of latitude should cut each at 90° .
(b) Scale is constant in all directions with in vicinity of a point.
Practically every chart used will have its method of projection noted on
it. Often the word conformal or ortho morphic will appear in the name. The
projections encountered are likely to be as follows:
22
PROJECTIONS
Not conformal: Gnomonic (Polar, Oblique, Equatorial), Orthographic,
Equi-distant.
Conformal: Lambert's Conformal Conic, Standard Mercator (cylindrical
conformal), Transverse Mercator, Oblique Mercator, Stereographic (Polar,
Oblique, Equatorial)
GENERAL PROPERTIES OF MISCELLANEOUS TYPE OF PROJECTIONS:
• Cylindrical projection
Construction: Point of tangency is equator and light source is at center of
the earth
Properties:
(i) Meridians are straight parallel lines equidistant from each other.
(ii) Parallels of latitudes are also straight lines but not equidistant from each
other. Distance between them increases from equator to poles.
(iii) Convergence on projection on these maps is zero. It is on earth surface
shown by 'n'.
n= d'long x sine lat
(iv) A straight line on this map represents a Rhumb line.
(v) Great circle is shown by a curved line concave to equator or concave to
rhumb line or convex to pole.
(vi) It is not an ortho morphic or conformal projection
23
===== [nav 0019]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Mercator projection: Mathematical corrections are carried out in simple
cylindrical projection to make it ortho morphic. This projection is called
MERCATOR.
Properties:
Appearance is similar to simple cylindrical projection.
It is non perspective projection
(iii) Rhumb line is a straight line and great circle is curved convex to the
pole, similar to cylindrical projection.
(iv) It is an ortho morphic projection.
Usage:
Suitable for Rhumb line tracks rather than G/C tracks.
Suitable for topographical maps near equator (8° N/S of equator). In low
latitudes, chart almost has a constant scale.
(iii) Met charts in India are Mercator charts.
Draw backs:
(i)  Can't be used in polar region and coverage is limited to 70° N/S of
equator, above its scale expansion becomes very large.
(il) Radic  (ii) Radio Bearings are great circles, which must be converted to rhumb
lines before plotting.
(iii) On a long route, the rhumb line can add appreciably to the distance.
Note: In Mercator projection scale in E/W direction is varied at the same rate
of scale expansion in N/S direction.
Scale at latitude = scale at equator × secant of latitude.

PROJECTIONS
MERCATOR
Origin of Projection  Cylindrical: The cylinder touches the reduced Earth at
the Equator. Projection is from the center of the sphere.
Graticule  Meridians: Parallel straight lines. Equally spaced.
Parallels of Latitude: Unequally-spaced parallel straight
lines, spacing increases away from the Equator.
Scale  Correct at the Equator. Expands away from the Equator
as the secant of the latitude.
Convergence  Correct at the Equator. At all other latitudes, chart
convergence is less then Earth convergence.
Rhumb Line  Straight line.
Great Circle  Curves convex to the nearer pole and concave to the
Equator. Equator and meridians are straight lines.
Conical Projection:
Construction: Point of tangency is one particular standard parallel which
can be changed by changing apex angle / cone angle. Light source is the center
of the earth.

Lambert Conformal Conic (LCC) : Ortho morphic / Conformal

Construction: Points of tangency are with in 2 standards of Parallels and
light source is at latitude of origin.
Properties:
(i) Appearance of meridian and Parallels of latitude are similar to simple
conical projection.
(ii) Mathematical corrections are carried out by choosing 2 standard
Parallels to make it ortho morphic.
(iii) G/C is a curved line concave to latitude of origin. But, for practical
purposes it may be considered a straight line.
(iv) Scale is correct at standard Parallels, between them scale contracts and
it is least at latitude of origin. The scale contraction is very less. i.e. less
then 1%, therefore between 2 standard Parallels scale is constant.
(v) Away from standard Parallels scale expands at high rate, map is ortho
morphic only between 2 standard Parallels.
Usage:
This projection is increasingly being used as a plotting chart. Its other uses
are as topographical charts, radio aid charts, airways charts and meteorological
synoptic charts.
Drawbacks:
The greater the latitude difference between the two standard parallels the
greater the scale error and distortion of the shapes. If the shapes are greatly
distorted, so are directions, with a loss of ortho morphic properties.
In mid way, rhumb line is Parallel with the GC. Flying that angle will make
aircraft follow RL path. Convergence (in degrees) = d long (in degrees) × sin lat
(in degrees)

PROJECTIONS
(iv) Convergence on this map is equal to one. Convergency reduces towards
the equator; where it is 0; so chart convergency is constant at 1, correct
only at the Poles, too large away from the Pole.
(v) When plotting in high latitudes not too far away from the Pole where
Convergency is correct; a GC is taken to be a straight line for practical
purposes. As distance from the Pole increases, a GC in fact curves concave
to the Pole, and finally the equator, itself a GC, will appear as a distinct
curve. Rather an academic point for a chart used for aviation in the
Polar Regions, perhaps. A straight line drawn on a polar stereographic
chart is sometimes referred to as a stereo drome.
(vi) R/L is a curved line concave to nearest pole or G/C or convex to equator.
(vii) The scale is correct at the pole. The distances measured very close to
the Pole is very nearly correct distances. Away from the pole the scale
expands.
POLAR STEREOGRAPHIC:
Origin of Projection  Azimuthal: The flat plate touches the reduced
Earth at the pole Projection is from the opposite pole.
Graticule  Meridians: Straight lines radiating from the pole
Parallels of Latitude: Circles centered on the pole.
The spacing increases away from the pole.
Scale  Correct at the pole. Expands away from the pole as
secant of the Co-latitude  . Scale is correct to within
1% to 78°N/S. and to within 3% to 70°N/S.
Convergence  Correct at pole. At all points on the chart,
convergence equals Ch Long.
Rhumb Line  Curves concave to the pole of projection. Meridians
are straight lines.
Great Circle  Curves concave to the pole. Meridians are straight
lines. Close to the pole may be considered a straight
line for plotting purposes.

PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Transverse Mercator: The Transverse Mercator is a cylindrical projection
placed horizontally on the reduced Earth such that cylinder and the globe touch
along a chosen meridian and corresponding anti-meridian, as shown at Figure
the chosen meridian and anti-meridian are known as the central meridians, and
the points at which the central meridians cross the equator are known as the
points of origin.
Properties:
(i) The chart is ortho morphic.
(ii) The scale is correct along the datum meridian and its anti-meridian.
Expands away from the datum meridian at the rate of secant of the great
circle distance.
(iii) Convergency is correct at the equator and the Poles. Elsewhere it is
incorrect and as the meridians are curved, no simple formula can be
given for chart convergence. However, up to 900 miles from the datum
meridian the error is very small and may be neglected for all practical
purposes.
(iv) Any straight line which cuts the central meridian at 90° is a great circle
by construction. Elsewhere, great circles are curves concave to the
datum meridian and anti-meridian giving complex curves. For practical
purposes any straight line drawn near the datum meridian may be taken
to represent a GC.
(v) All meridians and parallels are rhumb lines, curves concave to the nearer
pole.
CYLINDER WITH RADIUS OF REDUCED EART
REDUCED EARTH CENTRAL  SOURCE  EQUATOR
MERIDIANS

PROJECTIONS
TRANSVERSE MERCATOR:
Origin of Projection  Cylindrical: The cylinder touches the reduced Earth
at the selected meridian.
Graticule  Meridians: The datum meridian, the Equator, and
meridians at 90° to the datum meridian are straight lines.
Other meridians are complex curves.
Parallels of Latitude: Ellipses, except the equator.
Close to the pole are nearly circular.
Scale  Correct at the datum meridian. Expands away from the
datum meridian as secant of great circle distance from
the datum meridian.
Convergence  Correct at the Equator and poles.
Rhumb Line  Complex curves. Datum meridian, meridians at 90° to
the datum meridian are straight lines.
Great Circle  Complex curves except the datum meridian.
Datum meridian, Equator, and the meridian at 90° to
the datum can be taken as straight lines.
Any straight line at a right angle to the datum meridian
is a great circle.
Oblique Mercator: The oblique Mercator is a cylindrical projection. In
this case the cylinder touches the reduced Earth along a chosen great circle of
tangency (sometimes referred to as the False Equator).

Properties:
(i) The chart is ortho morphic (conformal).
(ii) The scale is correct along the datum great circle.

(iv) Great circles are curves concave to the datum great circle. Great circles

OBLIQUE MERCATOR
Origin of Projection  Cylindrical: The cylinder touches the reduced Earth

Graticule  Meridians: Curves concave to the datum great circle.

Within 500mm of the great circle of tangency may be

Rhumb Line  Complex curves.

tangency may be interpreted as a straight line
The representation of meridians, parallels, great circles and rhumb lines:
G/C is always towards the pole. Great circle is a curved line convex to the
nearer pole on Mercator chart. On Lamberts and Polar Stereographic charts G/C
is a straight line.
Rhumb line is always towards equator. On a Mercator chart R/L is a straight
line. On Lamberts and Polar Stereographic charts R/L is a curved line concave to
the nearer pole.
32
PROJECTIONS
The use of current aeronautical charts:
For plotting purposes, all conformal charts can be placed into two categories:
Category M: (Standard) Mercator
Category L: All others - usually one of the following: Lambert's Conformal,
Oblique Mercator, Transverse Mercator, Polar Stereographic.
Plotting Positions: With the understanding of the latitude and longitude
scales, we can quickly and accurately plot any known position.
Measuring RL Tracks And Distances:
On Lamberts: Join the two points by a straight line. Align your protractor
along the mid meridian between the two points on the straight line and read off
the R/L arack.
Distance may be measured along the scale diagram or latitude scale.
On Mercator's: On a Mercator projection, a rhumb line cuts every meridian
at the same angle. In other words, it is a line of the same bearing throughout.
Although it does not represent the shortest distance between the points it
connects, this fact is not important unless very large distances are involved.
Since rhumb line is a straight line on Mercator's, track can be measured by
joining the two places by a straight line and measuring track angle anywhere on
the line.
When measuring distances on Mercator's projection the middle latitude scale
between the two points should be used. On Earth's surface, 1" of latitude
may be considered 60 nautical miles in length; whereas, the length of 1° of
longitude varies with latitude. Therefore, the latitude scale must be used for
measuring distance. Al though this scale is expanded on a Mercator chart,
the expansion exactly equal to the expansion of distance at the same latitude.
Therefore, in measuring distance on a Mercator chart, one must be careful
to use the latitude and longitude scale in the area one is measuring. Never use
Plotting Bearings from ADF/VOR: Bearings are always plotted from the
station. Since bearings obtained from Radio Stations are Magnetic, they are first
converted to True. It is not necessary to apply convergency when plotting VOR/
ADF bearing on a Lambert or Polar Stereographic chart. It is however necessary
to apply a correction for conversion angle when plotting VOR bearings on a
Mercator chart, if the change of longitude between aircraft and station is significant.
Detailed procedure to plot bearings is given in Part 3, Radio Navigation.

PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Methods Of Indicating Scale And Relief:
The measurement or calculation of distance is an essential element in
navigation. On a chart, distance is normally measured using a scale bar or using
dividers, but a knowledge of the means of calculating distance is also required and
is covered in this section.
The main systems of measurement:
(a) Imperial measurement - statute mile, yards, feet and inches.
(b) Systems Internationals (SI) - The Metric System: kilometers, meters,
centimeters and millimeters.
(c) Angular Measurement - The nautical mile (nm).
SCALE: The scale of a map or chart is the ratio of a given distance on the
map or chart to the actual distance it represents on the Earth. The scale of a
chart at any point may be expressed in several ways. The four examples of scale
descriptions given below refer to the same chart, and all four are correct:
(a) 1/63,360
(b) 1 to 63,360
(c) 1: 63,360
(d) 1 inch to 1 statute mile (one statute mile being 63,360 inches).
Ratio of Map Distance to Earth Distance is called scale. On a map, scale is
given by following.
Representative fraction (rf)
Statement in words
• Graduated scale.
REPRESENTATIVE FRACTION:
Scale on map is given in a fraction with numerator always as 1.
e.g.  T0,000 • 1,000,000
The smaller the denominator of the representative fraction, the larger the
scale of the chart described.
STATEMENT IN WORDS: A statement gives ratio of map Distance to earth
Distance, e.g.
(i) 1 km = 10 Nm
(ii) On this map 1 cm represents 5 km.
GRADUATED SCALE: A calibrated scale gives, the ratio of map Distance to carth
Distance.
38

Relief. The inequalities in elevation of the surface of the earth represented
on the aeronautical charts by contours, hypsometric tints, spot elevations and hill
shading, the choice of method being affected by the nature and scale of the chart
and its intended use.
Contours and Form Lines: Contours are the lines drawn on a map or chart
joining places of equal elevation. When these are approximately drawn, they are
called form lines.
Spot Heights: Is the presentation of spot elevation in feet or meters as
indicated below: 1730.
Layer Tinting: In order to indicate height intervals, shades of color are
chosen which become deeper with increase of height. Thus at a glance, a pilot can
know the type of terrain.
Hachuring: Hachures are short tapered lines, usually employed to indicate
high ground on charts. These lines radiate away from peaks and high ground.
39
===== [nav 0027]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
In the middle of the tapered lines spot heights showing highest elevation can
also be shown.
Hill shading: is a method of shading so that if a bright light is shining across
the map, shadows are cast by the high ground.

Steps:

3_  Mark equal angles PAB and PBA. Lines AP, BP and MP will point to
the pole.
4. Measure angles at A and B from True North. Keep in mind the
hemisphere. In Northern hemisphere, North is towards the pole.
In Southern hemisphere, North is away from the pole. In this case
true track at A is 030°(T) and at B 150°(T).Convergence is 120°, same
as d'long. Longitude where course is anti clock wise West to East
{090°(T)} is mid longitude, ie, 000°E.

CONVERGENCY
Inclination between two meridians at given latitude is called convergency. It
is max at poles i.e. equal to d'long and is zero at equator and therefore varies as
Sin of latitude.
Earth Convergency(®) = Difference in Longitude(°) x Sine
of Latitude(°)
Unlike the formula for departure distance, which is accurate only when the
two points are at the same latitude, in the case of earth convergency the two points
may be at different latitudes, and the formula for calculating earth convergency
then becomes:
Earth Convergency(°) = Difference in Longitude(°) x Sine
of Mean Latitude(°)
Conversion Angle: Angular diff. between G/C and R/L is called conversion angle.
Conversion angle is a function of convergency and is maximum at poles.
Conversion Angle  = ½ Convergency
0'N
CONVERGENCY
Earth convergency between the two meridians shown in Figure is 40" at
latitude 30"N (80" x sine 30).
The great circle track measured at C is 070°(T); measured at D, it is 110°(T).
the great circle track has changed by 40ạẠi; due to the convergency between the
040°W meridian and the 040°E.
Earth Convergency on Mercator Charts:
Note that all rhumb lines on this projection are shown as straight lines. All
great circle tracks (apart from meridians and the equator) are shown as curved
lines which are concave to the equator, i.e. on the polar side of the rhumb line.
Earth convergency is only correctly shown on this projection at the equator.
At zero degrees latitude Earth convergency is zero (the meridian are parallel)
and this is correctly portraved on the chart. At all other latitude Earth meridians
converge, but on the Mercator chart they are shown as parallel straight lines,
regardless of latitude.
When both G/C bearings are given, to find the hemisphere apply the following
thumb rule:

TIME
Places east of the Prime Meridian experience day or sunrise earlier than the
places lying west of this Meridian. In other words, time is ahead in the East and
behind in the West, at the rate of 4 minutes per degree of longitude.
Following times are used for various purposes:
Local Mean Time (LMT): Time kept at a meridian with respect to position
of sun at observer's anti- meridian is called Local Mean Time. Any place east of
observer will be ahead in IMT. Any place west of observer will be behind in LMT.
LD : Local Date.
Greenwich Mean Time / Co-ordinated Universal Time (GMT/UTC): UTC
is time maintained with international atomic time, which is almost same as GMT.
GMT is LMT of P.M. or Greenwich meridian. This remains same at all positions.
GD/UD : Greenwich/Universal Date.
Zone Time (ZT): 360ồại longitudes divided in to 24 time zones with each zone
of 15° starting from A to Z except letters I and O.
Standard/ Mean Time (ST): Time kept with respect to a particular meridian
is called standard time.
Solving Problems
24 hours = 360ồại of longitude  1 hour = 15° of longitude
1 minute = 15' of longitude  1 second = 15" of longitude Conversely
360° = 24 hours of time  1° = 4 minutes of time
I'= 4 seconds of time  1"= 1/15 second of time
56
TIME
Observer/longitude east (of Greenwich) UTC least (slow on LMT)
Observer/longitude west (of Greenwich) UTC best (fast on LMT)
East Longitude  West Longitude
dlong  dlong
LMT = UTC +  LMT = UTC -
15  15
Some problems include flight time. If such is the case, calculate the T/O time
in UTC. Then add the flight time to obtain the landing time UTC and if required,
convert to LMT. ALWAYS WORK IN UTC.
INTERNATIONAL DATE LINE
When traveling eastwards from Greenwich, an observer would eventually
arrive at longitude 179°59'E, where the LMT is about to become 12 hours more
than UTC. Similarly, an observer traveling westwards from Greenwich would
eventually arrive at 179"59'W, where the LM'T is about to become 12 hours less
then UTC. There is a full day of 24 hours difference between the two observers,
although they are both about to cross the meridian.
When the anti-meridian of Greenwich is crossed, one day is gained or lost,
depending upon the direction of travel. When traveling west, a day is lost (added);
when traveling east, a day is gained (subtracted).
An imaginary line through the Pacific Ocean roughly corresponding to 180ạẠạiại
longitude, to the east of which, by international agreement, the calendar date is
one day earlier than to the west.
Crossing IDL:
1800 LMT  East to West Day is gained, Date lost,
lec 10
West to East Day is lost, Date gained.

PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Sunrise (S/R) and sunset (S/S):
The Sun rises and sets at different times on the same meridian, depending
on the declination of the Sun and the Earthly latitude of the observer. The times
of the phenomena are listed in the Air Almanac in LMT, so the conversion from
one to the other is very necessary knowledge.
A few examples of conversions GMT/LMT:
a) GMT 1200hrs,then L.M Ta60°E (1200 + 15) =1600hrs(60"are =4hrstme)
and LMT at 80W (1200 - 15) = 0640 hrs (80iarc = 5 h 20 m time )
b) What is the GMT and GD of a place in longitude 46ại25'E where LMT is
14h 23m 15s and LD is 2nd June?
46~5' in time = 3h 05m 40s  LMT and LD = 14h 23m 15s 2nd June
GMT least = 1lh 17m 35s and GD still 2nd June
c) IfGD and GMT are 2nd Dec 21h 02m 24s, give LD and IMT at longitude
96"47'E.
96"47' in time = 6h 27m 08s GMT least = 21 h 02 m 24 s 2nd Dec GD
LMT = 03h 29m 32s 3rd Dec LD
The occurrences at specified Latitudes on the Greenwich meridian
may be taken as the same for all longitudes.
Sun rises at the same LMT on same latitude on same date irrespective
of Longitude.
Therefore, find the LMT of the sunrise for that latitude and convert to
UTC for practical applications.
ZONE TIME
EARTH VIEWED
FROM SOUTH POLE
58
TIME
Zone Time (ZT): 360" longitudes divided in to 24 time zones with each zone
of 15° starting from A to Z except letters I and O.
Zone Number (ZN): It is a number which is to be added to zone time
algebraically to find UTC.
ZN = Longitude ÷ 15
When value is ≤ 0.5 → Use lower zone time
When value is > 0.5 → Use higher zone time.
ZT = UTC ‡ ZN (East +, West -)
E.g. ZT at 75°E = 01:00 hrs.  Zone No. = 75 ÷ 15 = 5
UTC = 0100 - 5 = 20:00 hrs.  ZT at 82.50E = 12:00 hrs
Zone No. = 82.5 ÷ 15 = 5.5  UTC = 1200 - 5 = 07:00hrs

True Direction: A direction which is measured with true north is true
direction. As a convention written as ° (T).
Magnetic Direction: Earth's magnetic axis and geographic axis are not
in line. The direction in which a freely suspended magnet will point is called
magnetic north. And the direction which is measured with Respect to magnetic
north is called magnetic direction written asạ(M).
True north is the direction toward the geographic north pole. The earth
rotates around a line between the geographic north and south poles. Most globes
can spin on that line, just as the earth does in real life.
Magnetic north is slightly different. Magnetic north is the direction toward
he magnetic north pole. The earth's natural magnetic field passes through the
north and south magnetic poles.
Compass Direction: When a magnet is placed in A/C in the form of compass
66
COMPASS AND DIRECTIONS
it gets affected by magnetic fields present in the aircraft. The direction in which
compass pointer points is called compass north, written asiạẠạC.).
Symbols:
North  North  North
(True)  (Magnetic)  (Compass)
Variation/ Magnetic Declination: Magnetic variation is the difference
between true north and magnetic north. This is important because a compass
points toward magnetic north, but maps and charts are made with respect to true
north. Magnetic variation can vary tremendously from place to place, and can
even be affected by such things as mineral deposits in the ground.
Magnetic North Pole.
h Pole
ic Equator
Geographic Seu  Magnetic South Pole
Angular difference between true north and magnetic north is called variation/
Magnetic Declination.
If magnetic north lies east of true north, it is called easterly variation (+) and
if it lies west of true north, its termed as westerly variation (-).
Variation and it's changes are given on charts and maps.
===== [nav 0041]

Isogonal Line : Lines joining places of equal variation are called isogonal lines.
Agonal /Agonic Line: Line joining places of zero variation are called agonic lines.
Note:
1. A line drawn on a map or on earth surface depicts or shows true direction.
2.  Variation is given on maps and charts
Variation east - Magnetic least Variation west - Magnetic best
Deviation: When a compass is located near a metallic, magnetic, or electrical
object, it may not point in the correct direction. The difference between the
direction of magnetic north and the direction that the compass points is known as
magnetic deviation. A compass located on an object such as a boat or airplane will
probably have some magnetic deviation.
MEN
Angular difference between magnetic and compass north is called deviation.
If compass north is east of magnetic north, it is termed easterly deviation (+) and
if compass north is west of magnetic north its called westerly deviation(-).
Deviation east - Compass least Deviation west - Compass best

The unit of distance most frequently used in navigation is the nauticall. Length of NM is more at:
fundamental to navigation.
The nautical mile is defined as the distance on the surface of the Earth along
a great circle which subtends an angle of I' of arc measured at the centre of
curvature of the Earth.
h Pole  Great
40°20 Nor
Circle
2420 nms
Equator
South Pole
A meridian is a semi-great circle; l' along a meridian can be assumed to
represent 1nm. Therefore, 1° of change of latitude along a meridian is assumed
a distance of 60nm.
Great Circle Distance = Change in latitide in degrees × 60 nm
Calculating Departure (Rhumb Line) Distance: The rhumb line distance is
in the east/west direction, along a parallel of latitude. Rhumb line distance is also
called 'departure.
One degree change of latitude along any meridian always represents a
distance of 60 nm. One degree of change of longitude will, however, only measure
60 nm at the Equator; this is because the Equator is the only parallel of latitude
which is a great circle.
Departure Distance is in nautical mile in east west direction at given latitude.
Departure is maximum at the equator and zero at poles, varies as function of
cosine of latitude.
Departure is Rhumb Line Distance:
Departure Distance (nm) = Difference in long (min) × cos lat

MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
General Principles: Magnetism is a property which may be possessed bi
iron, steel and a few other chemical elements such as nickel, cobalt and certain
special alloys. The main properties of a magnet are:
The ability to attract material made of iron and other magnetic
materials;
• When freely suspended it will tend to align in an approximately North-
South direction depending on its position on the earth;
The attracting force tends to be concentrated at each end of the
magnet. The end which is north-seeking is called the North (or red)
pole and the end which is south-seeking the South (or blue) pole;
Like poles repel, unlike poles attract;
The strength of the attracting or repelling force between two magnets
decreases as the square of the distance between them.
Terrestrial Magnetism:
Earth possesses a Magnetic field similar to that of a magnet.
Earth's magnetic field behaves as if there is a very powerful magnetic
bar located at the center of the earth.
Earth's magnetic pole is not co-located with Geographic North. It is
also not stationery. It rotates around True North slowly. It takes appx.
960 years for one orbit. At present it is located 750 mm from the True
Pole in North Canada.
86
MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
Resolution of the Earth's Total Magnetic Force into Vertical and Horizontal
Components:
Angle that a free compass card makes with horizontal is called magnetic dip.
A freely suspended magnetic card is affected by two forces that are
(i) Horizontal (H)
(ii) Vertical (Z)
The Effects Of Change Of Latitude On These Components:
Dip. Dip is the name given to the angle below the local horizontal, taken up
by a freely suspended magnet when aligning itself with the Earth's total magnetic
field. Since this field tends to follow the contours illustrated in Figure, it can be
seen that the angle of dip increases with increase in 'magnetic latitude' until, at
the North and South Magnetic Poles, it is 90° (with the magnet vertical). It can be
concluded therefore that the angle of dip is zero at the magnetic equator increasing
to a maximum of 90° at the magnetic poles. (Note, the term magnetic inclination
may also be used to describe dip). A direct heading compass is unreliable beyond
70ºN / 70°S due to dip.

magnetic and geographical poles.
AGONIC LINE - Lines joining places of nil variation.

Directive Force. The force that causes the alignment of the magnet with the
Earth's field is known as the directive force and it is given, by convention, the
representative letter H. it is the horizontal component of the total force present
(represented by the letter T) at any location.
Vertical Component. The total magnetic force T may consist ofboth horizontal
and vertical components depending on location, the vertical component is given
the representative letter Z. Figure illustrates the relationship between T, H and Z

The Variation In Directive Force: As can be seen from Figure, the proportion
of the total force which is H must reduce with increase in magnetic latitude until
it is zero at the poles (where only Z is present). It can be concluded, therefore,
that magnetic compass system give poor indications near to the poles and are
completely useless very close to the poles.
Variation: In navigation, at a given place and time, the horizontal angle
between the true north and magnetic north measured east or west according
to whether magnetic north lies east or west of true north, also called magnetic
declination.
In cartography, the annual change in direction of the horizontal component
of the Earth's magnetic field.

MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
Aircraft Magnetism
Hard Iron And Vertical Soft Iron
Hard Iron: They are difficult to magnetize and once magnetized, they tend
to retam magnetism.
Soft Iron: They are easy to magnetize but loose their magnetism when
magnetic force is removed.
A/C Magnetism
Hard Iron  Soft Iron
(Peramanent)  (Temporary)
Hard Iron Component: They are Permanent in nature and are acquired
during manufacturing process due hammering and earth's magnetic field. It does
not change with latitude or heading of a/c.
Soft Iron Component: It is induced magnetism due to earth's magnetic field.
It is not constant and changes with latitude and heading of a/c.
Vertical Soft Iron Component: Soft iron magnetism induced in parts of the
aircraft structure by surrounding fields-the most important of these being the earth.
The earth's field has a vertical as well as horizontal component. However, again for
our purposes we will within the constrains of the syllabus only consider vertical soft
iron (VSI) magnetism (Z is the vertical component of the earths field and H is the
horizontal component). The component Z has an increasing affect with latitude as
the compass magnets try to follow the earth's flux lines therefore VSI magnetism
must also vary with latitude. However, Z is zero at the equator, where the horizontal
component H is greatest, so no VSI magnetism is induced there.
The Resulting Magnetic Fields
Components of Hard Iron Magnetism:
Component: 'P' - It is permanent magnetism induced along longitudinal axis
or fore and aft axis. It is positive when blue pole/ south seeking pole is induced
ahead of compass.
Component: 'O'- It is permanent magnetism induced along lateral or
athwartship axis. It is positive when blue pole is induced right of compass.
Component : 'R' - It is permanent magnetism along vertical/ normal axis
of a/c. It is positive when blue pole is beneath/ below. Effect of component R is
negligible and can be ignored.

Components of Soft Iron Magnetism:
Component 'Cz': It is vertical soft iron component in longitudinal axis.
Component 'Fz': It is vertical soft iron component along lateral/ athwartships
axis.
Change of Deviation with Change of Latitude: Hard iron magnetism is
permanent and does not vary with magnetic latitude but, the deviation caused by
hard iron magnetism increases with latitude because in high latitudes where H
is weaker, the compass magnets are more easily deflected. The deviating effect of
hard iron magnetism varies with the heading of an aircraft.
Deviation due to Horizontal Hard Iron Component = 1/H
Vertical soft iron magnetism increases when an aircraft moves to a higher
latitude because Z increases. The deviation caused by vertical soft iron magnetism
in higher latitudes is doubly increased because H is reduced. The deviating effect
of the vertical soft iron component of coefficients B and C varies with the heading
of an aircraft.
Deviation due to Vertical Soft Iron = Z/H= Tan Dip

misalignment of lubber line and asymmetrically distributed horizontal soft iron
magnetism around the compass. It is calculated by taking the average deviation
on eight or more equidistant headings. This is caused due to following reasons:
(a) Misalignment of lubber line which is called apparent coefficient 'A'.
(b) Horizontal component of soft iron effect which gives deviation is called
real coefficient 'A'.

COMPASS SWING
The process of aligning compass north with magnetic north is called compass
Landing compass: There are many procedures for measuring the magnitude
of the co-efficients affecting an aircraft compass. Most of these techniques involve
an accurate datum compass (such as a Medium Landing Compass), the reading of
which is compared with the reading of the aircraft compass on various headings.
The Medium Landing Compass is a small portable bearing compass, having
a high degree of accuracy, which can be used for ground calibration of aircraft
compasses. It is designed to use on the ground and is fitted with a tripod and a
bubble level. The horizontal compass card is read through a prism which forms
part of the sighting head.
Occasions when a compass should be swung or may require swinging:
> On a acceptance of new aircraft from manufacturer.
> When a new compass is fitted.
• Every three months.
> After a major inspection.
> With any change of magnetic material in the aircraft.
> If transferred to another base involving a large change of latitude.
> After a lightning strike or after flying in static.
> After standing on one heading for more than 4 weeks.
• When carrying magnetic freight.
> Whenever specified in the maintenance schedule.
92
MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
> For the issue of a C of A.
> Following a heavy landing.
> At any time when the compass or recorded deviation is suspect.
Deviation: Deviation is the angular difference between the compass indicated
direction and the direction of the local magnetic meridian (Magnetic North). The
purpose of a compass swing is to analyse and correct for as much of this deviation
as possible.
Deviation can occur as a result of hard iron and soft iron magnetism within an
aircraft and also as a result of misalignment of reference lines from which readings
are taken or of the detector alignment in a remote sensing compass system.
Corrections For Deviation:
> Apparent Coff. 'A' is adjusted by adjustment of lubber line.
Coefficient 'B' and 'C' are corrected by micro adjusters / screws. Once
found, coefficients B and C can be neutralized to some extent by
setting up a local magnetic field, this correcting device is called the
> Coff 'B' is adjusted on easterly heading.
> Coff 'C' is adjusted on northerly heading.
> Residual deviation is corrected by providing a deviation card or
compass card.

ERRORS DUE TO DIP
Magnetic Dip causes acceleration/deceleration and Turning errors, as
explanied below. The effect of dip in direct reading compass is countered by low
center of gravity.
ACCELERATION / DECELERATION ERRORS
Acceleration and deceleration errors are fluctuations in the compass during
changes in speed. In the Northern Hemisphere, the compass swings towards
the north during acceleration, and towards the south during deceleration. In
Southern hemisphere, opposite happens. When the speed stabilizes, the compass
returns to an accurate indication. This error is most pronounced when flying on
a heading of east or west, and decreases gradually when flying closer to a north
or south heading. The error does not occur when flying directly north or south.
There is no Acceleration/ Deceleration error when flying near the magnetic
equator.
An acceleration always produces an apparent turn towards the
magnetic pole which is physically closest to the aircraft;
• A deceleration always produces an apparent turn towards the
magnetic equator:
• The memory aid, APDE may help in recalling this error.
96
MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
Remember AP: Acceleration produces apparent turn to the nearest Pole and
Deceleration towards the Equator:
There is no acceleration/Deceleration error when flying directly north or south

TURNING ERRORS
Turning errors are most apparent when turning to or from a heading of
north or south. This error increases as the poles are neared and magnetic dip
becomes more apparent. There is no turning error when flying near the magnetic
equator.
(a) During a turn through the pole which is physically nearer to the
aircraft, the compass will be sluggish. It is therefore necessary to roll
out early when using the direct reading compass.
(b) During a turn through the pole which is physically further from the
aircraft, the compass will be lively. It is therefore necessary to roll out
late when using the direct reading compass.
97
===== [nav 0056]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Remember PS. Turning through Pole compass is Sluggish (Under reads).
Turning through Equator compass is Fast (Over reads).
The amount of lead or lag is maximum on the north-south headings and
depends upon the angle of bank used and geographic position of the airplane
with regard to latitude.
An aircraft flying at 45°N latitude making a turn to north from cast or wes
naintaining a standard rate turn a pilot would need to roll out of the turn wher
the compass was 45 degrees plus one half of the bank angle before north. (From
east to north at 90 knots 0+ (45 - 8)= 37. A pilot would begin to roll out to straight
flight and on a heading of north when 37 degrees was read from the compass.
(From west to north at 90 knots 360 - (45 - 8) =323. A pilot would begin to roll
the aircraft out of the bank at 322 degrees read from the compass to fly on a
north heading. Making a turn towards south from west the pilot would have to
roll the aircraft out of the turn when the compass was 45 degrees minus half the
bank angle (from west to south at 90 knots 180 - (45 - 8) = 143, from east to south
180+(45 - 8) =217).
There is no Turning Error in East West Direction

arning from 135' to 225"
Turning from 045' to 315*
To remember both Acceleration/ Deceleration errors and turning errors:
Remember APPS: Acceleration produces apparent turn to the nearest Pole.
Turning through Pole compass is Sluggish. Inversely, decceleration produces the
apparent turn to the Equator and turning through Equator compass over reads.
Liquid Swirl: Ideally the compass bowl will have a smooth internal surface
and be full of a low viscosity fluid. If these conditions are not fully met, there will
be a tendency for the liquid to be dragged around the bowl during turns. Once
98
MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
in motion the liquid will continue to swirl under its own momentum. Should it
occur, this fluid swirl will carry with it the magnetic assembly, thereby displacing
it from its correct orientation. The displacement will therefore be in the direction
of the turn and a clockwise turn (135° through south to 225i) with liquid swirl will
result in a clockwise turn of the magnets and an under-reading compass.
Depending on the hemisphere and the direction of turn, the effect of liquid swirl
will either increase or decrease the turning error. At the magnetic equator liquid
swirl would be the only source of any error in a turn.
Keeping Magnetic Materials Clear Of The Compass:
The compass should be sited such that no single item of non electrical
equipment causes a compass deviation of more then 1°. The sum of the deviations
caused by all such equipment must not exceed 2ạ. Similarly, no single item of
electrical equipment, or its associated wiring, may cause a compass deviation of
more then 1°, and again the sum of the deviations caused by all such equipment
and wiring may not exceed 2°.
The Direct Reading Magnetic compass (DRC) is based on a simple magnetic
needle, and points toward the northern end of the Earth's magnetic field. It is
also installed in an instrument of dimensions and weight that makes it suitable for
use in aero planes. It is a mandatory requirement that all modern civil transport
aero planes carry a direct reading non-stabilised magnetic compass as a standby
direction indicator. The most commonly found direct reading compass is the 'E'
type, which is illustrated below.

PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
THE VERTICAL CARD COMPASS. The vertical card compass - which is
also known as the B-type or E-type - is the direct reading compass in general use.
It is usually the main magnetic heading reference in light aircraft and the standby
compass in larger aircraft.
It consists of a circular compass card attached directly to the magnet assembly
This combined unit is suspended in liquid within the compass bowl. A vertical
lubber line on the glass window of the bowl, enables the heading to be read off
the compass card.
THE GRID RING COMPASS. The P-type compass or grid ring compass is
found on older aircraft. It is more accurate than the vertical card compass and is
more stable.
It is however heavier, bulkier and more expensive. In addition it can only be
read in straight and level flight, as the grid ring has to be unclamped and aligned
with the north reference before a reading can be taken against the lubber line.
The grid ring compass also differs from the vertical card compass in that it
achieves a greater periodicity by the addition of damping wires which also rotate
through the compass liquid.
DRC magnetic compass - liquid swirled compass
Properties of a compass /DRC:
Jewelled
pivot  Collar
Sleeve
Magnets  Magnets
Pivot post
Fig. DRC magnet assembly suspension system
(i) Horizontality: A compass card should remain horizontal with respect
to earth surface when moved away form magnetic equator, This is achieved by
following:
(a)  Suspending magnets below pivot point to reduce effect of dip. The
dipping effect of earth's vertical component (Z) is opposed by the
weight of magnets.
100
MAGNETISM AND COMPASSES
(b) By shifting pivot point towards nearest pole.
(ii) Aperiodicity (Dead Beat): If a suspended magnet is deflected from its
position of equilibrium and released, it oscillates between positions on either side
of the equilibrium position for some time before coming to rest. The ideal compass
would stop without oscillation, when it could be said to be aperiodic. Aperiodicity
is attained in aircraft compasses by:
(a) The bowl is filled with methyl alcohol or a silicone fluid, and damping
filaments are fitted to the magnet system.
(b) Several short powerful magnets are used instead of one large one,
there by increasing the righting force, and reducing the moment of
inertia.
(c) The apparent weight of the system is reduced by the buoyancy of the
liquid, and that weight is concentrated near the center; by mounting
close to the pivot. The moment of inertia is even further reduced.
(iii) Sensitivity: Needle should stay firmly fixed along the magnetic meridian.
This is achieved by:
(a) increasing the pole strengths of the magnets.
(b) keeping pivot friction at minimum by using iridium for the pivot.
(c) this is suspended in liquid which reduces the effective weight of the
system and lubricates the pivot.
DRC - Serviceability Tests :
Check liquid is free from bubbles, discoloration, sediment.
Examine all parts for luminosity.
Ensure that grid ring rotates freely through 360°, and that locking
device functions positively.
Test suspension of bowl by moving gently in all directions and that
there is no metal to metal feeling.
• Test for pivot friction: deflect the magnet system through 10-15° each
way, and note the reading on return: each should be within 2" of the
other.
• Test for damping: deflect system through 90°, hold for 30 seconds to
allow liquid to settle and time its return through 85°. The maximum
and minimum times are laid down in the manufacture's Instrument
Manual, usually about 6.5 seconds to 8.5 seconds.
101
===== [nav 0058]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
The Direction Indicator (DI):
DI employs a horizontal axis tied gyro and utilizes the principle of rigidity to
indicate aircraft's heading. A spinning rotor has the property of rigidity in space.
Thus once the DI rotor attains its full speed and its axis is manually aligned with a
datum (true or magnetic north) it will continue to point in that direction in space
during the rest of the flight. When the aircraft alters its heading it does so relative
to the gyro axis, that is, the aircraft and the gyro case turn about the gyro axis.
Changes in headings are thus indicated instantaneously.
Working: It does not have its own north seeking element and needs to be
aligned after start and at frequent intervals with DRC.
Some more expensive heading indicators are 'slaved' to a sensor (called a
flux gate). The flux gate continuously senses the earth's magnetic field, and a
servo mechanism constantly corrects the heading indicator. These 'slaved gyros'
reduce pilot workload by eliminating the need for manual realignment every ten
to fifteen minutes.
REMOTE INDICATION COMPASS/SLAVE GYRO COMPASS/GYRO
MAGNETIC COMPASS
Drawbacks of Director Reading Compass:
• If suffers from turning and acceleration error caused due to dip.
• The magnet in a DRC is also susceptible to deviation due to A/C magnetism.
Drawbacks of DI:
• It needs to be aligned at frequent intervals.
• It also suffers from drift.
PRINCIPLE OF REMOTE INDICATING COMPASS:
Remote indicating compass utilizes the earth magnetic field to sense magnetic
north. The magnetic flux is used to provide constant correction required by Gyro
element of compass to maintain a magnetic no. reference.
Gyroscopic stabilization reduces the turning and acceleration error.
Since there is no magnet, you need to give continuous input to tell where
magnetic north is, which is given by detection.
102

DETECTOR UNIT:
It contains flux valve that senses the direction of earth magnetic field
related to A/C hdg.
A/C sensing unit consists of 3 simple flux valves connecting at central
point and spaced at 120" interval.
The assembly is pendulously suspended so as to maintain horizontal
regardless of A/C attitude. It is normally mounted at wing tip or near
top of fin, to isolate it from aircraft deviation sources.
WORKING:
1. The detector unit is mounted so as to remain horizontal (because it senses
in horizontal) in order to best sense the horizontal component of earth's
magnetism. This is fixed so that one spoke is permanently aligned with A/C
longitudinal axis.
2. The secondary output of magnetic flux is given to selsyning unit. The
combination of electro magnetic field reproduces a flux field identical to that
sensed by selsyning unit.
Selsyn Rotor - The null seeking rotor will always be at right angle to the
selsyn reproduced earth magnetic field. The pointer or card is attached to rotor.
ADVANTAGES OF A REMOTE INDICATING GYRO MAGNETIC
COMPASS:
The advantages of a remote indicating magnetic compass over a DI or direct
reading instrument are:
The DI suffers from real and apparent drift and needs resetting
in flight. In addition, when resetting to the magnetic compass, the
airplane must be flown straight and level, whereas the detector unit
constantly monitors the gyro magnetic compass.
103
===== [nav 0059]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
The detector unit can be installed in a remote part of the aero plane,
well away from electrical circuits and other influences due to airframe
magnetism.
The flux valve technique used in the detector unit senses the Earth's
magnetic meridian rather then seeking it, which makes the system
more sensitive to small components of 'H'.
• The unit provides a heading reference to higher magnetic latitudes
than the direct reading magnetic compass.
• Turning and acceleration errors are minimized because:
1. The detector unit is fixed in azimuth.
2. The precession signals aligning the compass are kept to a low value (typically
5°/minute).
3. There is a roll cut out switch that isolates the precession system during turns at
bank angles of greater than 10°.
The compass may be detached from the detector unit by a simple
switch selection to work as a DI, so a normal DI is not required.
The system can be readily used to monitor other equipment (e.g.
autopilot, Doppler, RMI, etc).
• Repeaters can also be made available to as many crew stations or
system as desired.
DISADVANTAGES OF THE REMOTE INDICATING GYRO MAGNETIC
COMPASS:
The disadvantages of a gyro magnetic compass over a DI or direct reading
instrument are that it is:
Much heavier than a direct reading compass.
Much more expensive.
Electrical in operation and therefore susceptible to electrical failure.
Much more complicated than a DI or a direct reading compass.
104

Q4. Given that T = 50 micro-tesla (m T), dip angle = 60°, calculate H and Z.
Ans.  H = T x cos dip, therefore, in this case  H = 50 x cos 60,
H = 25 mT;
and, Z = H x tan dip, therefore, in this case  7 = 25 z tan 60,
Z = 25 x 1.732 = 43.3 mT

Chapter
9
DEAD RECKONING
NAVIGATION (DR)
Dead reckoning, as applied to flying, is the navigation of an airplane solely by
means of computations based on airspeed, course, heading, wind direction and
speed, groundspeed, and elapsed time.
Basics Of Dead Reckoning:
Track: A physical path being followed or to be followed on earth surface is
always in °(T) unless specified. Required track (RT) is the track required to follow
for going from one place to another. Track Made Good (TMG) is the track or
path actually followed by aircraft over the surface of earth. It can be obtained by
joining starting ground position and known ground position at any time.
Heading(H): The direction in which A/C longitudinal axis is aligned / nose is
pointed. Direction measured in degrees clockwise from true north, in which the
nose of the plane should point to make good the desired course.
Course: The intended direction of flight. Direction of the line connecting
two desired points, drawn on the chart and measured clockwise in degrees from
true north on the mid-meridian.
Heading and course are in the horizontal plane measured clockwise in
degrees from Compass, Magnetic, True or Grid North.
Wind Velocity /Wind Speed: The direction from which the wind is blowing
and it's speed of movement on earth.
121
===== [nav 0068]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Airspeed (IAS, CAS, TAS, Mach Number) :
ASIR - Airspeed Indication Reading. That which is indicated by an ASI
without correction.
IAS - Indicated Airspeed. The speed shown by the pitot/static airspeed
indicator calibrated to reflect standard atmosphere adiabatic compressible flow au
MSL. And uncorrected for airspeed system errors.
CAS - Calibrated Airspeed. The IAS corrected for position and instrument
error. It is equal to TAS at MSL. in a standard atmosphere. (Note. In the UK, CAS
is often referred to as Rectified Airspeed (RAS) and is labeled as such on the CRp
5Computer).
EAS - Equivalent Airspeed. The CAS corrected for compressibility at the
particular pressure altitude under consideration. It is equal to CAS in a standard
atmosphere. (When applying the effect of compressibility, the raw TAS is found
first; if TAS is >300kt, this value is then corrected (reduced) by the amount
necessary for compressibility).
TAS - True Airspeed. The EAS corrected for density error and it is the true
speed of the aeroplane relative to the undisturbed air.
Mach number: It is ratio of TAS and LSS (local speed of sound). Since local
speed of sound is not constant, for same TAS Mach number changes.
Ground Speed: Applying head/tail wind component to TAS give ground
speed. Obtained by measuring the length of the True Course line on the wind
triangle (using the scale employed for drawing the diagram).
Total distance - Obtained by measuring the length of the TC line on the
chart (using the scale at the bottom of the chart).
Estimated time en route (ETE) - total distance divided by GS.
Fuel rate - predetermined fuel used per hour at cruising speed.
NOTE: Additional fuel for adequate reserve should be added as a safety measure.
ETA: Estimated time of arrival. For IFR flights, the time at which it is estimated
that the aircraft will arrive over that designated point, defined by reference to
navigation aids, from which it is intended that an instrument approach procedure
will be commenced, or, if no navigation aid is associated with the aerodrome, the
time at which the aircraft will arrive over the aerodrome. For VFR flights, the
time at which it is estimated that the aircraft will arrive over the aerodrome.
DR-Position: Dead reckoning (DR): is the process of estimating one's current
position based upon a previously determined position, or fix, and advancing that
122
DEAD RECKONING NAVIGATION (DR)
position based upon known or estimated speeds over elapsed time, and course.
While traditional methods of dead reckoning are no longer considered primary
means of navigation, modern inertial navigation systems, which also depend upon
dead reckoning, are very widely used.
A disadvantage of dead reckoning is that since new positions are calculated
solely from previous positions, the errors of the process are cumalative, so the
error in the position fix grows with time.
Fix: A fix is an accurate ground position of the aircraft determined by visual,
radio or radar navigation aids. The accuracy of the fix is dependent on the
accuracy of the aids used.
Use of The Navigational Computer: Navigation computers like manually
operated E6B and electronically operated CX3 can expedite calculations like
Speed, Time, Distance, Fuel Consumption, Conversions, Heading, Airspeed,
Wind Velocity etc. The mechanical or electronic computer has an instruction book
that probably includes sample problems so the pilot can become familiar with its
functions and operation. Scientific calculators are also useful in calculations.
The Triangle Of Velocities: Six variables given in the fig. constitute a
velocity triangle. If four variables are known, other two can be found. Heading/
TAS is represented by a single arrow, (Course) Track/GS by two arrows and Wind
Direction/ Wind Speed by three arrows. Single and three arrows always follow
each other and double arrow is the resultant opposing them.
The Air Vector: This describes the path of the aircraft through the air. The
heading is the direction the aircraft flies in relation to the air mass. The aircraft's
speed through the air is the true airspeed. The two sub-components of the air
vector are heading (HDG) and true airspeed (TAS). The air vector is shown below:
HEADING/TAS
The Wind Vector: The wind vector describes the movement of the air mass
through which the aircraft is traveling, over the surface of the Earth. Wind
velocity, when written, includes the direction from which the wind is blowina and
the speed (usually in knots). It is written as a 5 or 6 figure group eg, 330/25. The
diagram shows the air vector and wind vector.
HEADING/TAS  WD/WS
123
===== [nav 0069]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
The Ground Vector: This describes the direction and speed of the aircraf
over the ground. It is compass Course/track (TRK) and groundspeed (GS). The
diagram below shows the completed triangle of velocities:
HEADING/TAS  WD/WS
COURSE/GS
Wind Correction Angle: Wind Correction Angle (WCA) is the angle that
our Heading Angle must deviate from the Desired Course to correct for the
effect the Wind is having on our aircraft, determined from the wind triangle. It is
equivalent to Drift and applied on the opposite direction of the Drift eg, if Drift
is 7"S, WCA will be 7°P.
Drift (D): Angular difference between heading and track made good is called
drift.
HEADING/TAS  WD/WS
HEADING/TAS  WD/WS
DRIFT7"S
WCA7°P  WTA
COURSE/GS
COURSE/GS
WCA= Wind Correction Angle, WTA= Wind to track Angle
Desired Course = Heading + WCA or Heading + Drift
(WCA is added to heading if the wind is from the left; subtracted if wind is
from the right. Opposite is true for Drift.)
Drift converts heading to track or track to heading. If you want to go
from true to compass you need variation and deviation.
• Heading is the direction in which the aircraft is pointing; track is its
path over the Earth. The angle between heading and track is drift and
it is measured from heading to track.
• Starboard drift is subtracted from track to give heading or added to
heading to give track.
• Port drift is added to track to give heading or subtracted from heading
to give track.
When track/ (TMG) is right of heading it is called STARBOARD drift
and when track is left of heading, it is called PORT drift.
124
DEAD RECKONING NAVIGATION (DR)
Track Error (TE): Angular difference between track required and track
made good.
IF TMG is right of track required, it is called STARBOARD track error and
when it is left of track required it is called PORT arack error.
TMG/RT/COURSE RIGHT OF HEADING, DRIFT STARBOARD
TMG/RT/COURSE LEFT OF HEADING, DRIFT PORT

In UK usage, track refers to the aircraft's actual movement over the ground.
It may be the required or desired track, the calculated or DR (dead reckoning)
track or the track already achieved usually referred to as the TMG (track made
good).
In USA usage, and so often encountered when operating American aircraft
and instrument systems, course is used to indicate the required track and track is
reserved to describe the actual path over the ground.
Calculations: For doing calculations using CX3 calculator, Course and TAS
and Heading and GS are clubbed together. Wind vector is represented as wind
direction and speed. This pairing is because a pilot knows his course and TAS and
winds while planning. He has to calculate his heading and ground speed from
available data. While flying a heading into the winds, the pilot will get desired
course and resultant ground speed.

Time and Distance Problems: Find out Ground speed first and apply the
following formula-
Time = Distance ÷ Ground Speed
Runway and Winds: Questions can be solved easily by putting values in CX2
Alternately, use the following formulas.
Along-runway component = (-) WS X cost
(Ans - head wind, +tail wind)
Across-runway component = WS X sinel
(Ans + cross from starboard, - cross from port)
Where 0 is the difference between the wind direction and the runway.
0 = Wind direction (M) - Runway Heading (M)
The above formula, which are written as they will be entered into the
calculator, will give positive results in the case of head winds (0 less than 90° or
more than 270) but negative for tail winds (0 090" through 180" to 270°).
This accords with the standard practice for this type of calculation.
When evaluating runway components, it should be realized that runways
are normally described by their magnetic directions and so the WV should be
converted into a magnetic direction also.

- 40 cos 210 = 35, indicating a 35 kts tail wind component
Cross wind = 40xSin 210 = - 20 kts from Port.
