Determination of DR position
Need For DR: "Dead" Reckoning (or "Ded" for Deductive Reckoning) is a
basic navigational method used by low speed, small airplane pilots. It is based
on mathematical calculations to plot a course using the elements of a course line,
airspeed, course, heading and elapsed time. During this process pilots make use
of a flight computer. Manual or electronic flight computers are used to calculate
time-speed-distance measurements, fuel consumption, density altitude and many
other en route data necessary for navigation.
Confirmation of Flight Progress (Mental DR): Mental DR is the mental
calculation of the aeroplane's progress so that its position can be assessed.
alterations to heading determined, and revisions of ETA calculated, as necessarv
132
DEAD RECKONING NAVIGATION (DR)
Time Adjustments :
(a) Planning the route so that there is a convenient corner before the
destination or turning point which can be cut or extended to gain or
lose time.
(b) Flying a 'dog leg' or similar alteration of heading to lose time.
(c) Adjusting the airspeed.
Cutting the Corner: When there is a large track alteration along the route,
say 60° or more, it is possible to aajust timing by extending or cutting the corner
at that turning point.
Losing Time by 60° Dog Leg: Flying a dog leg can serve to lose a lot of
time in a short distance along track. The technique is to alter heading by 60° in
either direction for the length of time to be lost, then to turn through 120° in
the opposite direction for the same length of time in order to regain track. The
aircraft will then have flown two sides of an equilateral triangle, and the time lost
will be equal to the time taken to fly one side.
Adjusting the Airspeed: Providing that the speed range of the aircraft
permits and any fuel penalty is acceptable, small timing errors can be corrected
by altering speed.
Lost Procedures: Getting lost in an aircraft is a potentially dangerous
situation especially when low on fuel. If a pilot becomes lost, there are some good
common sense procedures to follow. If a town or city cannot be seen, the first
thing to do is climb, being mindful of traffic and weather conditions. An increase
in altitude increases radio and navigation reception range, and also increases
radar coverage. If flying near a town or city, it might be possible to read the name
of the town on a water tower.
If the aircraft has a navigational radio, such as a VOR or ADF receiver, it
can be possible to determine position by plotting an azimuth from two or more
navigational facilities. If GPS is installed, or a pilot has a portable aviation GPS
on board, it can be used to determine the position and the location of the nearest
airport.
Communicate with any available facility using frequencies shown on the
chart. If contact is made with a controller, radar vectors may be offered. Other
facilities may offer direction finding (DF) assistance. If the situation becomes
threatening, transmit the situation on the emergency frequency 121.5 MHz
and set the transponder to 7700. Most facilities, and even airliners, monitor the
emergency frequency.
133

135
===== [nav 0075]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Heading and TAS Vector Since Last Confirmed Position: It takes a certain
amount of time to plot a fix. Should the fix indicate that it is necessary to alter
heading to make the next turning point it is no use calculating the new heading
from the fix position, since the aircraft has by now flown beyond this point. It is
therefore necessary to DR AHEAD, and to calculate an alteration of heading from
a point ahead of the aircraft.
Application of Wind Velocity Vector: Winds aloft affect both the ground
speed of the aircraft, and the heading that the aircraft must fly to stay on the
desired true course.
Wind affects the aircraft's flight path in two ways, namely drift and
groundspeed. The angle between aircraft track and the wind direction is called
the wind angle; the actual values of drift and changes to speed will very depending
on the wind angle and wind speed compared to the aircraft's track and TAS.
Last Known Track and Ground Speed Vector: Heading TAS vector gives us
aircraft direction in still air conditions. Application of wind vector gives us Track
and Ground Speed vector.
Calculate estimated Air Position and ground position: A fix is an accurate
ground position of the aircraft determined by visual, radio or radar navigation
aids. The accuracy of the fix is dependent on the accuracy of the aids used.
Heading/TAS vector multiplied by time gives us estimated Air Position, Track
and Ground speed vector multiplied by time gives us estimated ground position.
Assessment of Accuracy of DR Position: The principle of visual navigation is
to compare an estimated or Dead Reckoning (DR) position with visual references
in order to determine the actual position of the aircraft and make the necessary
adjustment to speed and heading. Therefore the pilot must have the ability to:
(a)  Select Features
(b)  Identify landmarks
(c)  Estimate distance
(d) Estimate direction
The pilot will need the ability to build a mental picture of the terrain and
features along the planned route, estimating ranges and bearing of features
relative to each other both in the air and on the map will greatly assist the pilo:
in this task. It is also necessary to have a sound knowledge of the map being used
and the techniques of track keeping and timing. Thorough map preparation will
considerably reduce the complexities of map reading.
136
DEAD RECKONING NAVIGATION (DR)
Resolution of Current DR Problems by Means of Charts:
• Mercator Charts :
Track. Because a straight-line on a Mercator is a rhumb line, it is only possible
to plot rhumb line tracks. If it is necessary to plot the shortest distance between
two points a considerable distance apart, then the great circle would have to be
approximated by a series of rhumb line tracks; the length of these tracks being
dependent on the latitude and the distance between the points. The path of the
great circle would have to be transferred as a series of points from a gnomonic
projection if the route distance exceeds 1000nm. Between 12N and 12S, the
difference between the rhumb line and great circle is small enough to be ignored.
Distance. The scale expansion of this chart makes the measurement of
distance a more difficult task then on other charts. As one minute of latitude
equals one nautical mile, the latitude graduations on the meridians must be used
for this purpose. The track should be divided into segments of approximately
100nm to 200nm. Lengths are measured against the nearest meridian to the
segment at the same latitude.
• Lambert Charts
Track. The convergence of the meridians on the chart is equal to a fraction
of the change of longitude, known as the constant of the cone or convergence
factor. This means that the true direction of a track changes progressively along
the track. Because the DR ahead is usually only for a short distance, it is accurate
enough to use the initial track at the beginning of the track for this purpose.
Distance. It the separation of the standard parallels (SP's) on any plotting
chart is less then 16°, the scale error, either contraction between the SP's or
expansion outside of the SP's, is less then 1%. The scale is, therefore, considered
to be constant. The latitude graduations on the meridians or the scale bar in the
legend may be used for measuring distances.
• Polar Stereographic Projections
Track. Because this chart is not used for navigational purposes at latitude
less then 70°, then great circles are almost straight lines. Those that pass through
the pole are exact straight lines. For navigational purposes then, a straight line
is considered to be a great circle. Due to the convergence of the meridians, the
true direction changes rapidly along track. To accurately plot a track, its direction
should be plotted as a mean direction at the mid point of the track. Of course this
is not always possible then projecting a DR position ahead of the present position
but because this distance is relatively short it is sufficiently accurate to plot the
initial track direction at the start time and position.
137
===== [nav 0076]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Distance. The scale of this chart expands with latitude away from the pole. I
is less then 1% in error at 75° latitude. It is therefore considered to be a constant
scale chart. Distance may be measured from the latitude graduations on any
meridian or from the scale bar in the legend of the chart.
Miscellaneous DR uncertainties and practical means of
correction
DR position is only an approximation. There is, therefore, a degree of
uncertainty regarding this position. This is referred to as the circle of error. The
size of this circle is dependent on the time interval since the last positive fix. The
longer the time interval, the greater is the radius of the circle. The radius of the
circle of error is approximately equal to 5% of the TAS.
To plot a DR position, it is essential to accurately determine the track and
distance traveled along that track since the last fix. If the Doppler computer is
used to determine the DR position, then if it was updated to last positive fix, the
error around the DR position is an ellipse. The dimensions of the ellipse are.
along track 0.1% of the distance and across track 0.2% since the last fix.
Q 53. An aircraft at F 290 commences descend at 50 NMs from VOR and reports
over VOR at FL 80. GS 243 Kts. Calculate ROD.
Ans a) 1500 FPM b) 1600 FPM c) 1700 FPM
Answers

Relationship Between Density Temperature, Pressure And Relative Humidity :
Density a Temperature  a RH
Density a Pressure
> High temperature and high RH will adversely affect the density. Density will
Decrease. Low temperature and low RH will increase density.
> With increase in altitude, temp decreases which causes density to increase. At
the same time, with increase in altitude, pressure falls exponentially, which
causes density to decrease.

of fall of pressure is predominant, therefore with increase in altitude atmosphere
density reduces.

Altimeter Settings:
QFE -
The pressure observed at the airfield datum point. When set on an altimeter
the output is height above the aerodrome or runway threshold.
QNH -
The OFE reduced to mean sea level (MSL) pressure using the standard
atmosphere temperature lapse rate. (The pressure altimeter is calibrated to the
standard atmosphere, and so when ONH is set on the altimeter subscale the
instrument indicates the airfield elevation at the airfield datum point). When
141
===== [nav 0078]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
QNH is set on an altimeter, the indication is always called 'altitude.
Regional QNH. A regional QNH is the lowest value of QNH which it is
forecast (by the Meteorological Central Forecasting Office) will exist within a
given Altimeter Setting Region (ASR) for a specified period. ASRs are shown on
aeronautical charts.
1013 hPa -
1013 is the standard pressure setting used by aircraft flying above the
transition altitude and on which the flight level system is based. When 1013 is set
on an altimeter, the indication is called pressure altitude, or flight level.
QNE -
When 1013 is set on the subscale, the altitude shown on the altimeter when
the aircraft is on the ground is known as the QNE value.
QFF -
OFF is mean sea level pressure either measured or calculated from QFE
reduced to mean sea level using ambient temperature. It is reported by
meteorological observing stations and plotted as isobars on surface weather charts.
but is not used in aviation.
Transition Altitude: is the altitude at and below which vertical positioning is
controlled by reference to altitude.
The Transition Level: is the first flight level above the transition altitude.
The Transition Layer: is the layer between the transition altitude and the
transition level.
Types of Altitudes:
Altitude is vertical distance above some point or level used as a reference.
There are as many kinds of altitude as there are reference levels from which
altitude is measured, and each may be used for specific reasons.
• Indicated Altitude - The reading on the altimeter when it is set to the
current barometric pressure. The altitude read directly from the altimeter
(uncorrected) when it is set to the current altimeter setting (QNH settings).
• Absolute Altitude - The vertical distance of an airplane above the terrain.
or above ground level (AGL) (QFE settings or Radio Altimeter:)
• Pressure Altitude/Flight Levels/QNE Settings -The altitude indicated
when the altimeter setting window (barometric scale) is adjusted to 1013.2 hPa or
29.92ạor 760 mms. This is the altitude above the standard datum plane, which is
a theoretical plane where air pressure (corrected to 15°C) equals 1013.2 hPa. Hg.
Pressure altitude is used to compute density altitude, true altitude, true airspeed.
142
MEASUREMENT OF DR ELEMENTS PRESSURE
in the calculation of aircraft performance data and for providing separation
• True Altitude - The vertical distance of the airplane above sea level-the
actual altitude. It is often expressed as feet above mean sea level (MSL.). Airport,
terrain, and obstacle elevations on aeronautical charts are true altitudes. Exact
height above sea level is used when a pilot wants the most accurate calculation of
altitude-for example, when flying over mountainous regions or close to obstacles.

Density Altitude -This altitude is pressure altitude corrected for variations
from standard temperature. It is pressure altitude corrected for non standard
ISA temp. When conditions are standard, pressure altitude and density altitude
are the same. Higher the prevailing temp, more is the density altitude and vice -
versa. This is an important altitude because it is directly related to the airplane's
performance, used primarily to predict aircraft performance during takeoffs.
Aircraft performance graphs in the Pilot Operating Handbook usually provide a
built-in calculation.
Density Altitude = PA +118 (Actual-ISA Temp. in deg C)

1: 60 RULE
The One to Sixty Rule has many practical applications in air navigation. The
proper use of this rule enables the pilot to accurately calculate various navigational
problems such as: Wind corrections, Time and Distance calculations and Slope
calculations.
60 NM
A -
The theory of the One to Sixty Rule is based on the fact that a ONE degree
are on the Equator equals approximately 60 Nautical Miles or in other words a
ONE minute arc equals approximately 1 NM.
This thumb rule is applicable only upto 20°, where we can take the base
and hypotenuse to be same (B = H). In tan 0, for values of 0 < 20" tan 0 can be
replaced by 0/60, ie, tan 0 = 0/60 = P/H or P/B.
0 = (PX60)  (PX60)
Or
It does not have any units.
153
===== [nav 0084]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Radian Measures: The ratio of any arc length to the radius, s/r, will be the
radian measure of the central angle which that arc subtends. Thus the radian
measure is based on ratios/numbers that are actually taken from the circle. Thus
the radian number is a real number that represents the ratio of a curved line to a
straight line. The ratio's 'to 'r' depends on a unique angle 0.
Radian measure of 0 = →
SLOPES (ILS Glide Path/AWR):
The one in 60 rule forms the basis of calculations in the vertical plane of
height against range for an aircraft on the ILS glidepath, and of clout height
determination using the airborne weather radar. These calculations are covered
Another practical application of the One to Sixty Rule is estimating the height
of a cloud by using the RADAR echoes return. The RADAR antenna is tilted up
and down to points where there is no return.
The distance of the cell is given by the RANGE ARCS and the Tilt Angle is
derived from the difference between the lowest and the highest echoes.

===== [nav 0087]
Chapter
11
MEASUREMENT/
DETERMINATION OF
TEMPERATURE
Temperature: Temperature is the measure of the intensity of heat in a body
The ambient air temperature is therefore a measure of the heat in the free air
at that time and is referred to as the static air temperature (SAT). An aircraft
traveling through the air compresses the air ahead of it and suffers the full
effect of that compression at the aircraft leading edges of the wing etc. where
the air is brought almost to a halt momentarily. The compression heats the air
adiabatically, also in and around the area of the temperature sensing probe. The
temperature sensed and indicated is therefore a falsely high temperature. The
increase in temperature due to this effect is known as 'ram-rise' and the indicated
temperature is referred to as the total air temperature (TAT). Depending on the
type of temperature probe used to determine the temperature, 'ram-rise' can
be calculated with a varying degree of accuracy and the TAT corrected by that
amount. This is called the 'recovery factor' or 'recovery coefficient.
TAT/SAT Relationship:
TAT (Total Air Temp) / Impact Air Temp / Dynamic Air Temp / Indicated
Temperature (IAT) or Ram Air Temperatures (RAT). It is composed of SAT
(Static Air Temp) / OAT (Outside Air Temp) / True Air Temperature / Ambient
Temp, plus the heating up by the ram effect of the fast streaming airflow, coming
to a sudden stop and consequently the kinetic energy being transferred into heat
TAT is used by the FMC (Flight Management Computer) in modern airliners
to calculate and display among other things the thrust and engine performance
on the cockpit's CRTs (Cathode Ray Tubes) in a glass cockpit.
160
MEASUREMENT/ DETERMINATION OF TEMPERATURE
A pilot, needs SAT or OAT, for instance to mention in Position Reports at long
distance flights. Calculations for exams can be done by using CX2 or formulas.
There exists a simple formula to convert TAT to SAT and to calculate Ram rise
in temperature :
TAT = SAT{1+(0.2 x R x M2)}
SAT: Static Air Temperature (in Kelvin) TAT: Total Air Temperature (in Kelvin)
R: Recovery coefficient/factor  M: Mach Number
To obtain Kelvin in Temp Absolute, (never say degrees Kelvin, but just Kelvin
or K for short),
We have to add 273 to any temp in Celsius.
For instance, flying at FL310 and having a reading ofTAT = - 9°C in the temp
window and a Mach number M = 0.86, recovery coefficient 1, we first convert to
K: - 9°C + 273 = 264 K. Then using the conversion-formula we'll find: SAT (or
OAT) = 264 / (1 + 0.2 x 0.86 ^ 2) = 230 K. Converting this back to Celsius we get
230 K - 273 = - 43°C.
In above example, the ISA temp at 31000 feet should be + 15- (31 x 2) = -
47°C. So the temperature of the air mass trough which we are currently flying at
FL310 is ISA - 4°C.

Determination of Appropriate Speed
> ASIR + correction for instrument error → IAS
> IAS + correction for position / pressure error → RAS(Rectified Air Speed).
CAS (Calibrated Air Speed)
> CAS + Correction for compressibility error → EAS (Equivalent air speed)
> EAS + Correction for density → TAS (True airspeed)
> Ground Speed: Applying head wind (-) / tail wind (+) component to TAS give
ground speed. TAS ‡ winds → G/S (ground speed)
DENSITY ERROR. TAS is directly proportional to temperature and pressure
altitude and inversely proportional to density.
Air density and ASI indications (with constant Ground Speed) are inversch
proportional to temperature and pressure altitude. Hence:
When Temperature or Pressure Altitude increase: TAS increases:
Density decreases: IAS/CAS decreases.
• When Temperature or Pressure Altitude decreases: TAS decreases:
Density increases: IAS/CAS increases.
RELATION SHIP BETWEEN TAS AND IAS: Thumb rule for approximate
values of TAS is:
1.75× RAS× Flight Level

Relationship between CAS, TAS, LSS and Mach Number:
A relationship also exists between CAS, TAS, LSS and Mach Number under
ISA. Isothermal and Inversion layer conditions.
Increase in Pressure altitude and Temperature decrease Density.
When Density decreases: IAS/CAS decreases. TAS increases.
Decrease in Pressure altitude and Temperature increase Density.
When Density increases: IAS/CAS increases. TAS decreases.
ISS increases and decreases with Temperature.

MEASUREMENT/ DETERMINATION OF TEMPERATURE
The following graphs depict what will occur if one value remains constant.
How to draw the graph:
Draw altitude and airspeed on vertical and horizontal axis respectively.
Draw the line representing constant speed at right angle to speed. Draw lines
representing other two speeds to complete the following sequence left to right.
Climbing in ISA or Isothermal layers: CTM (Coffee, Tea or Me)
Descending in ISA or Isothermal layers: MTC
Climbing in Inversion layer: CMT
Descending in Inversion layer: TMC
ISA CONDITIONS

value remains constant:

===== [nav 0092]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
AIRSPEED  AIRSPEED
Even if the CAS or MN is unaltered on purpose the MN may change due
to a change in the OAT, eg. high temperature to low temperature, the LSS will
decrease, thus to maintain a constant MN the TAS must decrease. For example
if two aero planes are traveling at the same MN, but at different flight levels the
aero plane at the lower flight level will have a higher TAS.
ISOTHERMAL LAYER
If an aero plane climbs or descends through an Isothermal Layer the graphs
depict what will happen to the other airspeeds, if one of them remains constant
At a constant Mach number the LSS will remain constant so the TAS will also
remain constant, although the CAS will reduce during a climb, but will increase
during a descent, due to the density error.
Conversely at a constant CAS the TAS and Mach number will both increase
during a climb, but will both reduce during a descent?
170
MEASUREMENT/ DETERMINATION OF TEMPERATURE
INVERSION LAYER
If an aero plane climbs or descends through an Inversion Layer the graphs
below depict what will happen to the other airspeeds, if one of them remains
constant.
AIRSPEED  AIRSPEED  AIRSPEED
During a climb at a constant Mach number the LSS will increase due to the
warmer air so the TAS will increase, but the CAS will reduce due to the reduction
in density. If the aero plane climbs at a constant TAS both the Mach number and
the CAS will reduce. Conversely if the aero plane climbs at a constant CAS the
Mach number and the TAS will both increase?
During a descent the reverse will occur, because the LSS will decrease due to
the colder air, whilst the density will increase.

Chapter

MEASUREMENT OF ELEMENTS
PNR/ PSR/ R of A (Point Of No Return/ Point Of Safe Return/
Radius of Action):
It is the maximum distance upto which an a/c can fly out and still be able to
return to place of departure with in safe endurance of a/c. It is calculated for the
endurance.
IT IS PURELY DEPENDENT ON FUEL IN TERMS OF ENDURANCE.
R of A
The distance traveled from the departure aerodrome to the limit of the
radius of action and back to the departure point are the same. The endurance
is calculated in hours. First subtract the reserve fuel from the total fuel available
on departure then divide the remainder by the fuel flow. The total of the time
outbound and the time inbound must not exceed the calculated endurance.
The formula can be derived from above as:
Time out + Time home = Safe Endurance
R of A  R of A  E
GH
MEASUREMENT OF ELEMENTS
Multiply by GO and GH : [(R of A) x GH] + [(R of A) x GO] = Ex GO x GH
Isolate R of A: R of A x [GO + GH] = E x GO x GH
E x GO × GH
RofA =  NMs
GO+GH
E  endurance in hours in kts.
Where
GO  groundspeed outbound in kts.
GH =  groundspeed inbound (home)
To find the time to the R of A divide the distance by the groundspeed outbound
Fuel on Board - Reserve  FF
Safe Endurance = Fuel Consumption per hour  OR E = -  FC
E-Endurance in hours and minutes, FF- Flight Fuel, FC-Fuel Consumption
per hour: DPNR is always calculated with all engines operating unless specified.
E × GO × GH NMs
DPNR =  GO+ GH
For Tag calculations always use G (Ground Speed out all engines or normal (N)
TAS.)
Ex GH
TENE (Time to PNR) =  = GO+GH
The above formula will not work if there is a change in TAS or Fuel
Consumption/Flow. In such a case GFC formula is used. Alternately unitary
method may be used.
Gross Fuel Consumption (GFC) Formula: The Gross Fuel Consumption
(GFC) formula calculates the fuel used (in units) for each ground nautical mile
traveled.
If the fuel used for each nautical mile traveled out to the R of A is added to
the fuel used for each nautical mile traveled back from the R of A then the result
is the fuel used per nautical mile of radius of action along the track considered.
GFC (Units/GNM) = FUEL CONSUMPTION (Units/HR)  FC
GROUND SPEED (KNTS)  GS
DINR = GFC  - = NM

Unitary Method: In unitary method. DPR Is presumed to be at a pomt alon
the route and FF is calculated for that point. Subsequently, the PNR value,
derived at by proportionately adjusting the distance as per fuel available.
A Remember:
If flight fuel changes by certain percentage of dist to PNR, PNR
changes by the same percentage.
• Distance to PNR is maximum in nil wind condition, any type of wind
will cause DAR to reduce.
• If fuel consumption changes by certain percentage, DPAR will change be
same percentage
Critical Point (CP) or Equitime Point (ETP) or Point Of Equal Time (PET):
CP/PET = It is a point en route from which it takes equal time to either go
to destination or come back to place of departure/or a designated point. IT IS
NOT EQUIDISTANT POINT:
DCP is calculated primarily for the emergency of engine failure when a/c has
to fly on reduced TAS.

1 Remember:
Dist to CP is half way when O = H, that will be in following conditions
(a) Nil winds  (b) Beam winds
B
In case of head wind, De, will be more than half way, in case of tail
wind it will be less.
In case head wind component increases, distance to CP will increase
or it will move towards destination or it will move away from place of
departure.
If tail wind increases distance to CP will decrease or it will move
towards place of departure or will move away from destination.
177
===== [nav 0096]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Der always moves into the wind.
For same head winds if TAS reduces, Dist to CP will increase.
If beam wind component doubles, Dist to CP will be half way (or
remain same). Time to CP will increase.
• If head wind component changes to tail wind component or vice versa
Dist to CP also changes by same distance from half way as long as
component remains same.
If FOB/ FF / FC changes De, remains same.

Chapter
13
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
PRE-FLIGHT PLANNING
It is absolutely necessary to reduce the time spent on navigation in the ai
to a minimum. In this respect, good flight planning contributes to the success of
any flight.
Flight planning should be carried out on a basis that requires the pilot to
establish a position at the following intervals:
Immediately after setting heading to provide a definite departure point
and to establish a departure time on which to base ETA.
• At regular points along track to check the progress of the flight so that
corrections for track error or time may by made.
• At a final point close to the destination so that final corrections may be
With chart preparation, there are only a few absolute rules:
Time/Distance Markers - The track line can either be calibrated in units of
flight time or distance. If flight time is used, it can either be time elapsed or time
to destination. Similarly, distance can be distance flown or distance to go. The
choice between the two methods is a matter of personal opinion, but an advantage
of the distance method is that it facilitates application of the 1 in 60 rule.
Track Error Lines - Lines drawn at angles of 5i or 10°, either side of track
through departure point and destination, are most useful for quick estimation of
track error and for estimating heading alterations.
198
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
Folding Charts - The chart should be folded so that complete track coverage
is possible with the minimum number of page turns and without re-folding in
flight. Charts should be numbered and arranged in order of use. It is also a good
idea to have an emergency set of charts in an easily accessible spot to relieve any
situation that might arise.
FLIGHT PLANNING SEQUENCE
\ logical sequence is as follows:
- Review all information relevant to the flight, e. g. flight rules, navigation
warnings, etc.
- Study the meteorological situation, and obtain wind velocities and temperatures
required for planning.
- Select a flight planning chart and, if different, a set of charts for the route.
- Determine the route to be followed; consider the aim of the flight, flight rules,
the meteorological situation, the availability of navigation aids, and any other
factors involved.
- Draw in tracks, measure track angles and distance, and record them in the
flight log.
> Determine safe altitudes, and decide on flight altitude or flight level, as
applicable.
> From knowledge of aircraft performance, determine RAS for each flight stage.
Enter RAS in log, and in conjunction with altitude and temperature, calculate
TAS.
> Calculate headings to steer for each flight stage, and log them.
> Complete the log by the calculation of groundspeeds and fuels.
> Carry out a mental re-appraisal of the whole plan to check for obvious errors.
> Prepare the flight charts.
> Note positions of alternate airfields, and determine flight planning data from
destination to alternates.
Use of visual observations and application to in-flight navigation: Visual
navigation is a technique often employed in light aircraft, which operate at
relatively low speeds and heights, when weather is good and visual contact can be
maintained with the ground for most of the flight.
Map Reading. Aeronautical series Topographical Maps will show features
such as rivers, roads, railways and terrain. They will also contain information
199
===== [nav 0107]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
of special interest to pilots including the position of airfields, radio beacons and
airways. Remember; the larger the scale, the greater the detail available on the
chart.
The choice of map for any particular flight will depend on many factors,
including the length of the journey; the cruising height and speed; and other
available means of navigation. Bad weather can also considerably affect the choice:
the worse the weather, the larger the scale of map that should be used. The choice
is then determined by the properties of the maps available for the area to be flown
over.
Visual Check Points. A visual check point is a significant feature or landmark
on or near the aircraft's planned track. It must be large enough to be visible
but small enough to pinpoint the aircraft's position. It should stand out from is
surroundings and therefore should be unique. It is not always possible to find
such a feature, but combinations of features can provide useful alternatives.
General Features of a Visual Checkpoint. Coastlines can usually provide
some position information, but an inlet on the coast will be of more use than a
long, straight, featureless, sandy beach. It may be possible to use the beach as a
line feature for checking track or groundspeed, but it will be difficult to identify
exactly one's position on a beach without reference to another feature. An inlet.
a promontory, a pier, a small wooded area or some cliffs are the sort of features
one would be looking for to pinpoint one's position along a coast. When crossing
a coastline, checking the direction in which it lies with reference to the compass.
combined with a check feature should dispel any ambiguity in position.
Water features such as rivers, estuaries, canals and lakes can be invaluable
visual checkpoints. But beware: the changing seasons can drastically alter the
appearance of such features. A river in flood will be almost unrecognizable from
the information presented on a map, whilst drought conditions could cause a lake
to disappear completely. In winter, a frozen lake may well be indistinguishable
from its surroundings if it is covered in snow.
Mountains and hills will often provide a valuable source of check points:
however, it should be remembered that the higher one flies, the flatter the ground
beneath will appear. If the ground is obscured by low cloud or fog, the tops of hills
may still be visible and therefore usable as check points. It is also possible for the
tops of hills or mountains to be hidden in cloud, not only making identification
difficult, but also a potential flight safety hazard to the unwary.
When flying at low levels ground contours become more significant because
even quite small hills and hillocks become more obvious with the better perspective
offered at that height.
200
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
Villages, being quite small, can make good check points, but when flying
over an area where many villages are scattered it is important to cross-refer with
other features.
Towns, being that much larger then villages can often be identified simply
from their shape, but it is a wise pilot that will cross-check with other features such
as the roads and railways associated with a town.
On the other hand a city can usually be identified from its sheer size, but
being a large feature, it also has its drawbacks. It will not be possible to fix one's
position accurately from such a large feature and once again, it will only be by
comparing smaller nearby features that one's position will be established precisely.
Railways are prominent features easily visible from the air, but identifying a
particular stretch of railway (as with any 'line' feature) may not be possible without
reference to other features such as bridges, level crossings etc. Where a railway
is useful (and other line features for that matter) is when it is parallel or nearly
parallel to the desired track, then it will provide useful tracking information.
Alternatively, if the feature crossed the desired track at right angles, then timing
and groundspeed information can be derived.
Roads can also be an invaluable aid to tracking and/or timing as well as being
a source of check point; however, a number of points should be borne in mind:
a) Minor roads are more easily recognizable in sparsely populated areas; in towns
there will be too many roads to be able to identify any particular one.
b) Roads can disappear from view, hidden by terrain or woodlands or urban
complexes.
c) Motorways, like railways are easily distinguishable from the air, but a long
straight stretch of motorway presents the same problem as a stretch of railway:
it is impossible to pinpoint position without reference to other features. A
motorway junction or service area, however, is much more useful.
DR Positions and Actual Positions. The easiest way to navigate visually is by
selecting ground features which are marked on the map and looking for them
on the ground. If the weather is fine, this is not difficult provided that unique,
prominent ground features have been chosen. But if there is cloud below or the
visibility is poor it may be difficult to find the selected features.
In bad weather, the best solution is to navigate from ground to map. Look
for prominent features on the ground through holes in the cloud, and then
identify them on the map. The flight plan timings can be used to help in this, but
care should be taken because, depending on the map scale, certain features that
are easy to see from the air may not be marked on the map. For really important
201
===== [nav 0108]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
check points, (e.g. when approaching an airway) a line feature, such as a railwa
line or coastline, is usually better than an isolated point, because it is quick an
easy to see.
Plotting Visually Derived Intersecting Lines of Position. Pinpointing the
aircraft's position is achieved by estimating the range and bearing from a ched
point or by combining bearings from two different check points and referring
that position to the map. Adjustments can then be made to heading and speed in
order to maintain the desired track and timing.
Single observed position line to check flight progress. Study the route to
gain a mental picture of what to expect along the way and highlight features along
the track which will be suitable to use as visual check points. The chosen feature
should be 5-10 minutes flying time apart and, if possible, spaced equally ove
the leg distance. (Allow plenty of time for route study; initially, you can exper
to spend about the same length of time on route study as the flight is planned to
take.)
Prepare and Align a map /chart for use in Visual Navigation.
(a) A few minutes before flying over a pre-planned check point, establish
the aircraft's approximate position, ie calculate a DR position.
(b) Determine the position of the check point relative to the DR position
and decide where the feature should be relative to the view from the
cockpit.
(c) Relate the map information to what can be seen from the cockpit. Look
for the chosen feature in the predicted position. If the feature is exactly
where you expect it to be, then the aircraft is on track and on time.
If, however, the feature is not immediately visible, then the aircraft is
either off track, or not on time, or a combination of the two. (Bear in
mind that it is possible that the feature may be obscured from view and
could become visible later). It is then necessary to widen the scan of the
area around the aircraft. Bear in mind that the aircraft's DR position
is constantly changing and, therefore, the chosen feature's relative
position will be also changing.
(d) When the check feature is sighted, estimate the range and bearing and
note the time. Return to the map and using the observations, establish
an accurate position of the aircraft.
(e) Having fixed the position of the aircraft, take action to keep or return
to track and adjust speed to achieve the required timing
Angle of Observation. The altitude at which we fly will effect how features
appear. At low levels, masts and aerials can provide very good check point, because
202
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
of their vertical extent, but the higher one flies the larger the mast needs to be
to be clearly visible. Generally speaking, nearer objects will be identifiable from
their shape in plan form, while features at greater distances will be more easily
identified from their elevation. Hence towns, cities and lakes are useful close to
track whereas masts, bridges, church steeples and high ground will be easier to
identify when some distance away.
Size of Feature. Large features are easily distinguished and identified as we
have noted with cities, but an accurate position is more likely to be obtained from
a small or narrow feature.
Uniqueness of the Feature. Avoid choosing common or ambiguous features;
choose features with characteristics which make them unique in their vicinity. For
example, we have noted that aerials and masts stand out quite will at a distance
because of their vertical extent, but beware of aerial farms'. Similarly, an isolated
dump of trees may provide a useful check point, but a clump of trees in a wooded
area will be virtually impossible to distinguish and therefore useless. Be sure to
use uniqueness in your criteria for choosing a check point.
Contrast and Colour. Variations in contrast, colour, light, the position of the
sun, the season of the year and the amount of precipitation etc will all have an
effect on the appearance of the features to be found. Recognizing and identifying
features will be a lot easier if sufficient thought and imagination is applied to these
effects.
Navigation in climb descent. Climbs and descents are usually calculated
using DR principles, since it is not normal to fix the aircraft's position during
changes of altitude.
Assuming that an aircraft is required to climb at a constant CAS from one
level to another, then it is necessary to calculate the mean TAS at a point half way
up the climb. Next determine a mean wind, either by interpolation or inspection,
and again the W/V half way up the climb is used.
Time Th W/V |  Hde Var  Hag  / Dev  Hdg | Observations RAS  Press  Temp  TAS  Gnd  Dist  Time ETA
(M)  (0)  Alt  Spd

Navigation in Cruising Flight, Use of Fixes to Revise Navigation Data. The
centerline tracks of the airways are given in degrees magnetic. Route forecast
wind directions are given in degrees true. It is therefore necessary to convert
everything to a common north reference, and for flights along airways it is more
convenient to convent the wind direction to degrees magnetic.
203
===== [nav 0109]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
A fix is defined as the exact position of an aircraft at a specified time. It
may be derived from visual, radio or radar observations or any combination of
these navigational aids. The observations from different sources any be made
simultaneously or at different times and transferred to a common time to construct
a fix.
The most accurate fix is that derived from the aids least likely to be in error
and intersecting at a large angle i.e. 60° or 90". Therefore the most accurate fix
is obtained from a twin DME fix because the DME has the least error and the
position lines can be chosen to cut at between 60 and 90". If this is not possible, or
the Airborne Search Radar cannot be used to obtain a similar fix, the aids should
be selected to produce three position lines intersecting at 120ạbecause this will
tend to criminate any errors inherent in the equipment.
Beacon B  Beacon A
Beacon C -
The plotted position lines do not intersect at one position but form a 'cocked
hat'. However the centre of the cocked hat is at the same position as the corred
fix should have been.
NDB/ADF: The traditional way of presenting ADF bearing information to
the pilot is by means of the Relative Bearing Indicator (RBI). The sharp end of
the needle points to the NDB, and the bearing displayed on the RBI is relative
to the forward end of the aircraft fore and aft axis. In order to obtain a QDM it
is therefore necessary to add the aircraft heading ("M) to the relative bearing. In
order to obtain a QDR it is then necessary to add or subtract 180ạ to the QDM.
Finally, in order to obtain a QTE (bearing to plot), it is necessary to apply the
variation at the aircraft.
The more convenient method of presenting ADF bearing information is by
means of the Radio Magnetic Indicator (RMI). Again the sharp end of the needle
points to the NDB and the top of the instrument represents the forward end of
the fore and aft axis. The compass rose within the RMI is slaved to a suitable
204
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
gro magnetic compass, and therefore the aircrati's magnetic heading is shown
against the datam at the top of the instrument. The sharp end of the needle
is thus pointing at the QDM on the compass rose. In effect the RMI has done
mechanically what the pilot is required to do mentally (i.e. relative + magnetic
heading = QDM) when using the RBI.
VOR: VOR bearings may be presented to the pilot on either an RM1 (radio
magnetic indicator) or an OBI (omni-bearing indicator).
An OBI bearing may be given as either QDM or QDR (depending on whether
the TO or the FROM flag is showing).
Remember that, with a VOR, the variation at the station is used (rather then
the variation at the aircraft, as with an NDB). Since this is the case, the variation
to be applied will remain constant for cash VOR regardless of the position of the
aircraft. The bearing must be converted to become a true bearing which is plotted
against true north at the beacon.
To achieve a more accurate plot of the position line, draw the local meridian
(true north) passing through the VOR station position, and plot the position line
as a QTE (true bearing) from this local meridian.
DME: To plot a DME range position line, are a curve of the appropriate
radius from the DME station, as shown in the following example. It is not normal
to account for the difference between indicated (slant) range and true (horizontal)
range. Remember that a TACAN is effectively a military DME, and that a VORTAC
is effectively a co-located VOR and DME.
When discussing visual check point, it was noted that line features can be
used to check tracking when aligned with the track and to check timing when
aligned across track. Similarly, a single position line from a feature can be used
for the same purposes. Tracking information can be obtained from a feature on
or close to track, whilst timing information can be obtained when passing abeam
a feature.
An aircraft is tracking eastwards with a groundspeed of 240 kt. The following
bearings are obtained from the NDB
0826  NDB bears 040° RMI
0828  NDB bears 349° RMI
0830  NDB bears 300° RMI
Determine the aircraft position at 0830.
Since all of the bearings in this example are from one station this non-
smultaneous fix is known as a running fix. Invariably the time used for any
manufactured fix is the time of the LAST position line.

DISTANCE AND TIME:
(a) Flying a heading 90 deg to the Navigation Aid: The formalase for
determining time and distance to a station are also derived from the One to Sixty
Rule. The distance to a station can be calculated by flying an aircraft perpendicular
to a given bearing (or radial) and by noting the clapsed time between bearings. For
reasons of convenience, increments of 10 degrees changes in bearings are desired.
The One to Sixty proportion is the basis for obtaining the desired formula.
TAS × minutes flown
Distance to station =
Degrees between bearings
60 × minutes flown between bearing change
Time to station =
Degrees between bearings
To determine the time/distance to a station these steps are to be followed.
After tuning and identifying the VOR station:
Determine the radial on which you are located.
Turn inbound and re-center the needle if necessary.
o  Turn 80° right, or left, of the inbound course, rotating the OBS to the
nearest 10° increment opposite the direction of turn.
Maintain heading. When the CDI centers, note the time.
Maintaining the same heading, rotate the OBS 10° in the same
direction as in step 3, above.
o  Note the elapsed time when the CDI again centers.
o  Use the formulas shown earlier to determine the Time/distance from
the station.
210
IN-FLIGHT NAVIGATION
A similar procedure can be used to calculate Time/distance from a NDB
station.
(b) Flying a constant heading: At 60 NM from a station, you travel 1 NM for
every radial (1 degree) that you cross. If you cross 3 radials, you have traveled 3
VM. For this to work, you must maintain a constant heading
> Problems that use the 60:1 rule will supply
The number of radials crossed, and
The distance traveled, or

used to calculate the distance traveled).
> The heading flown may be given, but is not important (except that a constant
heading is maintained).

Enter revised navigational en-route data, for the legs concerned, into the
flight log. (e.g. updated wind and ground speed and correspondingly losses or
gains in time and fuel consumption).

flight log.
ROC (Rate of Climb) can be calculated using the following relationship:
ROC = TAS×Sin(Flight Path Angle).
The rate of descent can be calculated using the following formula:
ROD = TAS×sin(Glide/Flight Path Angle).
TAS = True Airspeed (in feet per second) .

Chapter
14
MASS AND BALANCE -
AERO PLANES
Introduction to Mass and Balance
Good airmanship requires that the loading, mass and center of gravity of the
aircraft all comply with the limitations specified in the approved Aircraft Flight
Manual.
It's the responsibility of the commander of the aircraft to satisfy himself that
this requirement is met.
Definitions:
Balance Arm (BA) is the distance from the datum to the center of gravity of
a mass.
Center of Gravity (C of G). The C of G is defined as the point through which
the force of gravity acts upon a mass, to give it its weight. The C of G is also the
point of balance of the aircraft and will, thus, affect its stability. Consult aeroplane
flight manual for take off, landing and cruise configuration.
Datum (relative to an aero plane) is that point on the longitudinal axis (on
extension thereof) from which the centers of gravities of all masses are referenced
Dry Operating Index (DOI) is the index for the position of the center of
gravity at Dry Operating Mass.
Loading Index (LI) is a non-dimensional figure that is a scaled down value
of a moment. It is used to simplify mass and balance calculations.
Ballast: Ballast is removable or permanently installed weight in an aircrafi
218
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
used to bring the center of gravity into the allowable range.
Center of Gravity Limits. Center-of-gravity limits, means the most forward
and most rearward center of Gravity position within which an aircraft may be
operated safely. CG limits are specified longitudinal (forward and aft) and/or
lateral (left and right) limits within which the aircraf's center of gravity must be
located during flight. The CG limits are indicated in the airplane flight manual.
The area between the limits is called the CG range of the aircraft.
Center of Gravity Datum. is a point from which all measurements are taken
in the calcalation of the center of Gravity.
The reference datum is a reference plane that allows accurate, and uniform,
measurements to any point on the aircraft. The location of the reference datum is
established by the manufacturer and is defined in the aircraft flight manual. The
horizontal reference datum is an imaginary vertical plane or point, arbitrarily
fixed somewhere along the longitudinal axis of the aircraft, from which all
horizontal distances are measured for weight and balance purposes. There is no
fixed rule for its location, and it may be located forward of the nose of the aircraft.
For helicopters, it may be located at the rotor mast, the nose of the helicopter, or
even at a point in space ahead of the helicopter. While the horizontal reference
datum can be anywhere the manufacturer chooses, most small training helicopters
have the horizontal reference datum 100 inches forward of the main rotor shaft
centerline. This is to keep all the computed values positive. The lateral reference
datum is usually located at the center of the helicopter.
Arm: The arm is the chord wise (fore-and-aft) distance from the datum to
any point within the aircraft.
Moment: Moment is the product of the mass and the balance arm. The
moment is a measure of force that results from an object's weight acting through
an are that is centered on the zero point of the reference datum distance. Moment
is also referred to as the tendency of an object to rotate or pivot about a point (the
zero point of the datum, in this case). The further an object is from this point, the
greater the force it exerts. Moment is calculated by multiplying the weight of an
object by its arm.
Mean Aerodynamic Chord (MAC): A specific chord line of a tapered wing. At
the mean aerodynamic chord, the center of pressure has the same aerodynamic
force, position, and area as it does on the rest of the wing. The MAC represents the
width of an equivalent rectangular wing in given conditions. On some aircraft, the
center of gravity is expressed as a percentage of the length of the MAC. In order
to make such a calculation, the position of the leading edge of the MAC must be
known ahead of time. This position is defined as a distance from the reference
219
===== [nav 0117]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
datum and is found in the aircraft's flight manual and also on the aircraft's type
certificate data sheet. If a general MAC is not given but a LeMAC (leading edge
mean aerodynamic chord) and a TeMAC (trailing edge mean aerodynamic chordy
are given (both of which would be referenced as an arm measured out from the
datum line) then your MAC can be found by finding the difference between your
LeMAC and your TeMAC.
It is important for longitudinal stability that the CG be located ahead of the
center of lift of a wing. Since the center of lift is expressed as a percentage of the
MAC, the location of the CG is expressed in the same terms.

Changes in Cg Location During Flight Due To Consumption Of Fuel:
It would now be wise to determine if the use of fuel during the flight will
move the center of gravity to a point outside the C of G envelope.
'Mass' is the amount of matter in a body.
'Weight' is the force produced when mass is subject to gravity.
A body will always have mass, but if there is no gravity then it has no weight.
'Mass' and 'Weight' are interchangeable in the text in this lesson.
Empty Weight: Means the measured or computed weight of an aircraft,
excluding the weight of all removable equipment and other items of disposable
load, but including the weight of all items of fixed operating equipment or other
221
===== [nav 0118]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
equipment which are mandatory for all operations like fixed ballast, engine
coolant, hydraulic fluid and fuel and oil quantities (both trapped and unusable)
in the aircraft and engine system.
Maximum Take-off Weight: Means the maximum weight, according to its
Certificate of Airworthiness or Flight Manual, at which an aircraft is permitted to
take-off.
Removable Equipment: Means items of equipment which are carried on
some of or on all flights, but which are not included in Empty weight and which
are not mandatory for the type of operation being conducted.
Weight and Balance: When the weight of the aircraft is at or below the
allowable limit(s) for its configuration (parked, ground movement, take-off,
landing, etc.) and its center of gravity is within the allowable range, and both wil!
remain so for the duration of the flight, the aircraft is said to be within weight and
balance. Different maximum weights may be defined for different situations; for
example, large aircraft may have maximum landing weights that are lower than
maximum take-off weights (because some weight is expected to be lost as fuel is
burned during the flight). The center-of-gravity may change over the duration of
the flight as the aircraft's weight changes due to fuel burn.
INITIAL WEIGHMENT:
Weighing of Aircraft. On every occasion of weighing, a 'weighing schedule
is compiled.
The Basic Empty Mass and center of Gravity of a new aircraft are determined
by the manufacturer.
Every aircraft shall be weighed before the issue of Certificate of Airworthiness.
In case a new aircraft is imported from outside the country, weight schedules
issued by the manufacturer or the previous operator weight schedule based on the
manufacturer's certificated weight and balance documents would be acceptable.
Requirements for Reweighing Aircraft of AUW more than 2000 Kg. Aircrati
having AUW more than 2000 kg. shall be re-weighed every five years unless it is
required to be re-weighed after major repairs/alterations.
Requirements for Reweighing of an Aircraft of all up weight (AUW) less
than 2000 Kg. Aircraft weighing less than 2000 kgs. need not be reweighed on
routine basis, unless it is required to be reweighed after major repairs/alterations.
REQUIREMENTS FOR REWEIGHING AFTER MAJOR REPAIR/ALTERATIONS:
An aircraft shall be required to be reweighed if it has undergone major repait.
or major alteration or there has been major change in the interior arrangement
222
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
of pilot/pax/cargo compartments which affect already determined weight and
balance data and which cannot be accurately computed without fresh weigh
ment. Decision of the DGCA whether the aircraft requires reweighing after major
repair/ alteration, or change in interior arrangement, shall be final.
Director General of Civil Aviation may require the re-weigh ment of an
aircraft at any time if he considers it necessary.
FORM OF WEIGHT SCHEDULE:
After the aircraft has been weighed as required, an appropriately licensed
IME in Category &B or a person approved for the purpose shall prepare a
Weight Schedule.
APPROVAL BY AIRWORTHINESS OFFICE:
The weight schedule after being prepared and duly signed shall be submitted
to Regional Airworthiness Office who would approve the same.
DISPLAY IN THE AIRCRAFT AND PRESERVATION OF RECORD:
A copy of the approved weight schedule must be displayed in the aircraft at
a suitable and prominent place. All persons concerned directly with the loading
of the aircraft shall be duly informed of the various weight figures for exercising
proper weight and balance control of the aircraft. A copy of the weight schedule
must be retained along with other documents of the aircraft or in the aircraft
logbook, till it is replaced by a fresh schedule.
COMPUTATION OF center of GRAVITY:
For all flights, it shall be the responsibility of the Pilot-in-Command to
ensure that the aircraft is satisfactorily loaded with respect to the total load, the
distribution of the load and proper securing of the load in aircraft (lashing of the
load). The distribution of the load shall be such that the C.G. position will remain
within the specified limits at the time of take off, during the progress of the flight
and at the time of landing.
In the case of scheduled operator, the responsibility for loading, lashing and
computing C.G. position, for take-off and landing phases of flight as stated in the
previous paragraph may be delegated to a person nominated by the operator, who
is specifically trained and authorized (by the operator) for the purpose. However,
renter of Gravity position computed by the nominated person shall be signed and
dated by him and the same shall be submitted to the Pilot-in-Command of the
aircraft for his scrutiny and acceptance; the acceptance would be denoted by the
pilot by affixing the dated signature.
In case a method other than the "direct calculating method" for the purpose
of computing C.G. is employed, the same shall be submitted to the Regional
223
===== [nav 0119]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Airworthiness Office for approval before adoption.
Every operator including scheduled, non-scheduled, State Government and
private aircraft operator shall prepare load and trim sheet for aircraft where the
manufacturer has provided necessary documentation for the purpose. The load
and trim sheet shall indicate the composition and the distribution of the total load
carried on board the aircraft as well as the calculated C.G. position for "take-of
and landing" configurations before the commencement of the flight. Such load
sheets shall be prepared and signed by the Pilot-in- Command or persons duk
trained in accordance with CAR Section 2 Series 'F Part XXI and responsible for
supervising the loading of aircraft. In case the load and trim sheet is prepared
by a person other than the Pilot-in-Command, the same shall be submitted to the
Pilot for his scrutiny and signatures before the commencement of the flight. One
copy of the load sheet shall be carried on board the aircraft and one copy shall be
retained by the operator for record purposes for a period of at least four months
from the date of issue.
STANDARD WEIGHT OF FLIGHT CREW/ PASSENGERS:
For preparation of load sheet and calculation of center of Gravity, the
minimum standard weight (including handbag) as given below, shall be applied
innut registered aircraft:
1. Crew 85 (75 + 10) kgs.
2. Adult passenger (both Male & Female) 75 kgs.
3. Child (Between 2 years and 12 years age) 35 kgs.
4. Infant (Less than.two years) 10 kgs.
Note: For training flights the actual weight of the crew may be taken for computation of
Load.
CALIBRATION OF WEIGHING SCALES:
The weighing scales used for the purpose of weightment of passenger
baggage, goods etc. shall be calibrated at specified intervals to the satisfaction of
the QCM/ DGCA.
INSTRUCTIONS FOR SAFE LOADING:
Specific seats shall be allotted to all passengers boarding at originating
stations of flights so that center of gravity of the aircraft can be
calculated accurately and the C.G. is kept within the permissible limits.
> During loading, it must be ensured that aircraft cabin floor loading
limitations are not exceeded.
224
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
The load must be securely tied so that there is no possibility of the load
shifting in flight and disturbing the calculated C.G. position.
- The load must be tied at the specified places provided in the aircraft
and the tying ropes must be of sufficient strength to withstand the
loads imposed on it in flight.
While placing cargo in the passenger cabin during mixed version
(passenger cum freight) operation, the load must be placed ahead of
the passengers in the cabin, the load must not block "emergency exit"
meant to be used by the passengers during" emergencies".
OBSERVANCE OF SAFETY INSTRUCTIONS:
The safety instructions required to be observed, as above, shall be observed
by Pilot-in-Command of non-scheduled operators, aerial work aircraft operators
and private aircraft operators.
In the case of scheduled operators, a comprehensive manual (weight and
Balance) shall be prepared which may detail the safety requirements for compliance

The Center of Gravity (CG) :
• The point that the total weight of the aircraft is said to act through
• The point of balance
• The point of the aircraft that follows the flight path
• The point that the aircraft maneuvers about in the air
• The point that the three axis of the aircraft pass through.
The position of the CG determines how stable or how maneuverable the
aircraft will be. Starting at the mid position of the fuselage, a CG moving towards
the nose of the aircraft will progressively increase the stability and, at the same
time, progressively reduce the maneuverability, similarly, a CG moving aft
forwards the tail of the aircraft will increase the maneuverability and decrease the
stability. Too much stability increases the flying control stick force and the work
load on the pilot trying to overcome them. Too much maneuverability makes the
aircraft unstable and difficult to control.
With regard to aero planes, the CG is not fixed. It moves in flight as a result
of fuel burn, flap positions, and crew and passenger movements. It is the aircraft
operator's responsibility to ensure that the CG movement is retained with the
226
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
timits imposed by the manafacturer.
The manufacturer sets down CG range of movement limits to ensure that
the average pilot is able to control the aircraft through all stages of flight without
undue concern or fatigue.
Factors Determining Cg Limits - Aircraft Stability: An airplane is designed
to have stability that allows it to be trimmed so it will maintain straight and level
flight with hands off the controls. Longitudinal stability is maintained by ensuring
the CG is slightly ahead of the center of lift.
This produces a fixed nose-down force independent of the airspeed. This
is balanced by a variable nose-up force, which is produced by a downward
aerodynamic force on the horizontal tail surfaces that varies directly with the
airspeed.
Lift
Variable
Fixed  nose-up force
nose-down force  dependent upon
independent of  airspeed
airspeed
Longitudinal forces acting on an airplane in flight
As long as the CG is maintained within the allowable limits for its weight, the
airplane will have adequate longitudinal stability and control.
> If the CG is too far aft,
It will be too near the center of lift and the airplane will be unstable,
and difficult to recover from a stall. If the unstable airplane should ever
enter a spin, the spin could become flat and recovery would be difficult
or impossible. Stalling speed will decrease.
If the aircraft is grossly tail heavy, the center of gravity will move well
behind the aft center of gravity limit.
If the center of gravity is outside the aft limit, large balancing upload
elevator deflections are required.
This will lead to reduced longitudinal stability...and the possibility of
overstressing the aircraft by applying excessive 'g'
227
===== [nav 0121]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
Recovering from a spin may be more difficult.
Range and endurance will decrease.
• Glide angle will be more difficult to sustain.
> If the CG is too far forward,
The downward tail load will have to be increased to maintain level
flight. This increased tail load has the same effect as carrying additional
weight; the aircraft will have to fly at a higher angle of attack, and drag
will increase. Stalling speed will increase.
If the aircraft is grossly nose heavy, the center of gravity will move to well
in front of the forward center of gravity limit.
If the center of gravity is outside the forward limit, a large balancing
download is required from the tailplane, i.e. large elevator deflections.
The forward C of G will cause increased drag because of the required
large elevator deflections.
The increased lift from the wings to balance the high tail loads will cause
an increase in induced drag. Aircraft performance is reduced.
The aircraft will be slow to respond to pitch inputs.
Range and endurance are decreased.
• The fixed-wing airplane has movable controls that alter the lift on various
airfoil surfaces to vary pitch, roll, and yaw. These changes in lift, in tum,
change the characteristics of the flight parameters. Weight normally decreases
in flight due to fuel consumption, and the airplane center of gravity changes
with this weight reduction. An airplane utilizes its variable flight controls to
compensate and maintain controllability through the various flight modes and
as the center of gravity changes.
• An airplane has a center of gravity range or envelope within which it must
remain if the flight controls are to remain effective and the airplane safely
operated.
Neutrally Stable Aircraft.
A neutrally stable aircraft refers to an aircraft configuration where the inherent
restoring forces are minimal. If disturbed from its trimmed or equilibrium state.
the aircraft does not inherently return to its original position, nor does it continue
to move away from it. In other words, it lacks inherent stability and requires
constant pilot input to maintain its new state.
This typically occurs when the center of gravity (CG) of the aircraft is located
at or very near the aerodynamic center.
228
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
Mass And Balance
The mass and balance system commonly employed among aircraft consists
of three equally important elements: the weighing of the aircraft, the maintaining
of the weight and balance records, and the proper loading of the aircraft. An
accuracy in any one of these elements nullifies the purpose of the whole system.
Maximum Floor Load
Each cargo hold has a structural floor loading limit based on the weight of
the load and the area over which this weight is distributed. To determine the
maximum weight of a loaded cargo pallet that can be carried in a cargo hold.
divide its total weight, which includes the weight of the empty pallet and its tie
down devices, by its area in square feet. This load per square foot must be equal
to or less than the floor load limit.
Factors Determining Maximum Permissible Mass : Structural Limitations
An operator must comply with additional structural limits such as the floor
strength limitations, the maximum load per running meter, the maximum mass
per cargo compartment, and/or maximum seating limits.
Weight: has pronounced effects on the aircraft's performance, handing and
aerodynamic properties, with an increase in weight:
> Performance is reduced:
Take off'and landing distances will increase. VI decision speed, VR rotation
speed, V2 screen height, and the stopping distance will all increase
The climb gradient, rate of climb and ceiling height will all reduce.
The rate of descent will increase.
The stalling speed will increase and maximum speed will reduce.
The safety margins and the effective speed range between low and high
speed buffet will reduce.
Drag and fuel consumption will increase.
Range and endurance will reduce.
• Wing root stresses will increase. The structural design of the wing root
incorporates considerations of material strength, aerodynamic loads, torsion
and bending moments, engine and landing gear loads, and safety margins to
ensure the structural integrity.
> Maneuverability will reduce. The aircraft will become less responsive to control
inputs and more difficult to fly.
• Wing root stresses and undercarriage loads will increase as will tyre and brake
wear.
229
===== [nav 0122]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
LOADING:
Mass Definitions:
Basic Empty Mass/ Basic Mass (BEM) is the mass of an aero plane plus
standard items such as: unusable fuel and other unusable fluids; lubricating oil in
engine and auxiliary units; fire extinguishers; pyrotechnics; emergency oxygen
equipment; supplementary electronic equipment.
Dry Operating Mass (DOM)/ Basic Operating Mass (BOM)/ Aircraft Prepared
for Service (APS) is the total mass of the aero plane ready for a specific type of
operation excluding usable fuel and traffic load. The mass includes items such
as:
i) Crew and crew baggage.
ii) Catering and removable passenger service equipment.
ili) Potable water and lavatory chemicals.
iv) Food and beverages.
Operating Mass (OM) is the DOM plus Take off fuel load but without traffic
load.
Take-Off Mass (TOM) is the mass of the aero plane including everything and
everyone contained within it at the start of the take-off run.
Taxi/Ramp Mass is the mass of the aero plane at the start of the taxi (at
departure from the loading gate). Sometimes referred to as Ramp Mass.
Maximum Taxi/Ramp Mass. Maximum weight approved for ground
maneuver. It includes weight of start, taxi, and run up fuel.
Traffic Load/ Pay Load. The total mass of passengers, baggage and cargo.
including any 'non-revenue' load.
Useful Load is the total mass of the passengers, baggage and cargo, including
any non-revenue load and usable fuel.
It is the difference between the Dry Operating Mass and the Take-Off Mass.
Useful Load = Traffic Load + Usable Fuel.
Zero Fuel Mass (ZFM) is DOM plus traffic load but excluding fuel.
Maximum Mass. The maximum authorized mass of the aircraft and all of its
equipment as specified in the Type Certificate.
Empty Mass: The manufacturer provides the aircraft operator with the
empty weight of the aircraft and the location of its empty weight center of gravity
(EWCG) at the time the certified aircraft leaves the factory.
230
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
Standard Mass: Crew, Passengers And Baggage, Fuel, Oil Water (Volume/
Mass Conversion Factors), Carry-On Luggage.
FOB: Fuel On Board. Total Fuel carried in tanks including Flight/Trip,
Reserve, Alternate, Contingency fuel etc.
Ramp/Block fuel: FOB + Taxi Fuel
FF: Flight Fuel/fuel burn/estimated Fuel consumption. Total Fuel consumed
during flight. FOB less Taxi, Reserve, Alternate, Contingency fuel etc.
FC: Fuel Consumption per hour
Structural Limitations These are the limits up to which the aircraft can be
loaded. Any load beyond this will be endangering the structure and hence is not
acceptable.
Maximum Structural Landing Mass (MSLM) the maximum permissible
total aero plane mass on landing in normal circumstances.
• Maximum Structural Take-Off Mass (MSTOM) the maximum
permissible total aero plane mass at the start of the take-off run. The
weight given by manufacturer which caters for all conditions at the
place of departure and arrival. This weight cannot be exceeded in any
circumstances. It is structural aerodynamic limit of a/c. Maximum weight
approved for the start of the takeoff run.
• Maximum Structural Taxi Mass is the structural limitation of the mass
of the aero plane at commencement of taxi.
• Maximum Zero Fuel Mass (MZFM) is the maximum permissible mass of
an aero plane with no usable fuel.
If the wing tanks are empty, there is maximum permissible weight of a/c
including all its contents i.e. basic weight and payload. Exceeding this weight
would cause unacceptable stress to the a/c structure. If any load is to be carried, it
should be in the form of fuel only. The maximum authorized weight of an aircraft
without fuel. This is the total weight for a particular flight less the fuel. It includes
the aircraft and everything that will be carried on the flight except the weight of
the fuel. The mass of the fuel contained in particular tanks may be included if
particularly mentioned in Flight Manual limitations.
Performance Limitations Such As - Runway Available For Take-Off And
Landing, Weather Conditions (Temperature, Pressure, Wind, Precipitation);
Rate-Of-Climb And Altitude Requirements For Obstacle Clearance; Engine-Out
Performance Requirements have effect on limiting the weight.
231
===== [nav 0123]
PART I - GENERAL NAVIGATION
• Performance Limited Landing Mass (PLLM) is the mass subject to the
landing aerodrome limitations or maximum weight approved for the
landing. This is take off weight Restriction due to prevailing conditions
at place of arrival.
> Performance Limited Take-Off Mass (PLTOM) is the take-off mas
subject to departure aerodrome limitations or take off mass/weigh
Restricted due to prevailing conditions at place of departure.
Regulated Landing Mass (RLM)/Maximum Landing Mass (MLM) is the
lowest of the 'performance limited' landing mass and 'structural limited' landing
mass.
Regulated Take-Off Mass (RTOM)/Maximum Take Off Mass (MTOM) is the
lowest of the 'performance limited' TOM and 'structural limited' TOM.
Note: MSTOM, MSLM and MZEM are published by the manufacturer. PLTOM and
PLLM are worked out by the pilot depending upon prevailing conditions.
Restricted Take Off Mass/Weight (RTOM/W) = FOB+MTL+DOM
Take off Fuel Load/Fuel On Board  Flight/Trip Fuel + Contingency Fuel + Alternate

TURBOJET FUEL CALCULATION:
Thrust (F): The required thrust of the engine in newtons or kilonewtons
(kN) or pounds (Ib).
Specific Fuel Consumption (SFC): The fuel consumption per unit of thrust.
usually given in kg/sperkN or Ib/hr per Ib of thrust.
Time of Operation (t): How long the engine operates at the given thrust.
232
MASS AND BALANCE - AERO PLANES
Flight Conditions: Altitude, air density, and temperature, which may affect
engine performance.
To calculate fuel requirements, the general formula is:
Fuel Consumption Rate = SFC × Thrust = SFC × F
Total Fuel Required = SFC × Thrust × Time of Operation = SFC × F × t
Maximum Traffic Load (MTL/Pay Load Calculations: In the examination you
will be required to calculate the mass/weight of the traffic load that can be carried, as
limited by one of three limiting maximum weights:
(a) Maximum Take-Off Mass/Weight.
(b) Maximum Landing Mass/Weight.
(c) Maximum Zero Fuel Mass/Weight.
The allowable (restricted) Take-off Mass is the lowest of:
- MZFM + Take-off Fuel
- Regulated Take-off Mass
• Regulated Landing Mass + Fuel used in flight
Step 1: Make the following table to find out Regulated Take off Mass/ Weight
(RTOM/W). It will be the least of the following calcalations :
TOM  RLM  MZFM
RTOM = Least of  RTOM = RLM + FF/Fuel Burn/  RTOM = MZFM
MSTOM/PLTOM or  Estimated fuel Consumption  + Take off Fuel
Ramp Mass -Taxy Fuel)  (RLM = Least of MSLM/PLLM)  (TOF)/FOB
Step 2: Apply following formula:
Regulated Take Off Mass (RTOM) = TOF/FOB+ MTL+DOM
DOM = Operating Mass - FOB
