Caging Knob: It provides a mechanical lock to prevent damage to gyro
frames. It can also be used for quick alignment of A/H. It is used in older versions
only, due to restricted pitch/roll movements. Gyro was caged during harsh
movements to avoid toppling.
Errors: Vaccum driver, Attitude indicators are free from most errors, but
depending upon the speed with which the erection system functions, there may
be a slight nose-up indication during a rapid acceleration and a nose-down
indication during a rapid deceleration. There is also a possibility of a small bank
angle and pitch error after a 180° turn. These inherent errors are small and
correct themselves within a minute or so after returning to straight-and-level
flight.
The following errors will occur assuming that an air driven gyro is rotating
anti clockwise when viewed from above:
(i) Acceleration/Decelerations: Error is caused due to left and right
pendulous vanes. During acceleration A/H shows climb and bank to the
right. During deceleration, it shows descend and bank to the left. (Left
and right pendulous vanes)
(ii) Turning Error: It is caused due to fore and aft pendulous vanes air
driven gyro. During left and right turns, Al shows climb. The magnitude
of the error varies with speed, rate of turn and self erection rate of gyro.

BANK ERROR
If a 180° steep turn is made to the right and the aircraft is rolled out to
straight-and-level flight by visual references, the miniature aircraft will
show a slight climb and turn to the left. This precession error, normally
3° to 5°, is quickly corrected by the erecting mechanism. At the end of a
360° turn, the precession induced during the first 180° is canceled out
by precession in the opposite direction during the second 180° of turn.
The slight precession errors induced during the roll-out are corrected
immediately by pendulous vane action. The following errors (assuming
an air driven gyro rotating anti-clockwise when viewed from above) will
occur:
Pitch Error: Shows climb. It is maximum at 180 and negligible at 360.
Pitch and Bank Angle Error: 90 degrees of turn - Indicates Climb and
under bank.
180 degrees of turn -Indicates Climb and almost normal with slight bank
in the opposite direction.
270 degrees of turn - Indicates Climb and over bank.
360 degrees of turn - Indicates normal attitude and normal bank.

A heading indicator displays headings based on a 360° azimuth, with the

Working:
(•)  It does not have its own north seeking element and needs to be aligned
after start and at frequent intervals.
(ii) Gyro is normally air driven.
Some more expensive heading indicators are 'slaved' to a sensor (called a
flux gate). The flux gate continuously senses the carth's magnetic field, and a
servo mechanism constantly corrects the heading indicator. These 'slaved gyros
reduce pilot workload by eliminating the need for manual realignment every ten
to fifteen minutes.
Errors: The Earth constantly rotates at 15" per hour while the gyro is
maintaining a position relative to space, thus causing an apparent drift in the
displayed heading of 15" per hour. When using these instruments, it is standard
practice to compare the heading indicated on the directional gyro with the
magnetic compass at least every 15 minutes and to reset the heading as necessary
to agree with the magnetic compass.
Wander:
(a) Real Wander/Random Wander or real drifi/real topple is due to
manufacturing imperfections, frictional forces and worn out bearings.
(b) Apparent Wander is due to the rotation of earth and movement of
Gyro on earth.

TURN AND SLIP INDICATOR (TSI)
Purpose: It gives rate of turn in degrees per second.
Type Of Gyro - Horizontal Axis Rate Gyro. It is normally electrically driven.
Principle: It uses property of gyro precession to give indicaaions.
TURN CO-ORDINATOR
Purpose: It gives both bank and rate of turn, when a/c is rolling in or out of
a turn it indicates bank angle.
When a/c is established in turn it gives rate of turn.
Principle:
Single Degree-Of-Freedom Gyro: A gyro the spin reference axis of which is
free to rotate about only one of the orthogonal axis, such as the input or output
axis.
A rate gyro (sometimes called a rate-of-turn gyro) consists of a spinning rotor
mounted in a single gimbal.
A gyro mounted in this manner has one degree of freedom; that is, it is free
to tilt in only one direction. The rotor in a rate gyro is restrained from precessing
by some means, usually a spring arrangement. This is done to limit precession
and to return the rotor to a neutral position when there is no angular change
taking place. Remember, the amount of precession of a gyro is proportional to the
force that causes the precession.
The main constructional difference between turn coordinator and TSI is
that longitudinal axis of the gyro gimbal is inclined at 30° to the horizontal. This
allows it to sense both roll and yaw. So that the gyro will respond to bank as well
as turning input force.
Rate 1 = 3°/ sec or bank not more than 25°  Rate 2 = 6° / sec
Rate 3 = 9° / sec  Rate 4 = 12° / sec

Working. As the aircraft yaws, a torque force is applied to the gyro around
the vertical axis, due to aircraft yaw, which causes gyro precession around the rol!
axis. The gyro spins on an axis that is 90 degrees relative to the direction of the
applied yaw torque force. The gyro and gimbal rotate (around the roll axis) with
limited freedom against a calibrated spring. The torque force against the spring
reaches an equilibrium and the angle that the gimbal and gyro become positioned
is directly connected to the display needle, thereby indicating the rate of turn.
Therefore even on ground the turn is indicated in correct direction.
Errors. When the aircraft yaws, or rotates about its vertical axis, it produces
a force in the horizontal plane that, due to precession, causes the gyro and its
gimbal to rotate about the gimbal's axis. It is restrained in this rotation plane by
a calibration spring; it rolls over just enough to cause the pointer to deflect unil
it aligns with one of the doghouse-shaped marks on the dial, when the aircraft is
making a standard rate turn.
Errors in TSI are caused due to load factor, RPM and spring tension, as
indicated in chart below.
378

Errors of Rate Gyro: Remember ROGOTU.
RPM more - Over read
G (load) factor more - Over read
Tension of spring more - Under read.
Rate-Integrating Gyroscope. The rate gyro's performance was limited by its
spring (i.e., the very mechanism that allows it to function also limits its -capability),
the performance requirements were mastered by the development of the single
degree-of-freedom, rate-integrating gyroscope. The integrating gyro is basically a
rate gyro in which the primary restraining torque on the gyro gimbal is a damping
reaction with a servo loop to maintain the gimbal at null. The perfection of the
integrating gyro is achieved by isolating the gyro gimbal by means of flotation
fluid and low reaction torque electromagnetic suspensions, pickoffs, and torquers.
INCLINOMETER/SLIP / SKID INDICATOR
The inclinometer in the instrument is a black glass ball sealed inside a curved
glass tube that is partially filled with a liquid for damping. This ball measures the
relative strength of the force of gravity and the force of inertia caused by a turn.
When the aircraft is flying straight-and-level, there is no inertia acting on the ball,
and it remains in the center of the tube between two wires. In a turn made with
a bank angle that is too steep, the force of gravity is greater than the inertia and
the ball rolls down to the inside of the turn. If the turn is made with too shallow
a bank angle, the inertia is greater than gravity and the ball rolls upward to the
outside of the turn.
379
===== [nav 0197]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
The inclinometer does not indicate the amount of bank, nor does it indicate
slip; it only indicates the relationship between the angle of bank and the rate of
yaw.
Use the simple rule, "step on the ball, to remember which rudder pedal
to press. In a slip, the rate of turn is too slow for the angle of bank, and the ball
moves to the inside of the turn. In a skid, the rate of turn is too great for the
angle of bank, and the ball moves to the outside of the turn. To correct for a slip,
decrease bank and/or increase the rate of turn. To correct for a skid, increase the
bank and/or decrease the rate of turn.
SLIP  SKID
More Bank  Less Bank (Acceleration due to gravity - (Ailerons - Bank)}
Less ROT  More ROT (Centrifugal Force - (Rudder - Rate of Turn)}
To correct  To correct
Less Bank  More Bank (Centrifugal Force - (Rudder - Rate of Turn))
More ROT  Less ROT (Acceleration due to gravity - (Ailerons - Bank)}
When taxing, the turn coordinator should indicate a turn in the correct
direction while the ball moves opposite the direction of the turn.
Slipping Turn  Skidding Turn  Coordinated Turn
If inadequate right rudder is applied in a right turn, a slip results. Too much
right rudder causes the airplane to skid through the turn. Centering the ball
results in a coordinated turn.

Chapter

INERTIAL NAVIGATION
A form of Dead-Reckoning" that relies on accelerometers and gyroscopes to
detect acceleration and velocity respectively along 3 perpendicular axes. This is
a technique for determining an aircraft's velocity and position by measuring its
acceleration with respect to a known set of axes, and processing that acceleration
in a computer. It has no need for external references, has nothing to do with
magnetism, no plotting is required, it is unaffected by weather, all corrections for
movement over the earth's surface and the movement of the earth itself are integral,
and position in latitude and longitude, distance flown and other navigational
information are presented on the flight deck. Provided that the vital preflight
setting-up is correctly done, there is no possibility of human error:
INPUT:
a.  Latitude and Longitude of the starting place. Insert way point
latitudes/longitudes.
b.  GPS Position.
c.  Barometric Altitude: helps to stabilise the vertical velocity and inertial
altitude outputs.
d.  True Air Speed (TAS): allows for the calculation of Wind Speed and
Direction.
388
INERTIAL NAVIGATION
OUT PUT:
Pitch, Roll and Yaw
b. True and Magnetic Heading
True Air Speed (TAS) and Ground Speed (G/S)
d. Latitude, Longitude and (inertial) Altitude
Wind Speed and Direction, and Drift Angle
Vertical Speed and Rate

Flight Management System

BATT
The inertial system measures and integrates the accelerations experienced in
flight by two accelerometers at right angles to each other.
389
===== [nav 0202]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
Gyro Compassing/Pre Departure: This is the process of aligning platform
with local meridian. Aircraft is to be stationary and in level position. Following is
the pre departure procedure:
Stand by: System is warmed up before inserting current position up to 6
seconds of arc.
Align: For leveling and alignment. This in fact continues while pre-departure
procedures are being carried out. The aircraft must not be moved during this
stage of the procedure, although the system is not affected by vibration such as
that caused by gusty winds, loading cargo and passengers, refueling etc.
Nav: Ready nav annunciator illuminates. INS makes all nav calculations and
displays them on CDU.
Attitude (ATT) Mode: In ATT mode, the IRS no longer calculates the position
and ground speed. The IRS can still provide the acceleration, angular rate and
attitude data. The ATT mode is normally used by a crew when the IRS self-detects
a critical fault that may cause excessive errors in navigation.
In such a case, the heading information needs to be manually entered
through the Control Display Unit (CDU) keyboard, and this entry has to be reset
frequently, approximately every 10 minutes.
Battery Light: Comes on in case of power failure. INS has a self contained 15
mins battery back up.
Accelerations are computed from velocity and time to latitude and
longitude of the aircraft's position, starting from the aircraft's start
position. Acceleration integrated with time gives velocity. Velocity
integrated with time gives distance.
The accelerometers are kept level on a stable platform by three rate
gyros and a system of gimbals and gimbals torques: thus only motions
tangential to the earth's surface are measured.
> Correction for the rotating earth, the aircraft's movement over a spherical
surface, the earth not being a perfect sphere, Coriolis, centrifugal
and centripetal acceleration are compensated for automatically via a
computer.
> INS Computer is programmed with values of Magnetic Variations at all
locations.
> All track, and return to track, requirements can be computed, displayed
or fed to the autopilot.
> Calculates winds by taking TAS inputs from ADC and using track
Heading and Ground Speed information from self contained computer.
390
INERTIAL NAVIGATION
Schuler Loop/Cycle/Damping: A stable platform which is maintained Earth
horizontal behaves like a pendulum with a length equal to the radius of the Earth.
The time taken for a pendulum to swing through one cycle is directly proportional
to its length. Where this length is the same as the Earth's radius, the time taken for
one cycle of the pendulum is 84.4 minutes. Older stabilised platform INS may have
position errors of 1 to 2 nautical miles (nm) per hour. Modern strap-down LRG
INS tend to have error rates of 0.5 nm/hr. Schuler tuning is bounded, that is to say
that the error does not increase with time beyond its original maximum value.
INERTIAL REFERENCE SYSTEMS (IRS) WITH RING LASER GYROS
There is no stabilised platform and three accelerometers are mounted
rigidly inside the inertial navigation unit, which is simply bolted to the aircraft
structure. The accelerometers are therefore effectively fixed to the air frame, and
are aligned with the aircraft's pitch, roll and yaw axes. Three ring laser gyros are
also mounted in the same unit, and again their sensitive axes are aligned with
the aircraft's pitch, roll and yaw axes. Because of its high degree of reliability
there are attractions in using a ring laser gyro in a strap-down system rather
than a gimbals platform with conventional spinning-mass gyros. In such RLG
systems, the inertial platform rotates with the aircraft. Three accelerometers
on the platform have input axes fixed in the longitudinal, lateral and vertical
axes of the aircraft. The three laser ring gyros on the platform sense movement
around these axes in roll, pitch and yaw relative to the surface of the earth. Their
outputs maintain the platform stable relative to earth mathematically rather than
mechanically. Thus the accelerometer outputs can be related navigation ally to
earth, i.e. North/South, East/West and in altitude. Among the advantages of ring
laser gyros is the fact that they have no spin-up time as with the conventional gyro
and are operational within a second so allowing a shorter INS alignment time.
IRS WITH RING LASER GYROS
391
===== [nav 0203]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
An IRS must be aligned before it can enter the NAV mode. Rotating the IRS
mode selector from OFF to NAV begins the IRS alignment. The IRS performs a
short power test, during which the ON DC light illuminates. When the ON De
light extinguishes the ALIGN light illuminates.
Advantages of the inertial system (Both INS and IRS):
(1) Indications of position and velocity are instantaneous and continuous
(2) Utterly self-contained, with no need of ground stations or whatever.
(3) Navigation information is obtainable at all latitudes and in all weathers.
(4) Navigation information is substantially independent of aircraft
manoeuvres.
(5) IFTAS is input, the W/V can be displayed on the CDU.
(6) Any inaccuracies may be considered minor as far as civil air transport is
concerned.
(7) Apart from the overriding necessity for accuracy in pre-flight
requirements, there is no possibility of human error.
(8) INS is a GC navigator hence follows the shortest route on earth surface.
Disadvantages of the inertial system (Both INS and IRS):
(1) Inertial Navigation Systems (INS) have a built in error due to the
precessing (caused by the earth's movement) that all gyroscopes
experience. In aircraft, this error can be as much as a half a mile per
hour: Over a 5 hour flight, for instance, an INS could be approximately
2.5 miles off and still be considered good. By comparison, a GPS over
the same period of time will usually have an accuracy of 30 yards or less.
For this reason, and the fact that inertial navigation systems are quite
expensive, INS is no longer considered state of the art technology.
(2) It needs to be fed data on ground only. Once shut or misaligned in flight,
it gives info in pitch and roll only. However, it is common nowadays for
GPS data to be used by INS for updating position info and for correction
of some of the errors while in flight.
(3) It has errors due to initial leveling and alignment, input inaccuracies.
real wander, drift, transport wander, Schuler Loop, Coriolis, earth
rotation, integration, accelometer, latitude and height etc. The ring
laser gyros do not suffer from any form of mechanical precession.
(4) Equipment is costly and is difficult to maintain and service.
(5) Initial alignment is simple when stationary, but difficult above 75° lat
and in flight.
392
INERTIAL NAVIGATION
Advantages of the IRS:
(a) High reliability before failure has been demonstrated. They are
completely solid-state units with no moving parts making the system far
more reliable, and much lighter.
(b)  Sensitive to a wide range of rotation rates (0.004 to 400 degrees/second).
(c)  The RLG does not suffer from mechanical precession, and cannot
topple.
(d) Much lower power inputs are required
(e)  Initial alignment times are faster with a strapped down system since
there is no platform to be aligned and the ring laser gyros achieve
their stable operating temperatures much faster than their mechanical
counterparts.
They are more accurate and have a longer life cycle.
(g)  They are also insensitive to 'g' so suiting high performance aircraft.
(h) The most modern IRS integrate is the satellite GPS. The GPS is extremely
accurate in itself. When combined with IRS, it creates one of the most
accurate navigation systems available.
(i) Replacement of the navigation unit is a much simpler procedure than
with a stabilised platform.
Disadvantages of the IRS:
(1) They suffer from a laser lock. To avoid it, dither motor is used, which
vibrates and opens the lock.
(2) Equipment is more expensive compared to INS.
Inertial Reference Unit (IRU):
Refers to a computer that integrates IRS outputs and provides inertial
reference outputs for use by other navigation and flight control systems, including
the Flight Management System (FMS).
Radial error of an Inertial Navigation System =
Distance of ramp position from INS indicated position - Elapsed time in
navigation mode, in hours
393

ALTITUDE ALERT SYSTEMS
An altitude alert system is a safety feature in aircraft avionics designed to
provide pilots with timely warnings or alerts related to the aircraft's altitude.
The primary purpose of this system is to enhance situational awareness and help
prevent altitude-related incidents, such as altitude deviations, controlled flight
18 | 19/20  into terrain, or inadvertent entry into restricted airspace.
bala  RADIO / RADAR ALTIMETER (RADALT)
The Radio Altimeter is a device capable of measuring the height of an aircraft
above ground with a high degree of accuracy. Apart from providing a flight deck
display of height above Ground Level (AGL), the radio altimeter has two other
important functions.
(a) It supplies the automatic flight system with data to affect automatic
landings when used in association with the ILS / MLS.
(b) The Radio Altimeter furnishes height information and rate of change
of height, to the Ground Proximity Warning System (GPWS), and is a
crucial component of this system.
The radio altimeter, or terrain-clearance indicator, is an absolute altimeter:
it indicates the actual altitude over terrain, however uneven. It operates by first
sending a radio signal from the aircraft to the earth's surface. A receiver in the
aircraft then picks up the reflection of the signal from the surface. The time it
takes for the signal to travel to the earth and back is converted into absolute
altitude which is displayed on a gauge. The radio altimeter does not depend on
barometric pressure settings.
The instrument makes use of primary radio principles transmitting a
Frequency Modulated Continuous Wave (FMCW) in an elliptical pattern vertically
below the aircraft.
399
===== [nav 0207]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
The Radio Altimeter determines the time it takes for a radio wave to travel
from the aircraft to the ground directly beneath the aircraft and back again.
During this time the transmitted frequency changes at a known rate from its start
level to +50Mhz and back again to complete a 'cycle".
The carrier frequency cannot be increased indefinitely and so after half
a wavelength the change is reversed, the frequency then being decreased at a
constant rate down to a specified value before being increased again, the complete
*modalation cycle/frequency sweep".
FREQUENCIES
Two frequency bands have been used in the past, but only the SHF band is
used at present:
4200 MHz to 4400 MHz - SHF band
The total sweep of the carrier frequency is automatically varied +50 MHZ
300 times a second. At very low altitude with the reflection returning almost
instantaneously, a wide sweep is necessary to give a measurable frequency
difference. The signal is transmitted downwards from a flush mounted horn
antenna. The conical / elliptical beam generated is wide enough to always allow
some portion of the beam to travel vertically even with pitch angles of +30° and
roll angles of 160°. The height will be determined from the shortest path to the
ground which, of course, will always be vertically below the aircraft.
4205 4202
4207 4205
4202 4201  4201 4200ł
4200
Transmission being continuous, it is necessary to use a separate antenna.
similar to the transmitting antenna for reception. The receiver antenna needs to
be positioned far enough away to avoid interference with the transmitted signal.
Radiated power generated is of the order of One Watt.

Fig- Radio Altimeter Indicator
Height Scale. The scale is logarithmic being expanded from zero to 500 feet
and at a reduced non-linear scale from 500 to 2500 feet.
Mask. The height pointer disappears behind a mask:
> When altitude exceeds 2500 feet
> When there is any fault in the transmitted signal
> When the altimeter is switched off
Failure Warning Flag. The flag appears when there is too much radio noise
which will corrupt the returning signal, or if local reflections are received from
the airframe itself, or in the event of a loss of power to the equipment.
Press to Test Button. When this button is pressed, the height pointer swings
round to a known pre-set altitude. This provides a confidence check for the user
indicating that the equipment is likely to operate satisfactorily.
Low Height Warning. The warning light illuminates if the aircraft is flown
below any pre-selected height set by the pilot. The pilot sets a desired height on
this instrument by means of the movable index (DH) control knob on the face of
the device. This occurrence is also audibly marked by the sudden cessation of an
alert tone which will sound with increasing loudness from approximately 100 feet
above the decision height setting.

Summary:
(i) A/C transmitter transmits frequency modalated continous wave of
continously and regularly changing frequency from 4200 MHZ to 4400
MHZ with avergae of 4300 MHZ.
(ii) Transmitted frequency is received after getting reflected from ground.
(ili) The transmitted frequency differ from frequency being received at
receiver.
(iv) If the height of a/c is more difference between transmitted and received
signal will be more and if the height of a/c is less, the difference between
them will be less.
(v) This time diff. is used to calculate height of a/c.
(vi) Radio altimeter can only be used up to 2500', and above that receiver
starts receiving same frequency as being transmitted.
(vii) Indications are in steps of 5'.
(viii) Radalt gives clearance below aircraft - not from terrain ahead.
(ix) Radio altimeter can be tested only on ground.
(x) Accuracy: Has a precision of +/- 2 feet between 0 and 500 ft.

ALTITUDE ALERT / WARNING SYSTEMS
TCAS (TRAFFIC COLLISION AVOIDANCE SYSTEM)
TCAS is also known as ACAS (Airborne collision avoidance system).
PURPOSE: It gives aural and visual alerts for proximate traffic.
Frequency: 1030 -1090 MHZ— UHF
Working:
(i) A/C equpt consists of interrogator and transponder
(ii) TCAS eqpt of a/c communicate with each other to generate traffic
advisory and if a/c is fitted with mode 'C/S' transponder, it generates
RA - Resolution advisory.
(iii) Maximum range of TCAS system is 30nm.
SYMBOLS ON EFIS/ DISPLAY
TCAS II will display four different traffic symbols on the Traffic Advisory
displays. The symbols change shape and color to represent increasing levels of
urgency.

WARNINGS:
TA = Traffic Advisory = no assessed threat at present.
RA = Resolution Advisory = threat, action given.
ACAS I: It generates TA only, alerts are generated in aural and visual e.g.
audio "TRAFFIC
Designed to aid visaal acquisition
Display range and approximate bearing, relative height (Mode 'C' or 'S')
Warns (TA visual/audio) 40 s from potential collision
ACAS II: It gives TA and RA, RA in vertical plane. RA is course of action to
be taken to prevent collision.
More accurate, offers RAs in vertical sense as well as TAs
If both with Mode 'S', computers talk, co-ordinate RA
Uses directional aerials, transponder, processor and CDU.
Power reduces in area of high traffic density.
ACAS III: Uses mode 'S' to give horizontal advisories also.
Preventive RA: It prevents conflict rather than resolving them.e.g.
MONITOR VERTICAL SPEED.
Corrective RA: It gives corrective action to be taken to reslove conflict. This
includes instruction to climb/ decend or to increase or decreases rate of climb or
rate of descend, e.g. 'CLIMB- CLIMB'DECEND - DECEND', " 1 CLIMB*... RA
is always accompanied with RAVSI.
The display comes either on seperate RAVSI or EFIS display (electronic
flight instrument system). If RA comes situation is serious.
Note:
(i) RA is generated 40 sec before potential collision.
(ii) No vertical RA is generated below 1000'. AGL..

TYPES OF RA TCAS IL
GPWS (GROUND PROXIMITY WARNING SYSTEM)
Purpose: It generates aural and visual alert when a/c is reaching dangerously
close to ground. The GPWS is designed to generate aural and visual warnings
if the aircraft enters a flight path to the ground that could lead to a potentially
dangerous situation. The various hazardous conditions that can be encountered
in flight are divided into five modes. These are:
409
===== [nav 0212]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
Mode 1: Excessive descent rate. p
This mode has an inner and outer warning boundary. Initial penetration
activates the warning lights and two "SINKRATE" aural messages. Should the  paul nevies entrate.
condition not be corrected and the inner boundary is penetrated, the aural
message changes to "WHOOP WHOOP PULL UP" which repeats continuously
until the unsafe condition is corrected.
Mode 2: Excessive terrain closure rate.
Mode 2 has two sub-modes, 2A and 2B. Mode 2A is active when flaps are not
in landing configuration and 2B is active when flaps are in landing configuration.
When the boundary is penetrated the warning lights come on and two&TERRAIN
messages sounds. If the closure condition remains the aural annunciation changes
to "WHOOP WHOOP PULL UP".
Mode 3:
Loss of altitude after takeoff (or go-around) when not in landing configuration.
Penetrating the boundary the warning lights come on and two "DONT SINK"
messages sound.
Mode 4: Insufficient terrain clearance when not in landing configuration.
This mode also has two sub-modes, 4A and 4B. When mode 4A is activated
and gear is not down the warning lights come on and a "TOO LOW, TERRAIN"
message sounds. Below certain height and speed the message is replaced by "TOO
LOW, GEARạ. If gear is down but flaps are not in landing configuration, mode 4B
is activated and the "TOO LOW, FLAPS" message sounds.
Mode 5: Descent below ILS glideslope.
When mode 5 is activated amber caution lights come on and the message
"GLIDESLOPE, GLIDESLOPE" is spoken with a low voice (soft warning). If
the terrain clearance decreases and/or the glideslope deviation increase, the
"GLIDESLOPE" message will become louder and continuous (hard warning).
The soft warning may be canceled.
Mode 6: Descent below selected minimum decision height.
Mode 6 is activated when descending through selected decision height
with gear down at where "MINIMUMS, MINIMUMS" will be announced. The
GPWS uses inputs from systems providing radio altitude, airspeed/Mach number,
landing gear and flap position, and DH setting.
410
ALTITUDE ALERT SYSTEMS
EGPWS (ENHANCED GROUND PROXIMITY WARNING SYSTEM)
EGPWS is also known as TAWS (Terrain avoidance and warning system).
Position of a/c from FMS (Hdg/ Trk, Altitude G/S).
Purpose: It performs all function of GPWS and in addition provides alert
and warning for terrain ahead, bank angle on approach and wind shear.
MODES OF EGPWS
MODE 1, 2,3 : are same as GPWS. In addition, mode 2 gives alert for terrain
ahead.
Mode 4 (a): same as GPWS and gives alert and warning below 500.
Mode 4 (b): gives alert for flaps below 256'.
Mode 4 (c): gives alert if radio altimeter reading reduces below a certain
proportion of its previous value after take off and go around.
Mode 5: Alert for excessive deviation from ILS G/S
(a) Soft Alert: this mode is activated when a/c is below 1000' of radio altitude
and deviation is more than 1.3 dot below glide slope. The alert comes in
half volume.
(b) Hard Alert: It comes on when a/c is 2 1/2 dot below, G/S or half of full
scale deflection, fly up indications. It comes in full volume.
Mode 6: It gives alert for decision height and bank angle. "BANK ANGLE
the envelop reduces as a/c descends below 150 feet.
Mode 7: It gives alert and warning for wind shear, below 1500'.
ACTIONS IN CASE OF GPWS ALERT: Stop/reduce descend, analyse alert,
take appropriate action and inform appropriate agency if necessary.
ACTIONS IN CASE OF WARNING: Select maximum power, climb with
pitch altitude which corresponds to stick shaker limit/ pitch limit. (max gradient)
and inform appropriate authority.

POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM
MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
Engine instruments give the pilot information about the health of the engine
and how it is performing during the flight. This information is also required to
keep the engine running within its operating limits so that it will remain reliable
throughout its lifetime. Engine data monitoring and logging tools create a history
of data in which trends can be detected helpful with preventive maintenance and
keeping the engine running for years to come.
Some common instruments are: RPM, pressure and temperature gauges:
fuel level and flow gauges are sometimes integrated in one unit. In multiengine
aircraft there is usually a set of gauges available per engine.
Engine Monitoring: Accurate engine monitoring is very important to keep
the engine running within its limits so as to ensure a safe flight. RPM, pressure
and temperature limitations may not be exceeded at any time.
Pressure Measurement
Pressure Gauge
Sensors: These are either made of a diaphragm or bourdon type sensors. This
depends on the pressure they are working with; bourdon types are more suited to
handle higher pressures. Reading is direct (with the pressure line running to the
cockpit) or indirect with electrical wires and the sensor close to the engine, either
on the engine or firewall.
414
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
Pressure Indicators
Oil Pressure. The direct reading type is made of a bourdon tube and this
is filled with thin engine oil. The indirect type uses a resistance ratio meter and
a firewall mounted sensor (sometimes on the engine, rotax). This sensor is a
diaphragm connected to a rheostat.
The ratio meter can be a millivolt meter or a electromagnet type with two coils
which attract the pointer and the rheostat is connected with a wire in the middle
of the coils so that the magnetic field is regulated with the varying resistance of
the rheostat.

Manifold Pressure (MAP)
Engines with a controllable propeller need the MAP gauge to set the correct
power setting with the selected propeller RPM. It reduces fuel consumption by
having a slight over square condition, for example 2200 RPM at 25iồạiỊMAP, engine
efficiency is also greatly improved.

Manifold Absolute Pressure Gauge
Usually an aneroid capsule or diaphragm is connected to a pointer with the
scale calibrated in pressure, inches of mercury (in HG) or bar. The instrument
can be made to show either only boost (pressure above atmospheric) or manifold
pressure, from 10 to 30" (in Hg) and for turbocharged engines the scale goes
higher, up 50ồẠỊạand sometimes more.
Fuel Pressure
This instrument is similar to a remote oil pressure described above. The
sensor is normally a diaphragm type and connected to an indicator in the cockpit.
Usually found on aircraft with fuel injected engines and low wing aircraft, although
some high wing aircraft do have one too.
The sensor should be fitted in the pressurized fuel line after any fuel filter
and pump to protect it from possible dirt in the fuel tanks.
Meaning of Coloured Sectors. Coloured arcs of Green, Amber, Blue and
Red indicate the range and limit of the system. A red radial line indicates the
maximum permissible MAP for take off power. Blue and Green arcs indicate the
lean and rich mixture ranges.
In case of an engine oil pressure gauge maximum and minimum pressures
are gauged by red lines and normal operating range by Green are.
Amber indicates caution, wherever used.
416
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
Temperature measurement
The various principles on which temperatures involving an aircraft can be
measured comprise:
1) The expansion of a solid as its temperature increases, so a measure of its
increase in length can be used to indicate its temperature rise. It is most likely
that in fact it will involve the differing coefficients of expansion of two dissimilar
metals where a helical bi-metallic strip coils and uncoils as the air temperature
changes.
2) The expansion of a liquid as its temperature increases so again measuring its
expansion, say along a tube, can be used to indicate the temperature rise.
3) The electrical resistance of a substance changes with variation ofits temperature,
so measuring changes of resistance can be used to indicate temperature change.
Usually the element used is a platinum resistance wire.
1) Dissimilar metals when joined at their ends (a thermocouple) produce an
electro-magnetic force (emf). This thermo-emf depends upon the temperature
difference between the junctions so measuring the emf gives a measure of the
junction temperature.
5) Many liquids change their state from liquid to vapour as the temperature rises.
Rise in temperature can be indicated by measuring the rise in vapour pressure
transmitted along a Bourdon tube. A Bourdon tube is a pressure-measuring
device consisting basically of a spiral or C-shaped metal tube having an oval
cross-section. One end, into which the vapour under pressure is fed, is fixed
while the other end is free to move and connected to the display system. As
pressure increases in the tube because the temperature has risen, the tube's
cross section tends to become more circular and the tube to straighten. The
tube movement is then linked mechanically to drive a pointer or a digital
display.
6) The radiation emitted by a body is determined by its temperature. If the
emitted radiation is measured this enables the body's temperature to be found.
From these basic principles, have been developed:
(a) expansion-type thermometers-solid and liquid elements
417
===== [nav 0216]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
(b) electrical-type thermometers -resistance or thermo-electric
(c) vapour-pressure type thermometers
(d) radiation-type thermometers.
Of these types, in aircraft the radiation pyrometer system may be used in
turbo-jet engines but for air temperature measurement, mainly one of the type
(b) is used.
Ram Rise
Thermometer sensor. In order to get the best measure of the ambient air
temperature, often referred to as the Static Air Temperature (SAT) for instrument
purposes, a position has to be selected for the temperature-sensing probe which
as far as possible avoids air subject to compressibility. Nevertheless, the usual
compromise between an ideal location and physically the most practical location
will result in a chosen position subject to some error in the indicated temperature.
7483
In some light aircraft, the thermometer sensor protrudes through the side
window of the cockpit and is directly connected mechanically to the instrument's
dial on the inside of the window. Most aircraft however have electrical resistance
sensors remotely located. Either they are mounted flush with the fuselage skin
-flush bulbs -or in specially designed probes, shielded from solar radiation.
Corrected outside air temperature (COAT). When the indicated outside
air temperature as given on the aircraft's thermometer dial on the flight deck
is corrected, either mentally by the pilot or automatically within the electronic
systems for known errors, it is the best determinable value of the temperature of
the air through which the aircraft is flying and for navigational purposes is called
the Corrected Outside Air Temperature or COAT for short.
Temperature Gauges. These instruments mostly use a thermocouple to
measure temperatures. A thermocouple consists of two dissimilar metals welded
together and they form the sensing probe, the hot junction. The cold junction
is at the instrument (a millivolt meter). When the hot junction is heated a small
voltage exists between the hot and cold junction inside the instrument. The scale
in calibrated in C or F.
Typical applications for temperature gauges in aircrafi piston engine
monitoring system are oil temperature, cylinder head temperature, carburetor
air temperature and exhaust gas temperature. In gas turbine engines oil
418
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
temperature and either turbine inlet or exhaust gas temperature gauges are
essential requirements. Air inlet temperature is also usually indicated.
Exhaust Gas Temperature (EGT). Fitted on the hottest cylinder exhaust pipe
of some carbureted engines and used for leaning the engine. If done properly,
each cylinder should have an EGT indicator. FADEC controlled fuel injected
engines can not live without this important measurement, since they regulate the
amount of fuel in combination with RPM and MAP to obtain the best mixture for
the power requirement without detonation in the cylinders.
Cylinder head Temperature (CHT). Also a thermocouple probe fitted in
the cylinder head. Each cylinder should have one, but it is usually installed on
the hottest cylinder (which ever that maybe, this doesn't so much depend on the
position but on the mixture entering the cylinders). Made from iron or copper
constantan (copper and nickel alloy) and able to measure up to 400°C.
Oil and Carburetor Temperature. This is not a thermocouple but either a
bourdon tube (older type aircraft) or a variable resistance (ratio meter) type. The
bourdon tube is a hollow flattened tube made of brass bend over a certain arc
and fixed at one end in the instrument, the other end attached to a pointer. Any
pressure applied in the tube will try to straighten it, moving the pointer.
A volatile chemical like methyl chloride is used as fluid in the probe and
bourdon tube in a partly liquid / gaseous state. Its pressure will rise when the
temperature of the probe rises, thereby moving the pointer.
The electrical resistance sensor changes its resistance related to temperature.
The circuit is a ratio meter as these are not sensitive to voltage variations; they
measure a current and this current change only as the resistor senses a change in
temperature
Meaning Of Coloured Sectors. Temperature indicators are coloured arcs to
show their operating range. Green for normal, amber for caution and Red upper
or lower limits.
The face of the oil temperature gauge is marked with a GREEN sector
to indicate the safe normal operating temperature range; RED line indicate
the minimum and maximum safe operating temperatures. The carburetor air
temperature gauge is typically calibrated from -50°C to + 150°C. There are three
coloured arcs on the face of the gauge. YELLOW, from - 10°C to + 15°C, indicates
419
===== [nav 0217]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
the icing hazard range. GREEN, from +-15°C to +40°C, is the normal operating
range. RED, above +40C indicates detonation hazard.
Tacho - RPM
RPM Indicators, Piston and Turbine Engines. The measurement of engine
speed is of vital importance, since together with other parameters accurate
control, and monitoring of the engine can be achieved. On piston engines it is
crankshaft speed that is measured, whilst on gas turbine engines it is the speed
of the compressor. The RPM indicator is called a Tachometer (Tacho). There are
three basic methods of measuring engine rotational speeds:
• Mechanical (Magnetic) Tachometer.
• Electrical Generator System. (Tacho Genny)
• Inductive Probe System.
Smaller aircraft piston engines commonly use mechanical tachometers,
in which a drag cup is driven directly from the engine by means of a flexible
drive. RPM measurement in larger piston engines is often by means of an
electric tachometer, in which the output of an engine-driven alternator powers
a synchronous motor and drag cup. Gas turbine engine RPM may be measured
using an electric tachometer in which the output of an engine-driven alternator
is converted to pulsed DC and drives the RPM gauge pointer through a torque
motor and potentiometer. Turbo-fan engines usually employ a magnetic tacho
probe method of RPM measurement.
Interfacing Of Signal Pick-Up To RPM Gauge
Older aircraft have a mechanical cable driven tachometer, inside the
instrument are a couple of flyweights to which a pointer is attached indicating
RPM. Not very common in newer modern aircraft anymore.
Electrical generator system types have the cable attached to a magnet which
rotates inside a aluminum cup. In this cup eddy currents (generated by the
rotating magnet) create a magnetic field coinciding with the magnetic field from
420
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
he magnet. The cup is fixed to a spring and pointer so it can not rotate but it is
dragged along by the magnetic field, hence the name: drag cup.
Electrical tachos have either a DC or AC generator on the engine. The DC
generator generates a voltage related to RPM, which drops if the wires are too
long. The AC types relate RPM to a frequency and these are immune to wire
length or resistance and are more reliable than the DC models.
The latest modern engines have an electric pickup from either a separate coil
on the engine (Rotax) or on the magneto. This signal is pulsed so that a digital
counter can be used to indicate engine RPM.
Meaning of Coloured Sectors. In line with other instruments, coloured arcs
or indicators lines are used to show ranges and limits of engine speed. Green
representing normal operating range, with Amber denoting caution. Red arcs
show maximum or minimum speed, and range that are restricted because
of excessive vibration. On a piston engine the reference RPM should also be
placarded.
RPM gauge displays usually incorporate coloured arcs and limit markings,
with a GREEN arc indicating the normal operating range of engine speeds and a
RED line indicating maximum permissible RPM.
Consumption Gauge
Fuel Flow. This is usually a vane sensor located downstream behind the fuel
filter and pump measuring the amount of fuel going to the engine. Fuel injected
engines might have a fuel return line running to the tank in use, so for a correct
reading the fuel returning must also be measured with a flow sensor.
There are two principal methods of measuring fuel flow in aircraft,
independent and integrated systems.
Independent systems use a metering device fitted in the fuel supply line
that converts mechanical movement of a spring-loaded vane into a proportionate
electrical output, either by means of a potentiometer or an a.c. synchrony of the
type described above in connection with pressure indicators and illustrated. The
electrical output operates an indicator, which is simply a milliammeter whose scale
is calibrated in rate of fuel flow (gals/hr, kg/hr and so on).
The moving vane flow meter normally incorporates a spring-loaded bypass
valve, which will open in the event of the vane sticking and blocking fuel flow to
the engine. Opening of the valve may activate a warning indicator on the flow
gauge.
Independent fuel flow system use a flow sensor in the form of a spring-loaded
421
===== [nav 0218]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
vane in the fuel feed line, the deflection of which increases in proportion to the
rate of flow and is converted into an electrical signal by means of a potentiometer.
The output of the potentiometer operates a milliammeter, the face of which is
calibrated in rate of fuel flow. Integrated flow measurement systems incorporate
an integrator which computes furl flow against time to display fuel used in
addition to fuel flow.
High Pressure Line Fuel Flowmeter (Function, Indications, Failure Warnings).
Integrated systems display both flow (kg/hr) and total fuel consumed (kg). To
achieve this the system includes an integrator, which computes fuel flow against
time to give fuel used. Fuel flow metering and transmission is a little more complex
then in the independent system. In essence it consists of a turbine impeller in
the fuel supply line to the engine whose speed of rotation depends upon rate
of fuel flow. Speed of rotation is converted into an electrical signal by electro-
magnetic induction and fed to a computer. The computer operates the fuel flow
and consumption gauges.
In large turbine engine fuel systems the flowmeter is usually situated in the
high-pressure fuel line between the fuel control units (FCU) and the burner
nozzles. The associated gauge typically indicates both flow rate and fuel used and
may incorporate a low fuel flow warning.
Fuel Gauge
Measurement of Volume/Mass, Units. There are two principle methods of
indicating the quantity of fuel carried. Either the Volume (e.g. Gallons) or Mass
(kg or Ibs) are measured. The former is now only used on light aircraft as the Mass
of the fuel is of more interest to the pilot. This assists the pilot in calculating the
aircraft's 'all-up-weight' and also gives a better indication of the energy that can be
released by the fuel. One pound of fuel has the same number of energy molecules
regardless of temperature and volume.
Measuring Sensors
Content, Quantity Indicators
Fuel Quantity. There are two types in use: the float and the capacitance
quantity sensor. The float type measures fuel level by a float in each tank connected
to a variable resistance. It is not very accurate due to turbulence, airplane attitude
and such.
The capacitance type is much more accurate especially during flight in
turbulent conditions. It consists of a probe made of two concentric metal rods
422
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS
solated from each other. These rods have a certain capacitance and when the air
between them is replaced by fuel that capacitance changes indicating fuel level on
a calibrated instrument.
The capacitance can be measured electronically with high impedance sensitive
circuits in such a way that at no time a high voltage is present inside the fuel tank.
As with the float type the instrument must be calibrated as each fuel tank has
a unique form causing the fuel level to drop on line air in the tank.
Reasons for Incorrect Indications
The disadvantages of the resistive method of tank contents measurement are
the false indications that arise from: (a) fuel movement in the tank and (b) fuel
expansion due to increased temperature. The capacitive system of tank contents
measurement overcomes (a) by using a number of measuring probes and an
"averagingi system in the electrical supply to the tank contents gauge. Expansion
due to temperature increase is offset by the decreased permittivity of the fuel, so
that the dielectric constant remain essentially the same despite an increase in fuel
level.
Float Type. The float and resistance systems are only accurate when the aero
plane is in steady straight and level flight. Additionally, if the aero plane accelerates
or the temperature changes, the volume of fuel indicated on the gauge also varies.
This type of gauge is also subject to inaccuracies if the voltage fluctuates, which
may be caused by voltage regulator settings or a week battery.
Capacitance Type. If there is a significant amount of water in the tank, it lies
at the bottom of the tank, affecting the compensating or balancing probe, which
results in an incorrect adjustment of the calibration. For example, if the tanks are
drained and then refilled to the same level with water, the pointer will tend to
move up the scale to a new point of balance. This is because water has a greater
density and dielectric constant then aero plane fuel.
Torque Meter
The power produced by a propeller is proportional to the torque, described
best as the turning moment produced by the propeller about the axis of the
output shaft.
The power developed by an engine driving a propeller is measured and
indicated by a torque meter. Some large supercharged piston engines are fitted
with these instruments, but in general they are more commonly associated with
turbo-propeller powerplants. Torque is the turning moment transmitted through
the propeller reduction gearing and is principally considered in terms of positive
423
===== [nav 0219]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
torque developed by the engine when it is driving the propeller. When the
propeller is wind milling it develops negative torque and drives the engine.
The power output of the engine is the product of rpm and torque, so clearly
if both are known the power can be calculated, either manually or by a simple
computer: Since exceeding the torque limitations of the engine and its reduction
gearing can lead to serious damage, it is essential that the pilot of a turbo-prop
aircraft be provided with a torque meter.
Indicators. Aircraft torque meters are usually either hydro-mechanical or
electronic devices.
The hydro-mechanical type operates on the principle of pistons that are
moved in their cylinders an amount proportional to the turning moment. The
cylinders are connected to a closed hydraulic system and so the system pressure.
due to the movement of the pistons, is directly proportional to torque and is used
to operate a pressure gauge calibrated to indicate torque.
Electronic torque meters are typically actuated by strain gauges placed in
a suitable location in the drive train between engine and propeller. The small
electrical output of the strain gauges is amplified and fed to the torque meter in
the cockpit.
Units: Units of torque are newton metre (N• m) or the joule per radian or
pound-foot.
Meaning of Coloured Sectors. Torque indicators are sometimes colour-
coded in a similar manner to other engine instruments, with red lines indicating
permissible torque limits and a green arc to indicate the normal operating range.
In some cases a yellow are in the lower range cautions against the onset of negative
torque.
424
POWER PLANT AND SYSTEM MONITORING INSTRUMENTS

BASICS OF ELECTRONIC
DISPLAYS
Flight Management Systems (FMS):
FMS has been described as 'pilot-interactive navigational computing and
display systems designed to assist in flying an aircraft with maximum economy
and safety to a previously planned route delined both laterally and vertically.'
The increased use in airborne applications of compact, high-speed computers
enables FMS to provide fuel-efficient aircraft operation together with navigational
guidance reducing flight-crew workloads especially in terminal airport areas.
(a)  Flight Management and Guidance Computer (FMC) - uses both
manual and automatic inputs of data to compute 3 dimensional position,
performance data etc. in order to fly the aircraft accurately and efficiently
along a pre-defined route.
(b) Multipurpose Control and Display Unit (MCDU) - the interface
between the pilots and FMC.
(c) Flight Control Unit - supplies the commands to control the lateral and
vertical flight path of the aircraft.
(d) Flight Management Source Selector - selects the sources of input to be
used by the FMC.
(e) Display System - any means of displaying the required data/information
to the pilots.
Flight Management Systems basically consist of two units - the flight
management computer unit (FMC) and the control/display unit (CDU). The
426
BASICS OF ELECTRONIC DISPLAYS
computer unit is the store for the navigational data base and performs the functions
of flight planning, optimum performance, computation, navigation and position
determination, air data and navigation sensor selection guidance and steering -
laterally and vertically. The data base of an FMC updates the information every 28
days. The data base includes information on: Airports (identified by their ICAO
4-letter codes): latitude and longitude, runways, approach aid frequencies and
idents, decision heights, SIDs and STARs; Waypoints (identified by name): latitude
and longitude, magnetic variation, associated holding procedures; Navaids
(identified by name): type, latitude and longitude, frequency, elevation, associated
holding procedures; Airways (identified by name); Performance aircraft type data
including lift, drag, engine fuel flows, engine thrusts, performance factors.
Control/Display Unit (CDU) is a cathode ray tube presenting the information
above the keyboard. The required fields are selected by the keys on the CDU and
the contents displayed on the CRT.
The FMS is operated through three groups of keys comprising:
(a) Function keys -used to select the display;
(b) Alphanumeric keys -used for data entry by the crew;
(c) Line keys -used for crew interaction with the displays.
FMS use may be divided into Route Management and Performance
Management. In large airliners, twin flight management systems are fitted.
Electronic Flight Instrument System (EFIS)
A 380 Displays
427
===== [nav 0221]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
Boeing 777-300 ADI (attitude direction indicator)
The natural process of evolution has now brought both civil and military
flight decks to the Electronic Flight Instrument System (EFIS) or the so called
Glass Cockpit with its coloured cathode ray tubes (CRTs) presenting information
previously shown by mechanically operated instruments. There are several
Electronic Displays in use with several common features. The features discussed
below are of general nature and may or may not be found in all displays.
EFIS displays information on two, approximately 5 inch square, screens for
each pilot. One screen corresponds to the ADI (attitude direction indicator) or
Primary Flight Display (PFD) contains the basic instruments information like
airspeed, altitude, attitude, azimuth etc., and displays mainly flight parameters.
The other corresponds to the HSI (horizontal situation indicator) or Navigation
Display (ND) contains navigation information. Terms PFD and ND arc associated
with Airbus EFIS and ADI and HSI with Boeing.
There is also a multi-function display (MFD) which may embody or
supplement the weather radar display. Other displays can include aircraft systems
status and engine performance monitors, an electronic centralized aircraft
monitor (ECAM), crew alerting system (CAS), or an electronic library (EL).
In put. The input to, and the basic components for, a typical EFIS are shown
in Fig

BASICS OF ELECTRONIC DISPLAYS
EICAS/ECAM
The computer-generated electronic displays which show the aircraft systems
are generally known as either the Engine Indicating and Grew Alerting System
(EICAS) or as Electronic Centralized Aircraft Monitoring (ECAM). Basically,
EICAS is a Boeing term whereas ECAM is an Airbus term.
EICAS. The sensors collect data from various points around the aircrafi and
transmit it to the computers. The computers process the sensor data and generate
display information. Only one computer is necessary to drive the system. The
upper display shows primary engine data and warnings and cautions. The lower
display show engine secondary data or other system information depending on
pilot selection. If one display fails the other will show a engine primary data and
other information in a compressed format. If both of the displays or both of the
computers fail the standby engine indicators show essential engine related data.
155. C3
The basic EICAS system has three modes of operation. These are Operational,
status, and maintenance. Only the operational and status modes are available in
43 1
===== [nav 0223]
PART II - AIRCRAFT INSTRUMENTS
flight. The maintenance mode is not available on the flight deck and can be used
only on the ground.
The system provides the flight crew with information on primary engine
parameters (full-time), with secondary engine parameters and advisory / caution
/ warning alert messages displayed as required.
ELECTRONIC CENTRALIZED AIRCRAFT MONITORING (ECAM). AS
far as the processing and display of information are concerned, the ECAM system
differs significantly from EICAS in that data relates essentially to the primary
systems of the aircraft, and is displayed in check-list and pictorial or synoptic
format.
The ECAM system has four modes of operation. These are normal, advisory.
failure and manual. In normal mode the system displays information relating
to the correct stage of flight. This information is shown in checklist form on the
left screen and in diagrammatic form on the right screen. In advisory mode is
selected automatically in the event of a non-emergency change in the status of
any of the monitored systems. Failure mode takes precedence over all other and is
selected automatically if any monitored system parameter exceeds pre-set limits.
All four ECAM modes are available in flight.

===== [nav 0232]
Chapter
1
BASIC RADIO THEORY
Simple AC Circuit: A changing electric field produces a perpendicular
magnetic field, and a changing magnetic field produces a perpendicular electric
field.
All of this can take place in open space, the alternating electric and magnetic
fields supporting each other as they travel through space at the speed of light.
This dynamic structure of electric and magnetic fields propagating through space
are better known as electromagnetic waves.
Direction of ratation
27G°  360
Current flow in a simple AC Circuit: Sine Wave Construction
Frequency. The number of radio waves passing through a point per second
is called the Frequency of Radio Waves.
Wave Length. The distance covered by a wave in units of length, while
traveling through 360 degrees, is called wave length.

Polarisation: - In an E.M. wave two component i.e. electrical and magnetic
component travel perpendicular to each other in a direction which is mutually
perpendicular to both. The direction in which electrical component lies is called
direction of polarization.
Polar diagram:- In gives aerial characteristics of a transmitter or a receiver.
For a transmitter its drawn up to a distance where, signal strength reduces up to
50% of transmission.

Types of Propulsion:
i) Continuous Wave Modulation: It is a continuous transmission. Transmitters
and receivers use separate antennaes and are capable of independent
functioning.
ii) Pulse modulation: - In pulse modulation transmission is made in the form of
short pulses of either A.M. or E.M. signal or information and is carried in the
452
BASIC RADIO THEORY
form of short coded pulses, e.g. used in primary and secondary radars. In
this case, transmitter and receiver use common antennae and switch functions
alternately.
A.M.
F.M.
Modulation: - Process of super imposing audio frequency or information on
to carrier wave is called modulation.
Modulation is of 3 types:-
(i) Amplitude  (ii) Frequency  (ili) Phase
(i) Amplitude Modulation:
The amplitude of the carrier wave is varied in accordance to amplitude of
audio frequencies keeping the frequency constant. VHF communications are AM.

Characteristics of Amplitude modulation:-
(a) Transmitter is complicated to design, where as receiver is simple.
(b) It is susceptible to static and noise.
Static - Any disturbance due to external;
Noise - disturbances with in the system.
(ii) Frequency Modulation:-
Frequency of the carrier wave is varied in accordance to frequency of audio
signal, keeping the amplitude constant.
Frequency
Modulation
Unmodulated
confer wave
Characteristics of Frequency Modulation:-
(a) Transmitter is simpler whereas receiver is complicated to design.
(b) Free from static and noise interferences. E.g. - FM radio, aeronautical
communication.
Draw Backs: - For same transmission power range is lesser.
454
BASIC RADIO THEORY
(¡li) Phase Modulation:-
Phase of carrier wave is varied after fixed interval (used in G.P.S.).

Properties Of Radio Waves:
E.M. waves travel at the speed of 3 × 10° m/s
(її)  They get refracted, reflected, diffracted and attenuated during their
propagation (attenuation is lessening of amplitude in time).
(Gi)  Their direction and velocity both change while passing from one medium to
other.

===== [nav 0235]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
While passing from medium of lower density to higher density they bend
towards the normal and vice-versa. They always bend towards the denser medium.
ormal
(c) away
Propagation of Radio Waves
EM waves travel from a transmitter to a receiver in two ways, i.e.
Ground Waves propagation
(11) Sky Waves Ionoshere propagation
Ground Wave: - All waves other than those refracted from atmosphere are
covered under ground wave propagation. These include:
Direct wave or Line of Sight waves: - A wave directly received at the receiver
from the transmitter is known as direct wave. Examples VHFUHESHF and EHE
LOS (range) = 1.25 (VheightofEx + VHofKx)
Ht = feet  Range = nm
HIGH FLYING AIRCRAFT
RECEPTION RANGE EXTENDE DI
LOW FLYING AIRCRAFT
AXIMUM RECEPTION RANGE
Direct wave
Surface Waves: - A wave which follows curvature of earth due to bending
caused by diffraction and attenuation is called surface wave. These waves bend
round the obstacles which come in the path of these waves. Due to this rounding
456
BASIC RADIO THEORY
through the obstacles, the intensity of the electromagnetic waves will decrease
rapidly. surface wave propagation is possible only for those waves which have very
less frequency, nearly 2MHz. Examples VLF, LF and ME.
Ground Reflected Wave: • A wave received at a receiver after a reflection
from the ground.
Duct Waves. Under certain meteorological conditions, radio waves in the
VHE, UHF and SHF bands, which normally travel only in straight lines, may
behave in a way which is at first sight similar to sky waves.
The meteorological conditions required for duct propagation are a marked
temperature, inversion and a rapid decrease in humidity with height. Figure
shows ducting which, in this case, is occurring between the surface and a low-level
inversion. The signal is effectively trapped under the inversion and may travel
hundreds of miles with little attenuation. In this way, when high pressure systems
prevail, interference may be heard from distant VHF communications stations
which are far beyond the normal direct wave range.
SURFACE WAVE FOLLOWING THE CURVATURE OF THE EARTH

PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
Sky Wave / Ionosphere Propagation. A wave received at receiver after
getting refracted from atmosphere. The sky waves have frequency range between
2MHz to 30MHz. Frequencies below approximately 10 MHz (wavelengths longer
than 30 meters), including broadcasts in the medium wave and shortwave bands.
propagate most efficiently by sky wave at night. Frequencies above 10 MHZ
(wavelengths shorter than 30 meters) typically propagate most efficiently during
the day. Examples of sky waves are MF and HE
IONOSPHERE
Thermosphere
Mesosphere
Stratosphere
Tropopause
111 kam
Troposphere
IONOSPHERE: The layer of negatively charged particles caused due to
presence of U.V. rays is called ionosphere.
WINTER DAY F  NIGHT  SUMMER DAY
WINTER OR SUMMER
E Layer 100 - 150 km (Aug - 125 km)
D Layer 50 - 100 km (Aug - 75 km)
458
BASIC RADIO THEORY
The lonospheric Layers
(i) Electron density is maximum in Flayer and least in D layer.
(ii) During night D layer disappears and electron density reduces in E layer
due to lack of UV radiation.
The height of ionosphere apparently increases during night.
Skip Distance: Distance from transmitter to a point where first sky wave
return is received.
Dead Space: Distance between limit of surface wave to the point where first
‹ky wave return is received. In dead space there is no signal available either from
ground wave or from sky wave.
Angle of Incidence: The angle which a radio wave forms with normal at
transmitter is called angle of incidence.
Critical Angle: The angle of incidence at which first sky wave is received on
earth is called critical angle. Signal lesser than critical angle does not return.
IONOSPHERE
The Ionospheric Refractions
Fading: Due to the disturbance created by the waves, some variations can
be seen in the signal strength at the receiver end. This is known as fading effect.
If the frequencies of the sky waves will be high, then the fading effect will
increase. Errors in data transmissions and data retrievals are also caused by
fading. Fading basically varies with time.
459
===== [nav 0237]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
NIGHT EFFECT ON HF COMMUNICATION: At night A/C goes into dead
space and hence frequency has to be reduced.
During night D layer disappears and electron density decreases in E layer.
A wave leaving transmitter has to penetrate further in ionosphere before getting
refracted to earth. Due to this, skip distance increases and a receiver comes in
dead space. HF communication frequencies are reduced to half during night to
prevent receiver to come in dead space.
Remember Sun up, frequency up, sun down, frequency down
VLE, LF are absorbed by layers. VHF and above pass through the layers.
Part of MF and HF are reflected back.

ADF / NDB
The ADF navigation receiver is connected electronically to a rotating pointer
or needle on the face of the ADF indicator in the instrument panel. The ADF
receives signals from non-directional beacons (NDBs) or from broadcast stations
within (roughly) the 190 - 1750 khz bands.
Note that the ADF receiver in the cockpit is a different set altogether from
the NAV/COM radio. Frequencies of specific NDBs can be found on the charts.
NDBs operate within the Low Frequency (LF) and Medium Frequency (MF)
bands between 190 kHz and 450 kHz. Voice information can be transmitted from
an NDB ground station to an aircraft but it is not possible for a pilot to reply using
the same frequency on the ADF radio set.
Function Switch
BROAD F
3 3 9-0  KHz
SHARP  Frequeney Selected
IDENT VOL TEST
Frequency Selection Controls
RBI Indicating a RB of 080°  ADF Control Panel
There are many different designs in ADF control panel but they all have
these items in common:
465
===== [nav 0240]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
Function switch which can be turned to:
• OFF, the ADF is off;
ANT (or REC), the ADF is not automatic, use this setting for tuning;
ADF (or COMP), the ADF is in automatic mode.
CW (or BFO) Switch to make the IDENT audible.
Frequency selector, to tune to the required NDB or broadcast station.
Test button which, when pressed, the station 'ident' is verified. Even though
the correct frequency may have been selected, it is possible for the frequency
selector mechanism to malfunction. However, in modern ADFs which use digital
LED read-outs, the frequency shown is the frequency selected.
NONAIA. The signal consists of two separate elements, the NON portion
and the AIA portion. The NON part of the signal is, as we know, continuous
carrier wave, and is ideally suited to enable the ADF to establish the direction from
which the signal is arriving. The AIA part of the signal periodically replaces the
NON transmission and, being interrupted carrier wave, is used to carry the two/
three letter Morse identifier for the NDB. NONAIA beacons are normally used
with high power outputs for long range NDBs. However, ICAO recommends
NONAA beacons for long range beacons.
NONA2A. Similar to NONAIA stations but now the station identifier is
carried by the A2A signal (keyed signal tone amplitude modulation). NONA2A
beacons are normally used for medium range NDBs.

ADF / NDB
Beat Frequency Oscillator (BFO)
To make the un modulated parts of the AlA signal audible, ADF incorporates
a BFO.
This produces a signal slightly removed from the received frequency that is
then mixed with it.
The mixing of the two frequencies produces an audible beat frequency, the
difference of the two.
e.g. a BFO would generate a signal of 262 KHz to match a received signal of
260 KHz.
The mixing or heterodyning of the two produces signals at 260 KHz, 262
KHz, 512 KHz and 2 KHz.
The beat frequency of 2 KHz is the audible tone.
Selecting the BFO on makes the NON carrier wave audible and allows the
AIA type of ident to be heard.
BFO is not needed for an A2A signal which is already modulated to an
audible frequency.
ADF = Automatic direction finder - A/C Equipment
NDB = Non directional beacon- Ground Equipment
Purpose and Use: - Used for homing, holding, approach and en-route
purposes.
Frequency: - LF aad ME 190 - 450 KHZ (NDB), 190-1750 (ADF).
NDB band is from 190 kHz to 450kHz.Because 500 kHz is the international
maritime distress (emergency) frequency, it is not used for NDB. 190 - 1750 KHZ
(ADF). This includes 530-1750 kHz for AM commercial radio stations Frequency
band (High LF and Low MF).AM radios can be tuned on ADF for guidance.
Principle Of Operation: - Relative Bearing by loop theory, switched cardioids.
Type of Bearings:
• True bearing - measured from True north.
• Magnetic bearing - direction measured from magnetic north.
• Relative bearing - measured from nose of a/ c or heading.

Working:-
ADF consists of 2 antenna i.e. loop and sense antenna
Loop antenna has directional property. When loop is aligned (at 0°)
with NDB, both vertical arms receive signal in different phase, causing
potential different which in turn induces EMF.
When loop antenna is 90" to NDB, induced EMF is zero. Therefore
induced EMF varies as function of antenna angle.

ADF Loop Aerial
A simple loop antenna suffers from 180° ambiguity.
Direction property error (180" ambiguity) is resolved by using a sense
aerial.
Polar diagram of loop aerial is figure of 8 and that of a sense antenna
is a circle. Combined Polar Diagram of both aerials is a cardioid which
has only one maxim and one minim.
Null position of cardioid is used to rotate pointer of RBI (Relative
Bearing Indicator).
Loop antenna is rotated in such a manner that null is always 90° to
ground station.

ADF / NDB
Factors Affecting Range Of NDB:
(i) Tx power: more power, more will be range.
Range aVTxpower
To Double NDB range, increase power 4 times.
(ii) Terrain: Between land and sea, sea will have more range.
(iii) Tx frequency: lower the freq. more will be range.
Night Effect: It causes NDB range to reduce to max of 70 nms.
Operational DOC: Causes NDB range to reduce.
Accuracies (day only): Upto 70 NMs + 2°, Beyond 70 NMs ‡ 5°( within DOC.)
Types of NDB :
Locator NDB: less power NDBs, are normally co- located with ILS
marker beacon. Locators have 15 W power, 10-25 nm range and are
NON A2A.
Homing/ Holding/aerodrome NDB: Used for medium range navigation
and instrument approaches. Have 200 W power, 50 nm range and are
NON A2A.
• Long range NDB: Used for long range nav. No fixed range, range
depends on factors like frequency, transmission power, location etc.
Factors Affecting Accuracy of ADF:
1. Night Effect: During night, due to lesser electron density, sky wave is present
in MF band also. This sky wave interferes with horizontal arm of loop causing
distortion of the melt position. RBI pointer haunts oscillates. There is a loss of
signal and fording. This error is called night effect.
Method to Reduce Night Effect:
(i)  Use the beacon which is transmitting at low frequency because lower the
frequency more is the lonospheric attenuation. Presence of sky wave
will be less. Night effect is maximum at dawn and dusk.
(ii) Use the NDB which is within 70 nm from a/c position. As first sky wave
return is received at a skip distance of 70 nm or more.
(iii) Use a beacon which is transmitting at high transmission power, as
ground wave will be stronger.
2. Coastal Error: Atmospheric density over sea is lesser than density over land.
A radio signal leaving coastline will bend away from normal or towards land/
denser medium. This will cause relative bearing of a/c to be shown in error.
This error is called coastal error.

Coastal Error
Methods to Reduce Coastal Error:
(i) Fly as high as possible as atmospheric density will be similar at higher
level.
(ii) Use a beacon whose signal is leaving coastline at 90". Acuter the angle
more is coastal error.
(iii) Use a beacon which is located near to coastline than inland.
(iv) Use a beacon which is transmitting at higher power as the bending will
be less and so the error.
3. Quadrantal Error: Electromagnetic field and metalic surfaces of a/c causes radio
signal to undergo a direction change before it reaches loop Ariel. This error
is called quadrantal error. The error is maximum when signal is approaching
the a/e quadrantal or on inter cardinal heading.
Quadrantal correction card is provided with a/c. Quadrantal Correction
(Q.C.) is applied to A.D.F. Reading, also known as Loop Scale Reading (L.S.R.) to
eliminate Quadrantal error.
Relative Bearing (R.B.) = L.S.R. ‡Q.C.
470
ADF / NDB
270° No error
Quadrantal Error
4. Interference Error: DOC is Designated operating range. When A/Cis operating
within coverage area of more than one NDB, it may experience interference.
The error caused due to this is called interference error. To prevent this DOC
is given in AIP. In DOC it is guaranteed that there will not be any interference
from any other NDB. It is also called protected range.
DOC for NDB is valid only during day.
e wave overlap
Designated Operating Range
5. Mountain Effect : Hills and mountains reflect and re-radiate the LF or MF
signals of an NDB. Consequently, the ADF in a low-flying aircraft may receive
both the great circle signal and a re-radiated signal at the same time. This will
result in an erroneous bearing, as shown at Figure.

Mountain Effect
6. Static Interferences and Precipitation: The interference caused by external
atmospheric conditions e.g. thunderstorms, CB. All precipitation, including
snow reduces range and accuracy of NDB. These disturbances cause greatest
inaccuracy in ADF bearings.
7. Lack of Failure Warning Systems: The majority of ADF instruments do not
incorporate a failure warning indication. Consequently, failure of any part of
the airborne receiving or ground transmitting apparatus may produce false
bearing indications which are not readily detectable.
8. Bank Angle (Dip): The angle ofbank induces emfs into the horizontal elements
of the loop aerial resulting in a bearing error.

heading to be steered by the aircraft, with no wind, to make for the station is
.. Degrees.

bearing: Magnetic bearing of the aircraft in nil winds is  degrees.
QTE/TBF/track or TMG on a map or chart from a station: True bearing of
the aircraft in nil winds is  . degrees

QUJ/TBT/ track or TMG on a map or chart to a station: True heading to be
steered by the aircraft, with no wind, to head for the direction finding station is

Remember:
Winds are always True. Aerodrome METAR and ATIS winds are Magnetic.
Runway heading is always Magnetic.
Track and TMG is always True, unless otherwise stated in the question. ATS
Routes are Magnetic.
TMG RIGHT OF HEADING, DRIFT STATBOARD
TMG LEFT OF HEADING, DRIFT PORT
TMG RIGHT OF TRACK REQUIRED, TRACK ERROR STARBOARD
TMG LEFT OF TRACK REQUIRED, TRACK ERROR PORT
Heading: The direction in which A/C longitudinal axis is aligned / nose is
pointed.
TMG = H + D
TMG = RT + TE
Starboard and (+), Port subtract (-)
Relative/ADF/Radio Compass/NDB Bearing: Bearing from the longitudinal/
fore and aft axis (heading) of the aircraft to the station.
QDM =QDR + 180°
True Trk to go to stn. (QUJ) = HDG (T) + RB
Mag Trk to go to stn (QDM) = HDG (M) + RB
QUJ=QDM ‡ Variation
True bearing from stn (QTE) = QUJ # 180°
QTE= QDR ‡ Variation

Reciprocal Heading
To find reciprocal of a heading, if the heading is equal to or more than
( ≥) 180°, subtract 180°. If less than (<) 180°, add 180°.

TRACK AND DRIFT QUESTIONS
09-  An A/C is flying hdg with 12° S drift and making good a trk 5 nm to the left
of center line of the airway but parallel to it. Find RB of NDB situated at
centerline of the airway 30 nm ahead.
Presume heading of ac 090° to the station. (You may presume any heading).
MBT(Required Track) = 090° + 12° = 102",  TEA = 0 = (5 X 60) + 30 = 10i
Final MBT(TMG) = 112°, RB = Final MBT(TMG) - heading = 112° - 090° = 022
Q10. An A/C is flying a constant hdg with 12° P drift and making good a trk. 5 nm
to the right of center line. Find ADF reading for NDB situated on center and
30 nm behind.
Presume heading of ac 090° from the station.
MBF(Required Track) = 090° - 12° = 078.  TEA = 0 = (5x60) + 30 = 10°.
Final MBF(TMG) = 078° + 10° = 088°
RB = Final MBT(TMG) - H = 268° - 090° = 178.
Q11. An a/e is flying a constant heading with 8°S drift and making good a trk 5 nm
to the right of centerline of an airway but parallel to it. Find ADF reading
from an NDB situated on the centerline of airway 30 nm ahead.
Presume heading of ac 090° (to the station).
MBT (Required Track) = Heading # Drift = 090° + 8i = 098
TEA = (5 × 60) + 30 = 10°, Final MBT(TMG) = 088°,  H = 090°,
RB = Final MBT (TMG) - H = 088°- 090° = - 2° + 360 = 358°
