Chapter
4
VERY HIGH FREQUENCY OMNI
DIRECTION RADIO RANGE (VOR)
NAV COM SET
HOO 260 M
COM1-
A.133
DISPLAY FROM
The VHF omni Range (VOR or omni) is a high-quality line-of-sight radio
nav aid and is one of the most widely used aids to en route navigation. Each VOR
operates on a frequency in the VHF band between 108.00 MHz and 117.95 MHz.
Typical VOR frequencies are 113.70, 116.30 (shown below), 117.75 MHz and so
on.
The VOR radio set with controls and switches is normally built into the same
panel as the VHF-COM transceiver; the two radio installations combined are then
referred to as a NAV/COM set.
The pilot cannot transmit using a VHF-NAV radio but it is possible for an
Air Traffic Service ground station to transmit on a VOR/NDB ident frequency.
e.g. when providing Automatic Terminal Information Service (known as ATIS) or
when using the frequency in situations where this may be the only way in which
to contact an aircraft which has suffered radio communication failure.
486
VERY HIGH FREQUENCY OMNI DIRECTION RADIO RANGE (VOR)
Purpose and Uses: Navigation, magnetic bearings by day and night. Airways,
routes, instrument approaches, holding, radial interceptions, track maintenance
and position information.
Band: 108 - 117.95 MHZ.A total of 160 channels.
108 - 112 MHZ it shares with ILS localizer.
All odd first decimals e.g. 109.5, 110.5 localizer:
All even first decimals e.g. 110.2, 109.4 VOR.
Remember LOVE (Localizer- odd, VOR- even).
112 - 117.95 MHZ - exclusively for VOR.
Principle: Bearing by Phase comparison of two 30 hz. signals.
Radial: magnetic track radiating out from the station.
VOR ARE OF TWO TYPES; CONVENTIONAL VOR (CVOR) AND
DOPPLER VOR (DVOR).
Principle of CVOR Operations: The VOR facility transmits two signals at the
same time. One signal is constant in all directions, while the other is rotated about
the station. The airborne equipment receives both signals, looks (electronically) at
the difference between the two signals, and interprets the result as a radial from
the station.
Fixed signal  Variable signal of
30 Hz  30 Hz
Fixed reference  Rotatonyant
FM
A fixed aerial transmits a ref. signal (frequency modulated) on transmission
freq. from an omni directional antenna. The signal is received in same phase in
all 360° directions. Polar diagram of fixed antenna is a circle. A directional or
variable signal of 30 HZ amplitude modulated is transmitted by an directionally
rotating antenna at a rate of 30 cycle per second or 1800 rpm. Antenna rotation
is clockwise.
The phase difference between the reference and variable signals for a
conventional VOR is the magnetic bearing from the station (MBF/QDR).
487
===== [nav 0251]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
For example if QTE is 220°, variation 10°W, MBF/QDR will be 230°. Therefore
the phase difference is 230°.
This signal is received in different phases depending on direction of
antenna. Polar diagram of rotating antenna is rotating fig. of 8.
Both reference and variable signals are in phase with magnetic north.
Phase diff. between signals at any place corresponds to magnetic
bearing from the ground station.
A/c receiver measures phase diff. to give indications.
VOR transmitter transmits 360° Magnetic tracks.
Combined polar diag. of VOR transmitter is LIMACONS

Comparison of ADF (Cardioid) and VOR (Limacon):-
1) Cardioid - is created at a/c (receiver)
2)  Limacon-is created at ground (Tx)
3)  Cardioid - has a well defined null
4) Limacon -does not have a well defined null
VOT: One acceptable way to formally check VOR accuracy is with a VOR
Test Facility, more commonly called a VOT. A VOT is a low-power omni station
located on many of the mid-to-large size airports. A VOT differs from a standard
omni in that it transmits only a single radial, the 360° radial. It is used to test
VOR receiver. The difference between reference and variable signal is 180° in all
directions.
Rotate the OBS until the CDI needle centers. Read the number from the
omni Bearing Indicator ring or digital display. To be legal, the gauge must be
within 4° of either 180° with the TO flag showing or of 000° with the FROM flag
showing.

VERY HIGH FREQUENCY OMNI DIRECTION RADIO RANGE (VOR)
Remember: Cessna 182.
OBS = 180° + 4° (176"- 184)→ CDI center → To- From → To
OBS = 000 + 4° (356" - 004) → CDI center → To- From → from
NOTE: VOT can be tested any where on ground. VOR test is at designated points.
ACCURACY:
i) Accuracy of transmitted signal should be # 3° at 95% of time for ground
equipment.
ii) Accuracy for VOT + 4°
ili) Accuracy of VOR during airborne check + 6°.
iv) Overall equipment accuracy is # 5° at 95% of time for both ground and aircraft
equipment.
NOTE: A VOR identification code is transmitted every 10 sec.
Identification code is removed if any of the following takes place.
Bearing information is wrong by more than 1i
(ii) Failure of monitoring equipment
(iii) Reduced signal strength i.e. 15% below normal transmission power.
CONE OF CONFUSION
A VOR transmits a horizontally plagiarized signal at an angle of 40°- 80"
above the horizontal. There is no signal transmitted above the transmitters. This
area is in the form of cone where no signal is available is called cone of confusion.
Indications over cone of confusion are
(a) To/ From will flicker
(b) CDI will oscillate
(c) Failure warning flag may come on.
489
===== [nav 0252]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
CONDITION IN WHICH FAILURE WARNING FLAG COMES UP:
a) No power or low power to a/c equipment
b) Failure of a/c equipment.
c) Failure of ground station equipment.
d) Failure of indicator.
e) Weak signal from reference/ variable signal.
f) During tuning of equipment.
FACTORS AFFECTING ACCURACY OF VOR
Site error  SI
(ii) Airborne equipment error  Aggregate Error
(iii) Propagation error  P
(iv) Beacon Alignment Error
(v) Pilatage Error
1. SITE ERROR: The error is caused due to presence of structures, uneven
terrain or physical obstacles in the vicinity of VOR transmitter. The error may be
caused due to presence of trees, wires, fences or over grown grass. This is also
called course displacement error.
Maximum permissible is 1°. If the error exceeds, either code is removed or
transmitter is switched off.
Direct wave
Reflected wave
Obsiacle
2. AIRBORNE EQUIPMENT ERROR: Error is caused due to VOR receiver
inaccuracies induced during manufacture or over a period of time. Maximum
permissible error is + 10.
3. PROPAGATION ERROR: Error is caused during the propagation through
medium. The error may be induced due to reflection, refraction or diffraction.
4. BEACON ALINGMENT ERROR: If reference and variable signal are
not aligned with magnetic north, radial may get displaced. This error is called
beacon alignment error.
5. PILOTAGE ERROR: Close to a VOR station, radials are closely packed
causing CDI to become sensitive and it becomes difficult to follow a particular radial.
490
VERY HIGH FREQUENCY OMNI DIRECTION RADIO RANGE (VOR)
FACTORS AFFECTING RANGE OF VOR:
Tx Power (it)  LOS (Line of sight) (ili)  DOC
1. Tx Power: Higher the transmission power more is range. Hence to double
the range, power is to be increased 4 times.
Range a V Txpower
2. LOS (range) = 1.25 x (Vheightof Tx + VHofRx )
Ht = feet  Range = nm
3. DOC (Designated Operational Coverage): If an aircraft is operating at
high altitude the situation may arise whereby the VOR set is receiving signals
from two stations operating on the same frequency. One of these stations will
presumably be the station selected by the pilot, the other station will therefore be
interfering with the desired signal. The consequence of this station interference
will be an erroneous bearing indication.
DOC is the area in which it is guaranteed that there will not be any interference
from nearby operating VOR. DOC causes VOR range to reduce. For VOR DOC
is given in both range and altitude, whereas for ADF it was only range.
DVOR - Doppler VOR: VOR transmitter aerials should be sited on flat
terrain to minimise site errors. If such a site is not available, a complex aerial
system may be employed to transmit the VOR signal. This type of station is known
as a Doppler VOR (DVOR) beacon and produces a signal which is reasonably free
of site errors even when the transmitter is sited in hilly terrain DVOR uses 50
antenna to eliminate site error. Its' working principle is otherwise same as that of
a conventional VOR. Airborne equipment to receive both type of VORs is same.
In this system fixed signal is on AM and variable signal is on FM. Antenna
rotates anticlockwise.
FREQUENCY: frequency Band and Principle of operation are same as that
of VOR.
ADVANTAGE: It does not suffer from site error.
TYPES OF VOR
Standard VOR (VOR): Widely used to define airways centerlines, transmitter
power normally 200 watts.
Terminal VOR (TVOR): Low-powered beacons used as airfield location aids.
Widely used in some parts of the world in conjunction with DME, as a procedural
approach aid.
Broadcast VOR (BVOR): Broadcast VOR beacons provide bearing
information in the usual manner. Additionally, a voice modulation is superimposed
491
===== [nav 0253]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
on the carrier wave. The audio information normally provides an Aerodrome
Terminal Information Service (ATIS) giving present weather, runway in use and
serviceability state of the airfield and associated navigation aids.

NAV Flag
a) Omni Bearing Selector: It is rotating knob by which any of the 360°
mag. tracks can be selected with respect to the ground station.
b) Course Deviation Indicator: It gives deviation from selected track.

(i)  OBS selection
(ii)  Position of aircraft with respect to ground station.
d) Alarm Flag: A prominent alarm flag will appear whenever:
(i)  The airborne receiver fails, or power supply is lost.
(i¡)  The aircraft receives no acceptable VOR signal, due to range, height,
or because the aircraft is directly overhead or abeam the station.
(iii) The VOR ground station fails.
When the alarm flag is not visible it will be replaced by either a TO or
a FROM indication depending on the aircraft position in relation to the VOR
station and to the magnetic track selected in the window.

RMI Indicates QDM / MBT:
Compass Rose of RMI is slaved to the Gyro Compass.
ADF needle shows QDM driven from the mechanical addition of magnetic
hanicat adding and adde
heading and relative bearing as a result of compass rose rotating and adding
these inputs.
VOR needle gets its input directly from the VOR station.
Information available on a RMI is:
(i)  Magnetic heading
(ii) Relative bearing
(iii) QDM / QDR (Head / Tail)

HOLDING, RADIAL INTERCEPT, BEARING TO PLOT
HOLDING SPEEDS (IAS)

Entry Procedures: The pilot is expected to enter a holding pattern according
to the aircraft's heading in relation to the three sectors shown in Figure, recognizing
a zone of flexibility of five degrees on either side of the sector boundaries. For
holding on VOR intersections or VOR/DME/TACAN fixes, entries are limited to
the radials or DME arcs forming the fix as appropriate.
Parallel Entry: Figure depicts the hold entry procedures. A sector 1 entry
is known as a 'parallel entry and involves flying outbound along the published
inbound track of the hold for one minute (adjusting for head/tail wind component)
before turning left (in a right hand hold) or right (in a left hand hold) back to the
beacon.
Offset/Teardrop Entry: A sector 2 entry is known as a 'teardrop' entry and
involves flying a magnetic track 30 degrees off of the reciprocal of the published
inbound track for one minute (correcting for wind) before turning back to the
beacon.
Direct Entry: A sector 3 entry is known as a 'direct' entry and in this case the
holding pattern is immediately entered on crossing the beacon.
Non Standard Hold: It is a mirror reflection of standard hold. Turns are
to the left. There may not be a standard timing. Other things are same as per
standard hold.
Non Standard Pattern  Standard Pattern
Turning left at the facility  Turning right at the facility

Sector 2
Sector 2  Procedure
Offset  Procedure  Sector 1  Holding

Scetor 3

Sector 1  side  Offset

Holding Patterns: Drift and Time Corrections:
Correction for drift:
(i) On out bound → THRICE of drift.
(ii) On In bound → Actual drift.
There is no change in the rate of turn but rather the drift is applied thrice on the
outbound leg to make correction.
Time Correction: Only on outbound. Increase/ decrease 1 second for every knot of
HW/TW..

RADIAL INTERCEPTS
• Three standard intercept angles are 30°, 60° and 90°.
• Use Intercept Angle given in question, otherwise calculate standard.
• Intercept Angle = 2x (MBT ~ Final MBT), rounded to next 30°, 60" and 90°.
Steps:
Find out MBT/MBF and final MBT/MBF from the question.
Calculate intercept angle if not given in the question.
Intercept angle = 2x(Angular Difference between MBT/MBF and
Final MBT/MBF), rounded upto 30, 60 or 90 degree standard intercept.
3. New Heading (Heading to intercept track) = Final MBT/MBF # Intercept Angle.
There are two possible New Headings. One is obtained by adding intercept angle to
MBT/MBF and second by subtracting. Chose the more suitable of the two to ensure
the more practical option.
4. To find Relative Bearing use;
Relative Bearing (RB) = Final MBT - MH
Find in following questions:
(i)  Intercept Angle
(ii) Which side a/e should turn and through how many degrees.
(ili) New hdg to intercept
(iv) ADF reading at intercept

BTP (Bearing To Plot)
Plotting VOR / ADF Bearings.
Bearings are always plotted from the station. Only true bearings
(QTE) / (TBF) are plotted.
In order to obtain a bearing to plot from a VOR station to the aircraft
using an OBI simply rotate the OBS until the needle is vertical with the
FROM flag showing. The reading in the window can now be plotted
after applying variation and convergency as given below.
• In case of ADE, calculate QTE from RB and Magnetic Heading. If
required, apply Quadrantal Correction to Loop Scale Reading.
- RMI can be used for getting QDR for both VOR and ADE. Apply variation
to convert QDR to QTE. Variation at the station/aircraft is applied for
VOR/ADF and the resultant bearing plotted from the meridian passing
through the station.
- It is not necessary to apply convergency when plotting VOR/ADF bearing
on a Lambert or Polar Stereographic chart. It is however necessary to
apply a correction for conversion angle when plotting VOR bearings on
a Mercator chart, if the change of longitude between aircraft and station
521
===== [nav 0268]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
Steps
1. Find out QTE.
QDM ÷ Var + 180° = BTP
LSR + QC + hdg (T) ‡ 180° = BTP
2. On Lamberts chart QTE = BTP.
3. On Mercator chart, apply Conversion Angle to QTE. QTE ‡ CA = BTP.
When using ADF use variation at the a/c position
When using any other ground beacon apply Ground Station
(G/S) variation.
To find mean hdg to fly, use mean variation.

===== [nav 0270]
Chapter
7
ILS (INSTRUMENT LANDING
SYSTEM)
Localiser Antenna
In addition to the VOR, there is another radio (approach and landing) aid
known as the instrument landing system (ILS). Localiser of ILS operates on
VHF frequencies between 108.00 MHz and 111.95 MHz using the same VHF-
NAV receiver which controls the VOR, only few are equipped with the additional
ILS facility.
When you fly the ILS, you're really following two signals: a localiser for
lateral guidance (VHF); and a glide slope for vertical guidance (UHF). When you
tune your Nav receiver to a localizer frequency a second receiver, the glide-slope
receiver, is automatically tuned to its proper frequency. The pairing is automatic.
There is more to an ILS than the localizer and glide slope signals.
• Guidance Information: the localizer and glide slope.
• Range Information: the outer marker (OM) and the middle marker (MM)
beacons or a low power DME collocated with Glide Path.
• Visual Information: approach lights, touchdown and centerline lights and
runway lights.
526
ILS (INSTRUMENT LANDING SYSTEM)
Localizer (azimuth information): The localizer signal provides azimuth, or
lateral, information to guide the aircraft to the centerline of the runway. It is
similar to a VOR signal except that it provides radial information for only a single
course; the runway heading. Localizer information is displayed on the same
indicator as your VOR information. Some localizers are designed to sine a back
beam in the area behind the lopcalizer.
When tracking the localizer the pilot turns towards the needle in the same
manner as with VOR navigation.
047
Tracking inbound on the Localizer to runway 05, from left to right, the
aircraft is 1deg Right of course, two dots (turn left to return), on course, and
Idegree left of course(turn right to return).
Frequency: 108 - 112 MHz (odd first decimals i.e. 110.3, 119.9, 110.1).
Band = VHF band.
Principle = Bearing by lobe comparison or DDM (difference in depth modulation).
Vertical Coverage = -1° to +7
527
===== [nav 0271]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
HORIZONTAL COVERAGE:

Elevation Coverage  Azimuth Coverage  Radiation Pattern
Glide slope (Elevation Info): The Glide Slope is the signal that provides
vertical guidance to the aircraft during the ILS approach. The standard glide-
slope path is 3" downhill to the approach-end of the runway. Follow it faithfully
and your altitude will be precisely correct when you reach the touchdown zone
of the runway.
Think of the glide slope as a localizer laying on its side, squirting a signal 3°
up into the air, and you'll have it just about right.
Tracking the glide slope is identical to tracking a localizer. If the glide-slope
needle swings away from center-up or down maneuver the aircraft towards the
needle by adjusting the engine's power. Don't point the aircraft's nose up or down.
047
Inbound to runway 05, from left to right, the aircraft is a above glide path, on
glide path and below glide path

or to such lower angle, down to 0.30° 0, as required to safeguard the promulgated
glide path intercept procedure upto atleast I0NM.

modulation (above the glide path).

Instrument landing system reference point: A point that is on the centerline
of a runway served by an ILS and that is designated as the optimum point of
contact for the landing aircraft. A point at a specified height located vertically
above the intersection of the runway center line and the threshold and through
which the downward extended straight portion of the ILS glide path passes.
Marker Beacon
Marker beacons are used to alert the pilot that an action e.g., altitude check
is needed. This information is presented to the pilot by audio and visual cues.
The ILS may contain three marker beacons: inner, middle and outer. The inner
marker is used only for Category I1 operations. The marker beacons are located
at specified intervals along the ILS approach and are identified by discrete audio
and visual characteristics.
All marker beacons operate on a frequency of 75 MHz.
The OM, 3.5 to 6 NM from the runway threshold, normally indicates where
an aircraft intercepts the glide path when at the published altitude. The MM,
3500 feet from the runway threshold, is the Decision Height point for a normal
ILS approach. On glide path at the MM an aircraft will be approximately 200'
above the runway. The IM 1000 feet from the runway threshold is the decision
height appx. 50' point for a Cat 11 approach.

Frequency Pairing GP. The GP frequency is paired with localiser as per the
list published by ICAO. Pairing facilitates one switch activation of both receivers,
the probability of error is eliminated and only one identifier is needed. Identifier
usually starts with letter I in morse code with a tone of 1020 Hz. It goes off if
equipment fails. However for localiser approaches it may be kept on and pilots
cautioned about the non availability of GP
DME pairing with ILS. A low power DME frequency paired with ILS is
widely used to supplement or replace markers/locators. The DME ranges are zero
referenced to runway threshold.

Primary Radar. With primary radars EM waves are beamed from the
transmitter, via the aerial, into the atmosphere. In the event that these pulses
strike a target, a small proportion of the transmitted energy will be reflected back
to the aerial and fed into the receiver. The direction in which the aerial is pointing
at this time denotes the bearing of the target, while the time between transmission
and reception of each individual pulse is used to determine target range from the
radar head.
EM waves may be transmitted in pulses (pulse radar) or continuously
(continuous wave radar). In pulse radar single antenna is used for transmission
and reception. Pulses are regulated for optimum range as explained below.
PRP/T/I
Pulse width/Pulse Length/Pulse Duration: time interval elapsed in micro
second from start of a pulse to end of a pulse.
Pulse Recurrence Period/Time/Interval (PRP/T/I): It is time interval elapsed
from start of one pulse to start of the next pulse.
Pulse Recurrence Frequency/Rate (PRF/R): Number of pulses/second
PRF =
PRI
541
===== [nav 0278]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
PRIMARY RADAR CALCULATIONS
PRIMARY RADAR RANGE CALCULATIONS:
Timing in Radar: 1 second = 1000 or 10° milliseconds or 1000000 or
10° microseconds (us).
• The distance traveled by the pulse (metres)=Speed of propagation
(metres/sec) x Time between transmission and reception of the pulse.
The target range = Half the distance traveled by the pulse.
MAX RANGE: In order to avoid range ambiguity each pulse must
return to the radar head before the following pulse is transmitted. The
pulse repetition frequency affects the maximum range of a pulsed radar;
the higher PRF the shorter the maximum range.
Max Radar Range = (C x PRT) ÷ 2 or C ÷ (PRFx2)
* MIN RANGE: If the reflected signal is received whilst the aerial is still  formed co aved.
transmitting and has not transformed into a receiver, the reply will not
be received.
Min Range = speed of propagation x Pulse width + 2 or (C x PW) + 2
THE RADAR RANGE AND POWER EQUATION:
To increase radar range by a factor kk, the power required increases by kk ^ 4.
For example:
To double the radar range, the power required increases 16-fold.
(k=2), power increases by 2 ^ 4=16.
To triple the range (k=3), power increases by 3 ^ 4=81.
To quadruple the range (power increases by 4^4=256.
Questions:
Q1  The time between transmission and reception of a single pulse is 300
microseconds. Determine the range of the target.
a) 45 nm  b) 90 nm  c) 45 kms.
Sol. : Max. Range = (C x PRT)/2 = (3 x 10º) x (300 x 10-°) +2 = 45000m = 45 kms.
542
RADAR
Q2  Ignoring pulse width and fly back, calculate the maximum range in nautical
miles for a primary radar having a PRF of 500 pulses per second.
a) 162nm  b) 300mm  c) 162 kms.
Max. Range = C/2 x PRF = (3 x 10) ÷ (2 x 500) = 3 x 10°m = 300kms = 162nms.
Q3  Ignoring pulse width and fly back, calculate the maximum permissible PRF
for a primary radar which is required to give a range of 200 nm.
a) 370 pps  b) 405 pps  c) 150 pps
Sol.: PRF = C/Range x 2 = (3 x 10*) ÷ [2 x (200 x 1852)] = 405 pps
The maximum range of a pulsed radar is governed by which parameter?
a) PW  b) PRF  c) Speed of Propagation

THE RADAR RESOLUTION RECTANGLE:
Azimuth or Horizontal Resolution: A narrow beam is necessary for pulsed
radar systems, in order to achieve maximum range by greater concentration of
power, and to accurately determine the target bearing. The ability of a radar to
discriminate in a lateral direction between two targets is called azimuth resolution.
The lateral dimensions of the target are distorted (stretched) by one whole beam
width. Therefore two targets to paint on radar should be separated by a beam
width. For example, a radar has a beam width of 4°. Using the 1 in 60 rule it can
543
===== [nav 0279]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
be calculated that two targets at a range of 45 nm will be stretched by about 3
m, and would therefore also have to be separated by 3 nm in order to show as
separate returns. Narrower beam width has a better Azimuth Resolution.
Radial or Range Resolution: The pulse width or pulse length will determine
the minimum range of the system, whilst the pulse repetition frequency will
govern the maximum theoretical range. The higher the, PRF the shorter the
maximum range. A target continues to paint on the screen until the trailing edge
of the pulse has ceased to illuminate the target. The depth distortion is governed
by half the pulse width, since the time involved covers the return journey.
In modern radars resolution rectangle is resolved by suppressing the target
paints and replacing them with electronically produced crosses.
Q10. If the pulse width/length in a primary radar was 2 u sec, the range resolution
would be?
a) 1000m  b) 300 feet  c) 984 feet
Sol.: Range Resolution = (3 x 10*) x (2 p sec) ÷ 2 = 300m = 984 ft
011. The advantage of CW radar over a pulse system is that:
a) there is no minimum range.
b) a single antenna can be used for TX and RX.
c) is useful for SSR operations.
Q12. Beam Width 3°, Pulse Width 3 p sec. Two targets 2nm apart will appear as
separate targets at what distance from the radar head?
a) 80NM  b) 50NM  c) 40NM

RADAR
RADAR APPLICATIONS
GROUND BASED RADARS
En-Route Surveillance Radar (RSR). En-route surveillance radars are used
to monitor airways traffic at ranges up to 250 nm. Range and bearing information
is provided by a Primary Radar, with a Secondary Surveillance Radar providing
additional information. The preferred frequency for these radars is 600 MHZ,
giving a wavelength of 50 cm. At these relatively long wavelengths, rain and
weather present far less of a problem than with higher wave length. Antenna
rotation rates are low (5-15 rpm) as target movement at long ranges is relatively
slow.
AIRFIELD RADARS
Terminal Area Surveillance Radar (TAR). Operate on 5 and 15 rpm.
Beam has a horizontal width of one degree and a vertical depth of 40 degrees.
Frequency range is from 600 MHz to 3000 MHz. Range is appx. 75 um. Employ
lower frequencies (i.e. longer wavelengths, typically 10-20 cm), lower PRFs and
larger pulses to give greater ranges with less attenuation.
Aerodrome surveillance (approach) radar. A short range, approximately
25nm, primary radar providing positional information. Used for the control of
aircraft in the vicinity of an aerodrome and for limited approaches.
Precision Radars. These are confined to military airfields.
Airport Surveillance Detection Equipment (ASDE). Use short wavelengths,
typically 3 cm to give short pulses at relatively high PRFs for low minimum range,
better resolution and greater accuracy. Antenna rotation rates are high (up to
60 rpm) as target movement at short ranges is relatively fast and frequent radar
updates of position are therefore required. Short range ground/surface movement
radars, which are referred to as Airport Surveillance Detection Equipment (ASDE)
are installed at major aerodromes to control the safe movement of aircraft on the
ground, principally during low visibility operations (LVO). Ground movement
radars operate in the SHF band. This frequency gives the required target
definition with acceptably low levels of weather clutter at the very short ranges
required of the system. Range is appx. 2.5 to 6 nms.
Weather radar. A primary radar used by the meteorological service to
supplement current weather conditions like thunderstorm location, finding
upper winds etc.
545
===== [nav 0280]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
AIRBORNE RADARS
AWR (AIRBORNE WEATHER RADAR)
PRINCIPLE: Echo and search light
FREQUENCY: 8 - 12 GH
BAND: SHF (X Band)
WAVE LENGTH: 3 cm. Large water drops and storms give good reflection at
3 cm. wavelength and small drops like fog/mist and stratus clouds do not reflect
at this wavelength.
RANGE: 150mm (With range selection options of 20,50 and 150mm)
Gyro stabilization of radar antenna is done in pitch and yaw.
LCD Colour Screen Indications:
Heavy/strong - Magenta/Red
Moderate/mild - Yellow
Light - Green
GROUND MAPPING MODE:
Cosecant squared Beam: If weather beam is used for ground mapping, the
nearer parts of beam would give stronger reflection as compared to farthest part.
In mapping mode transmission power is varied at the rate of cosecant square
of depression angle. The farthest part of beam has more transmission power in
comparison to nearest part.
DETERMINING THE HEIGHTS OF CLOUD TOPS
The angle of tilt of the scanner is gradually increased/decreased until the
cloud in question just ceases to paint on the screen. The tilt elevation and the
range of the cloud are then noted.
P= 0 x D

and for cloud bottom + sign)
P = Height of cloud above/below aircraft in feet
D = Range in NMs x 6080'
Tilt angle is angle between center of the beam in level flight and the center
of the tilted beam.

DOPPLER RADAR
DOPPLER EFFECT:
According to Doppler effect, the frequency of the received signal will increase
if the target is moving towards the direction of Radar. Similarly, the frequency of
the received signal will decrease if the target is moving away from the Radar. The
difference between the received frequency and the transmitted is known as the
Till 1960s Doppler Radar was used by aircraft for onboard air navigation to
get ground speed and drift by measuring Doppler frequency shift. At present it is
used in MTI Radar to eliminate stationary targets on radar screen.
MTI RADAR:
The purpose of moving-target indication (MTI) radar is to reject signals
from fixed or slow-moving unwanted targets, such as buildings, hills, trees, sea,
rain etc., and display signals from moving targets such as aircraft. MTI Radar
uses the principle of Doppler Effect for distinguishing the non-stationary targets
from stationary objects by comparing the phase and amplitude of received signals
to a reference signal. This allows it to filter out clutter from stationary objects.
Pulse Doppler radar detects position and velocity of targets using pulse trains and
analyzing Doppler shift in received frequencies.
M'TI radar continuously transmits and compares received signals to a reference
to detect moving targets. FMCW radar works by continuously transmitting a signal
with a modulated frequency, such as a saw tooth wave. The distance of a target is
determined by measuring the beat frequency between the received signal and the
transmitted reference signal after mixing.
548
RADAR
BLIND SPEEDS:
Can be a serious limitation in MTI radar since they cause moving targets to
be canceled. Blind speed can be explained as the relative velocities at which the
MTI response is zero.
Vn (nth Radar Blind Speed) = n x A (wave length) ÷ 2 x PRT
The methods for reducing the blind speeds are:
Operate the radar at lower frequencies [or long wavelengths].
Operate with a high prf.
Constantly change the pulse repetition time (Staggered PRT).
Constandy change the TX- frequency (Frequency-Diversity).
>  By introducing slight, controlled variations in the PRF (Jitter PRF)

549
===== [nav 0282]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
SECONDARY RADAR SYSTEMS.
Secondary radar does not rely on reflections of the interrogation pulse
arriving back at the radar head via the target. Instead, a booster transmitter or
transponder is situated at the target and this is used to revitalize the interrogation
pulse for the return journey. Obviously such a system requires the co-operation
of the target. Two examples of secondary radar will be considered shortly, namely
SSR and DME. Secondary radar has the following advantages when compared
with primary radar:
Since only enough energy need be transmitted for a one-way journey,
the required transmitter power is lower and consequently the equipment
lighter and less bulky.
Pulse sequences may be coded, thereby conveying additional intelligence.
for example mode C (pressure altitude readout) with SSR.
SECONDARY SURVEILLANCE RADAR: (SSR)
Transponder: When using the transponder you have to select a four-figure
code in the transponder window. This code can be either a pre-determined code
for a class of aircraft (e.g. VFR light aircraft) or a 'discreet' code issued to you by
the controller for certain situations (e.g. to track you through some controlled
airspaces). This code is known as Mode A information and transmitted back to the
radar with each interrogation.
Most transponders can also transmit their altitude with the interrogation.
This is known as Mode C information.
IX RX
Mode pulse
Range - OXI
C= 3x10* m/s
TX RX e  Interrogater
T = t1 + 12
Airborne SSR Control Panel and Working
Range = V = (C x T) ÷ 2 - Tp (C= 3X108, T = t1 + 12, Tp = Processing Time)
550
RADAR
Most transponders have function switch with the following positions:
OFF - SBY (stand-by) - On - ALT (altitude) - TST (test)
When set to OFF, the transponder is off.
When set to SBY, the transponder is switched on but will not reply to
interrogation. This position is commonly used after engine start-up
and prior to take-off, when operating in a controlled circuit, and after
landing.
In the ON position the transponder will reply to interrogation but will
send Mode A information only. Thus in this position, the 4-digit code
will be sent but altitude information will not be sent.
When ALT is selected, the transponder is on and will reply to
interrogation. Both Mode A and Mode C altitude information will be
sent. This is the function normally used when airborne (other than in
the circuit of a controlled aerodrome). When this mode is set radar
controller is able to see your altitude in hundreds of feet as well as your
position.
• The TEST position is a transponder test function.
Transponder IDENT:
All mode A, C, and S transponders include an IDENT button (short for
identify). Pressing the button adds the SPI (Special Purpose Identification) pulse
to SSR replies. This pulse is after the last framing pulse and will be automatically
and continuously transmitted for about 20 seconds. It causes a momentary
distinctive display to appear on the controller's screen so that a controller can
pick out a particular aircraft by asking the pilot to "Squawk Ident". This button is
to be used specifically on controller request or to indicate communication failure
to the controller.
Occasionally the radar controller will instruct you 'squawk 'ident'. This
means you must press the transponder IDENT button (just once). This causes
your aircraft symbol on the radar screen to flash so that the controller can readily
Principle: Mode and code pulse:
FREQ: Interrogator (1030 MHZ) AND Transponder (1090 MHZ)
BAND : UHF band
551
===== [nav 0283]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
WORKING:
SSR and DME interrogators and transponders work on different frequencies.
This is done to avoid 'self triggering', which means that the transmitted wave may
be reflected by clouds/obstacles/ground and received by the interragator giving
wrong indication.
(i) Ground interrogator, interrogates on 1030 MHZ in mode pulses.
(ii) A/C transponder replies for mode pulses in coded pulses on 1090 MHZ.
Mode A - The interrogator transmits two pulses 8 microseconds apart.
Achieves positive identification of primary radar returns, using the four-figure
coding. A maximum of 4096 codes can be selected.
Mode B - The interrogator transmits two pulses 17 microseconds apart.
Serves the same purpose as mode A, but is not presently used.
Mode C - The interrogator transmits two pulses 21 microseconds apart.
Achieves height (pressure altitude) readout, using the three-figure coding.
Mode D - The interrogator transmits two pulses 25 microseconds apart.
Presently used to research future possible applications of SSR.
NOTE: Altitude info passed is with respect to standard altimeter setting of 1013.25 hPa
(PA/FL). Irrespective of sub scale setting. In mode 'C' altitude information is in steps of
100°.
MODE 'S'- It performs all the functions of mode 'C' transponder and in
addition it can be used as data transponder through A/C FMS.
It transmits altitude information is steps of 25'. More than 4096 codes can be
selected.
Mode 'C' or mode 'S 'of SSR is not to be used when indication differs more
than +300'.
SSR MODE ACCURACY:
Mode A Squawk code (identification): Depends on radar tracking.
Mode C Pressure altitude (100-foot steps): +50 to 100 feet.
Mode S Altitude, ID, and more (selective): 25-foot altitude resolution, high
horizontal accuracy with ADS-B.
552
RADAR
Codes with Specific Meaning:
7500 - Unlawful Interference (Hijack)
7600 - Communication Failure
7700 - Emergency On Board
2000 - No Code Allotted / Exiting FIR
0000- Transponder Failure
When an a/c is flying within coverage area of more than one SSR it may cause
interference in one station while replying to other. This is reduced by allotting
different PRF to adjacent SSR.
GARBLING: When 2 or more a/c are operating close to each other, they
may cause interference by giving overlapping response. This is removed by using
killer circuit.
Close to each other: (Distance less than 1.7 nm.)
DISTANCE MEASURING EQUIPMENT (DME):
DME allows you to determine your distance in nautical miles from a DME
ground station. The DME facility operates in the ultra high frequency (UHF)
band close to 1,000 MHz.
There are generally two types of DME selector, those which use the VHF-
NAV set combined with a detached DME read-out window and those that have a
DME receiver and display fitted as an individual unit.
In the first-mentioned case, the equipment has a selector knob on or near the
display window which is used to 'slave the DME to a selected VOR. In many cases
this selection of frequency and data display is on the separate unit.
In cases this selector allows slaving to the no.I VOR or to no.2 VOR. In the
second case, selection of frequency and data display is on the separate unit.

===== [nav 0284]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
Purpose: It gives slant range with respect to a ground station
Principle: Random PRF
Frequency: 962 MHZ. - 1213 MHZ
Band: UHF
WORKING:
When a/c eqpt. is tuned on a particular frequency, the interrogator starts
transmitting in pair of pulses at a PRF of 150 Pulses/ sec which is called
search or scanning mode.
• Ground station replies to each pair of pulse at a fixed delay of 50 & sec
with a frequency of 63 MHZ removed from it to avoid self triggering.
• During lock on mode interrogator transmits at changing PRF of 24 to 30
pulses/ sec with an average of 27 PPS.
• If no response is received during scanning mode, the interrogator
changes over to stand by mode and transmits at 60 PPS (stand by).
A ground st. Can reply to maximum of 2700 PPS. 100 a/c can use one
stn. at a given time.
Summary:
Range (V) = ((C x T) ÷ 2) - Tp  (Tp = Processing Time)
Search mode = 150 PPS
Locked on = 27 PPS
Stand by = 60 PPS
Functions of the DME in an approach:
When installed with the ILS and specified in the approach procedure, DME
maybe used:
• In lieu of the OM,
• As a back course (BC) final approach fix (FAF), and
• To establish other fix
Note: When asked how many a/c can use it, answer is 126 (in search mode/S/BY mode)
but in lock on mode it is 100 a/c. If more than 100 a/c try to achieve lock - on, swamping
takes place.
Swamping: It is a process of breaking the lock of low powered/ older version/
furthest A/C.
554
RADAR
Slant Range Error: Difference between ground distance and slant range,
Slant range errors are negligible when a/c is at height less than 1000' per n/m. A
DME never shows zero. Over a stn it shows height of a/c in nm.
Ground Speed: By monitoring the rate of change of range, the airborne
DME equipment is able to determine the aircraft's groundspeed. A read-out of
groundspeed derived by DME will be reasonably accurate only if the aircraft is
tracking directly towards, or directly away from, the transponder.
(Indicated)
Ground Range (True)
True range 19 NM
Ground/True Range (nms) = (Slant/Indicated Range in nms)½-(Height in nms)2
Maximum Range: DME operates in the UHF band and is therefore limited
to line of sight range. Maximum theoretical range may be determined for an
aircraft at a given altitude using the by now familiar formula:
Maximum Range (nm) = 1.25 (VHIx + VHrx)
Identity: The DME transmits its identifier once every 30 seconds and the VOR
once every 10 seconds. The third VOR identifier is suppressed, and is replaced by
the DME identifier. In this event the two identifier are heard at different tones, to
enable the pilot to distinguish one from the other.
Tactical air navigation (TACAN): It is a military navigation aid which gives
both azimuth and range information to a suitable equipped a/c and is similar to
VOR and DME co-located.
Civil a/c can use only distance or range element of TACAN.
Memory Circuit: If the DME signal is lost, range display remains frozen for
10 seconds before equipment enters search mode again.
DME (ILS): It is collocated with ILS glide slope eqpt and shows zero for ILS
threshold. Indication of ILS/ DME is correct and reliable in approach direction
only. i.e. with in localizer coverage area, upto appx. 6000

Chapter
9
GLOBAL NAVIGATION
SATELLITE SYSTEMS (GNSS)
Two satellite Assisted Nav Systems (SATNAV) are currently operating, ie,
Global Positioning System (GPS) owned by USA and Global Orbiting Navigation
Sattelite System (GLONASS) owned by former Soviet Union.
Medium Earth Orbits.
The satellites in the Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS), such as those
in the GPS (Global Positioning System), GLONASS, NAVIC, Galileo, and BeiDou
constellations, are typically deploved in orbits that are inclined relative to the
Earth's equator. These orbits are known as Medium Earth Orbits (MEO) and are
not exactly aligned with the equatorial plane. For example, in the case of GPS, the
satellites orbit the Earth at an inclination angle of approximately 55 degrees to the
equator. This inclination allows the satellites to cover a wider range of latitudes as
they orbit the Earth. The inclination is a design choice to ensure global coverage
and to maximize the number of satellites visible from any given point on Earth.
564
GLOBAL NAVIGATION SATELLITE SYSTEMS (GNSS)
The NAVSTAR/GPS Segments.
The GPS system relies on three segments working together: satellites in
space (Space Segment), ground-based control and monitoring stations (Control
segment), and the GPS receivers used by end-users (User Segment).
The GPS Satellite Constellation:
24 Satellites in nearly circular orbits around Earth.
>  6 orbital planes.
> 4 satellites in each orbital plane 20,200 km altitudes (about Earth radii).
GPS orbits 1200 kms above Glonass.
• Orbital periods are 11 hours 58 minutes.
• Each satellite carries 4 atomic clocks, one operational and three spares.
• Constellation is designed so that at least 4 or 5 satellites are in view from
any where on Earth.
>  Satellites broadcast their positions to GPS users.
• Model of Earth used for GPS is WGS84.
GPS Receiver (User Segment)
• GPS receiver identifies a given satellite by matching the (unique) code
from that satellite.
Data superimposed on the code gives satellite position (ephemeris) and
GPS system time at which -the signal was emitted.
> Receiver has a clock and it calculates how much time it took the signal to
travel from the satellite to receiver.
• The speed of light is 3x10° m/second, and the distance from satellite to
receiver is computed.
>  Corrections are made due to slowing down of signal through atmosphere.
>  Using the computed distances to 4 satellites, the receiver computes its
3-dimensional position by mathematical triangulation.
Principle: The receiver gives ground position by measuring simultaneous
distance ranges from satellites.
Frequency: The satellite transmit Pseudo random noise, code of I milli sec
duration on frequencies in VHF band. Each satellite has its own unique code.
L1 frequency (1575.42 MHz): transmits course acquisition code repeated
every millisecond. The precision code is modalated with frequency of 10.23 MHz
and is repeated every 7 days.
565
===== [nav 0290]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
L2 frequency (1227.6 MHz): Transmits P code. The P code may be encrypted
to prevent unauthorised use. In such case it is referred as Y code.
To give good coverage to GPS receivers anywhere on earth, there are always
at least 24 satellites (or Space Vehicles, SV's, as they are often referred to) in orbit
Of these, three are spares. Four SV's travel in each of six orbits, each inclined
at 55° to the equator so that four or more of the SV's are at all times visible
from any point on the surface of the earth. In addition to the atomic clocks and
the communications equipment each satellite also contains fuel for its small
maneuvering engines, giving it a limited capability of orbit adjustment.
WORKING:
(1) 21 - Operational Satellites
3 - Spare Satellites
24 - Total number of satellites
ORBIT at a height of 20,200 km in 6 orbits mutually inclined at 55ồẠại
(2) The information contained in P, R, N codes is following:
a)  Satellite position
b)  Clock time.
c)  Clock error.
d) Information on ionospheric condition.
e) Supplementary information.
(3) The angles as received at the receiver are not accurate and therefore
called Pseudo ranges.
Pseudo Ranges: The receiver has an accurate crystal oscillator to provide
time, however the accuracy does not compare with the accuracy of satellite
clock therefore will always be an error in time measurement and hence in the
computation of range. Further more the receiver clock is deliberately kept in
error by small factor to ensure that the correction process can only go in one
direction.
If the receiver clock is permanently 1 millisec fast the receiver accuracy will
be out by 162 nm. Therefore receiver must correct for error in pseudo ranges.
Determining User Position:
566
GLOBAL NAVIGATION SATELLITE SYSTEMS (GNSS)
Consider two satellite clocks, labeled A and B, that are located at
known positions and are synchronized.
• Assume that you (the user) are located somewhere between clocks A and B,
and that you have a clock that is synchronized with clocks A and B
• Assume that you simultaneously receive radio signals from clocks A and B,
and these signals tell you the time at which the signals left clocks A and B,
respectively.
• Using the known time of arrival of the signals at your position, and the time
the signals left each clock, you can figure out the distance that each signal
traveled (shown by the arrows), since each signal travels at the speed of light,
3x10° m /second. You can then draw a sphere centered at each clock with a
radius equal to the distance traveled from that clock.
• The intersection of the two spheres is a circle. Your position is somewhere on
this circle.
- We need a third satellite, to resolve the ambiguity in position along the circle.
- Adding the simultaneous reception of signals from a third satellite C, adds
another sphere centered at C.
• The intersection of the circle (shown in dark) with the sphere centered at C
gives our probable position as two points, shown as P1 and P2.
• Finally, the simultaneous reception of a fourth satellite (not shown) gives
another sphere, which resolves the ambiguity in position between points PI
and P2.
1 Satellite - Position in space.
2 Satellite - Ambiguous position on carth surface.
3 Satellite - Ambiguous position in space
4 Satellite - Fix in space
567
===== [nav 0291]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
5 Satellite - RAIM (Remote Autonomous Integrity Monitoring)
Max number of satellites can be seen at a time is 5.
RAIM (Receiver Autonomous Integrity Monitoring):
> GPS receivers for critical applications, such as IF flight, have RAIM
algorithms that check integrity of GPS data.
Integrity - The ability of a system to provide timely warnings to users
when the system should not be used for navigation.
> To perform the RAIM function, at least one extra satellite, in addition to
the ones used for navigation, must be tracked by the receiver
> RAIM algorithms need a minimum of 5 satellites, 4 for position
determination plus one extra
> Alternative RAIM scheme: 4 satellites plus baro-aiding provided by
barometer -- the altimeter setting must be entered manually
> Some receivers are capable of isolating which satellite signal is corrupt
and can remove it from the position solution. These receivers need 6
satellites, or 5 satellites plus baro-aiding.
Without RAIM capability, the pilot has no assurance of accuracy of GPS position
ERRORS OF GPS:
1)  Ephemeris Error: The error is caused due to incorrect satellite position.
The position of satellite may be shifted by the gravitational effect of sun,
moon and planets. The maximum error permissible is 2.5 m.
2)  Satellite Clock Error: Each satellite has 4 atomic clocks with accuracy of one
nano sec. The satellite clock is checked every 12 hrs. and any error past 2
satellite is included in the broadcast. Maximum permissible is 1.5 m.
3)  Ionospheric Propagation Error / Ionospheric Group Delay: This is most
significant error of any satellite navigation system. 2 different frequencies
are used to find and reduce ionospheric group delay. Maximum error for
single frequency operation is 5m.
4)  Troposphere Group Delay: Maximum permissible is 0.3 m
5)  Multi Path Reception: Reflection from ground and parts of A/C result in
multipath reception. This can be minimised by carefully selecting a position
of aerial.
6)  GDOP: Geometric Dilution of Position: Caased due to poor cuts between
position lines. GDOP occurs when satellites are closer to each other.
568
GLOBAL NAVIGATION SATELLITE SYSTEMS (GNSS)
Vertical Position: The altitude above the WGS-84 ellipsoid can be different
7)
from the MSL altitude on the aircraft altimeter by hundreds of feet. This is
a serious consideration during instrument approaches. Use your altimeter!
Typically, GPS vertical position is less accurate than GPS horizontal position.
DGPS (Differential GPS): This basic idea of differential GPS is used in
WAAS(Wide area augmentation system) and LAAS(Local area augmentation
system), and all other augmentation schemes. WAAS provides en route navigation
plus Category I-type approaches for most airports.
LAAS provides navigation capability in areas where WAAS is not available
plus Category 11/111 approaches and landings at selected locations.
To attain accuracy levels on the order of one to 3 meters, differential
correction is essential. The three main methods currently used for ensuring data
accuracy are real-time differential correction, reprocessing real-time data, and
post processing. The underlying premise of differential GPS (DGPS) is that any
two receivers that are relatively close together will experience similar atmospheric
errors. DGPS requires that a GPS receiver be set up on a precisely known location.
This GPS receiver is the base or reference station. The base station receiver
calculates its position based on satellite signals and compares this location to the
known location. The difference is applied to the GPS data recorded by the second
GPS receiver, which is known as the roving receiver. The corrected information
can be applied to data from the roving receiver in real time in the field using radio
signals or through post processing after data capture using special processing
software.
The accuracy of GPS is improved by comparing the true position and position
shown by GPS. The difference would be same in the given area. This difference is
transmitted to user to improve accuracy.
Services: The 2 services provided by GPS are
SPS - Standard Positioning Service using CA (Course Acquisition) code.
Available to all users, such as civilians, FAA, and other civilian organizations.
Positioning Accuracy: 100 meters horizontal position, 95% of the time 300
meters horizontal position, 99.99% of the time
PPS - Precise Positioning Service using CA and P-code (Precise Code, or
Y-code (encrypted)). Only available to U.S. Department of Defense and other
Authorized Users
Positioning Accuracy: 16 meters horizontal position, 98% of the time.
569
===== [nav 0292]
PART III RADIO NAVIGATION
Advantages / Disadvantages
Advantages:
Capable of use worldwide at all operating speeds and altitudes
Highly accurate
Available throughout the 24 hours with no diminution of accuracy
Provides three-dimensional positioning on a continuous basis
5)  Unrestricted range
6)  Entirely space-based
7)  Coupled with inertial navigation systems, can make terrestrial-based aids
such as NDBs, VORS, DMEs, etc., redundant
8)  When GPS/GLONASS development is complete and all satellites available,
the accuracy of GNSS will enable aircraft separations to be reduced
9)  GNSS when perfected will be able to supplant MLS and ILS as a precision
approach aid
10)  For light aircraft, there are lightweight, relatively inexpensive and portable
models available.
Disadvantages :
1)  Basically it is a military system which civil aircraft are allowed to use:
currently the US has agreed to providing GPS to civil users free of charge
and similarly the CIS is providing GLONASS free.
2)  As a military system, the existing GNSS may conceivably be interrupted if a
serious military need develops.
3)  The system, although it has evolved rapidly, is still technically under
development.
4)  Not as accurate in the civil mode as it is in the military mode (by deliberate
policy).
5)  Present system cannot guarantee the pilot with immediate indication of
satellite malfunction or GNSS degradation; this makes it currently unsuitable
as an approach aid for high category landings.
570
GLOBAL NAVIGATION SATELLITE SYSTEMS (GNSS)
Latest developments
1) There is an international disagreement on the use of MLS and DGPS
as a future landing aid. Europe favours the continued use of MLS until
DGPS has the same CAT3 performance ability. America wishes to phase
out MLS and concentrate on the development of DGPS to the same
CAT3 capabilities and integrity. This they hope to achieve sooner than
Europe deem possible (2015)!
Undoubtedly DGPS will provide the final answer. Meanwhile ICAO is
approving both systems and a multi-mode receiver (MMR) for their
2) GAGAN is the acronym for GPS Aided GEO Augmented Navigation.
The GAGAN uses a system of ground stations to provide necessary
augmentations to the GPS standard positioning service(SPS) navigation
signal. A network of precisely surveyed ground reference stations (Indian
Reference Stations - INRES) is strategically positioned across the country
to collect GPS satellite data. Using this information, the master control
centre (Indian Master Control Centre - INMCC) generates messages to
correct any signal errors. These correction messages are then uplinked
through (Indian Land Uplink Station - INLUS) and broadcast through
communication satellites (Geostationary) to receivers onboard aircraft
using the same frequency as GPS.
The GAGAN is designed to provide the additional accuracy, availability,
and integrity necessary to enable users to rely on GPS for all phases of
flight, from en route through approach for all qualified airports within
the GAGAN service volume. GAGAN also provides the capability for
increased accuracy in position reporting, allowing for more uniform
and high-quality Air Traffic Management (ATM). In addition, GAGAN
provides benefits beyond aviation to all modes of transportation,  d high genets beyond.
including maritime, highways, and railroads.
Indian Regional Navigation Satellite System (IRNSS) renamed NAVIC-
Navigation with Indian Constellation is India's version of the US's
globally used GPS system, and covers a wide swath of Asia and the Indian
Ocean. It is a constellation of seven satellites in a geosynchronous orbit
around Earth, and they coordinate to provide centimetre-level tracking
precision to the Indian armed forces. The three atomic clocks used
onboard each satellite of the IRNSS are used for precise coordination
